The Pepper Programming Language

Introduction

Pepper is a compiled programming language for writing fast, native programs that are safe by default. Like Rust and Swift, it aims to rule out whole classes of memory bugs without asking you to manage memory by hand. It puts three things first:

  • Clarity. Code should say what it does. Pepper has a small, familiar syntax, one way to write most things, and error messages that point at the exact token you need to change.
  • Simplicity. There is no inheritance, no garbage collector to tune, and no ownership annotations to learn. Structs hold data, methods and extensions add behavior, and traits describe what a type can do.
  • Concurrent safety. Shared data should be safe to use from more than one thread. Only thread-safe values can be shared: numbers, Atomic<T>, and structs declared [Shared], whose fields the compiler checks.

Here is a complete Pepper program:

# Every program starts at main. Its return value is the process exit code.
main(): int {
    print("Hello, world!");
    return 0;
}

How Pepper manages memory

Every struct value in Pepper is a reference to an object on the heap, and the object is freed when the last reference to it goes away. The model is exactly C++’s std::shared_ptr<T> passed by value: storing a reference in a variable, a field, an array element or a parameter adds a reference, and that reference is released when the variable goes out of scope or is overwritten. There is no separate notion of a “borrowed” reference to learn. Numbers and other primitive values are copied, as in C.

Because the rules are the same everywhere, you can predict exactly when an object is freed and when its destructor runs. The chapter on memory and reference counting covers the model in detail.

How Pepper is built

The compiler is written in Rust. It parses your program, checks it, and generates native code through LLVM, then links an executable with the system’s C toolchain. The same compiler powers a language server, so editors such as VS Code show Pepper’s errors as you type.

Pepper runs on Windows (x64), Linux (x64) and macOS (arm64).

Current status

Pepper is young. The core language works and is tested: variables, functions, structs with constructors, destructors and methods, enums with match, tuples and multiple return values, generics (with type arguments inferred at calls, and generic methods), type aliases, traits (with default methods, and usable as types), function types and closures, modules (with export * from re-exports), Result-based error handling, Optional values, iteration (with map and filter adapters), with blocks, weak references, and calling C (including C callbacks). The standard library covers strings, arrays, hash maps and sets, atomics, threads, channels and mutexes, and file-system access. That is enough to write real programs, but expect gaps. pep-fmt, the code formatter, is itself written in Pepper. Some that you will meet quickly:

  • Functions are values: a named function, a method (p.norm, Point.norm), a constructor (Point), a generic function’s specialization (larger<int>) or a closure such as (x: int) => x * 2 can be stored, passed and called through a function type such as (int) => int (see function types and closures).
  • A constructor marked [Implicit] converts a value into its struct wherever the struct is expected, so io.Path parameters accept a String (see implicit conversions).
  • Free functions, methods, operators, extension methods and trait members can be overloaded; constructors cannot yet.
  • Generic parameters cannot require a trait yet (no <T: Shape>).
  • Threads can share only thread-safe values, which the compiler checks, closure captures included. A channel copies what it is sent with copy, so a String or an Array crosses threads that way; mutexes and Lazy hold thread-safe values only, and a trait value cannot be copied yet (see threads).
  • Reference counting does not reclaim cycles on its own: two objects that refer to each other are never freed. Break the cycle with a weak reference on one side.
  • print writes each argument on its own line.

Each chapter of this book says plainly what is and is not supported. When a chapter shows a feature, the example was compiled and run to check it.

How this book is organized

  • Tutorial. Getting started builds the compiler and runs your first program. A tour of Pepper then walks through the main features by building a small word-counting program.
  • Language guide. One chapter per part of the language, from types and variables to calling C. Read it in order, or jump to the topic you need.
  • Standard library reference. Every type and function in the runtime, generated from the runtime’s own documentation comments. Start at the overview.

Every complete program in this book lives in the repository’s docs/examples/ folder and is compiled and run by the test suite, so what you read is what works.

Versions of this book

This documentation is versioned with the compiler. Use the version picker in the top-right corner of each page to switch between releases; dev follows the main branch. Read the version that matches the compiler you are using.

Getting started

This chapter gets the Pepper compiler built on your machine, compiles and runs a first program, and sets up your editor. Pepper has no binary releases yet, so you build the compiler from source.

Build the compiler

The compiler is a Rust program that generates code through LLVM. Building it takes three steps on every platform. The repository’s README has the full details for each platform; this is the short version.

  1. Install Rust with rustup. You need Rust 1.85 or newer. On Windows, use the default x86_64-pc-windows-msvc toolchain.

  2. Install the platform prerequisites, which provide the C toolchain that links your programs:

    • Windows: Visual Studio 2022 or newer with the C++ workload (or the standalone Build Tools), and PowerShell.
    • macOS: the Xcode Command Line Tools: xcode-select --install.
    • Linux (Debian and Ubuntu package names): sudo apt install build-essential clang curl xz-utils.

  3. Set up LLVM. Clone the repository, then run the setup command from its root:

    git clone https://github.com/justinboswell/pepper
cd pepper
cargo run -p xtask -- setup --devtools

    This installs a development build of LLVM (from apt, Homebrew, or the official release archive on Windows) and writes .cargo/config.toml so that plain cargo commands find it. --devtools also sets up the lldb debugger; you can leave it out. Run cargo setup --check at any time to see what is configured.

Now build the compiler:

cargo build --release

The compiler is target/release/pepper (target\release\pepper.exe on Windows). To check that everything works, run the test suite with cargo test --workspace.

Your first program

Pepper source files end in .pep. Create a file named hello.pep:

# Every program starts at main. Its return value is the process exit code.
main(): int {
    print("Hello, world!");
    return 0;
}

Every program starts at main, which takes no arguments and returns an int: the process’s exit code, where 0 means success. print writes its argument to standard output, followed by a new line. Comments start with # and run to the end of the line.

Compile and run it

Pass the source file to pepper. It compiles and links a native executable, but does not run it, so run the result yourself:

pepper hello.pep -o hello
./hello

Hello, world!

On Windows, name the output with .exe (-o hello.exe), since Windows will not run a file without an extension, and run it as .\hello.exe. Without -o the executable is written to the current directory, named after the source file with no extension (hello for hello.pep).

The examples here write pepper; use the path to the binary you built, or add target/release to your PATH. From the repository root, you can also run the compiler through cargo: cargo run --release -- hello.pep -o hello.

Where the compiler finds the standard library

Every program is compiled together with Pepper’s standard library, the runtime/ folder of the repository. The compiler looks for it in this order:

  1. The directory named by the PEPPER_RUNTIME environment variable, if it is set. Nothing else is searched.
  2. Starting from the directory you run it from: runtime/, .pep/runtime/, then ../runtime/, ../../runtime/, and so on, up to five levels up.
  3. Starting from the directory that holds the compiler executable: runtime/ beside it, then in each parent directory, up to five levels up.

So a compiler built in target/release finds the runtime of the clone it was built from, wherever you run it. To use a different runtime, set PEPPER_RUNTIME or link the runtime into a project’s .pep folder:

mkdir .pep
ln -s /path/to/pepper/runtime .pep/runtime

If the compiler cannot find the runtime, it stops with an error:

error: cannot find the Pepper runtime: no runtime directory near /home/me/project or the compiler; set PEPPER_RUNTIME to its path

The compiler also writes intermediate files to .pep/build/ under the directory you run it from, and keeps a build cache in .pep/cache/: each file of the program (and the standard library) is compiled to its own object, and an object whose code did not change is reused on the next build. Deleting .pep/cache/ is always safe.

When something is wrong

The compiler reports errors the way clang does: the file, line and column, a message, and the source line with a caret under the exact token to fix. Store the message in a variable first, then misspell its name:

main(): int {
    var greeting = "Hello, world!";
    print(greting);
    return 0;
}

The compiler reports:

main.pep:3:11: error: use of undeclared identifier 'greting'
    3 |     print(greting);
      |           ^~~~~~~

Errors are colored when the output is a terminal; set NO_COLOR to turn color off. The chapter on compiler errors covers them in more detail.

A failed assert or a panic at run time prints its message and stops the program abnormally. For example, value() on a failed Result prints Result.value on an Err value.

Useful compiler options

OptionWhat it does
-o <file>Name the output executable.
-I <dir>Add a directory to search for imported modules. See modules and imports.
-l <lib>Link a C library. See linking.
--import <name>Load a module into every file of the program, like the standard library.
--build <dir>Write intermediate files to <dir> instead of .pep/build/.
--emit-llvmAlso write the generated LLVM IR to the build directory, as <name>.ll.
--emit-astParse only, and print the syntax tree instead of compiling.
-vPrint what the compiler is doing.
-T timePrint how long each stage of the compile took.
-T cachePrint which objects came from the build cache.
--no-cacheCompile everything without reading or writing the build cache.
--cache <dir>Keep the build cache in <dir> instead of .pep/cache/ (also PEPPER_CACHE_DIR).
--helpList every option.

A program can span several files: pass the entry file, and the compiler finds the files it imports on its own.

Editor support

The VS Code extension adds syntax highlighting and a language server, which reports compiler errors as you type and provides hover information, completion, go to definition, and an outline of each file.

The packaged extension is published as a .vsix file on the repository’s releases page, under the release whose tag starts with vsix-v. Download it and install it in VS Code with Extensions: Install from VSIX… from the command palette. The language server is bundled with the extension, so there is nothing else to install.

A few things are useful once it is installed:

  • Pepper: Restart Language Server restarts the server on demand.
  • Where a value is converted by an [Implicit] constructor, the editor shows the call it stands for as an inlay hint (Path( before "notes.txt" and ) after), and hovering the value says so. The pepper.inlayHints.implicitConversions setting turns the hints off.
  • The pepper.lspPath setting points the extension at a language server you built yourself (cargo build --release -p pepper-lsp builds target/release/pepper-lsp). The server restarts whenever that file changes.

To install the extension from the branch you have checked out instead of the release, see VS Code extension from any branch in the README.

What to read next

  • A tour of Pepper builds a small program step by step and shows the main features of the language along the way.
  • The language guide covers each part of the language in depth.
  • The standard library reference lists every built-in type and function.

A tour of Pepper

This chapter walks through the main features of Pepper by writing small programs, and ends with a word counter that reads a file and reports how often each word appears. It moves quickly and shows the common case of each feature; every section links to the chapter of the language guide that covers it fully.

Every program here is complete: save it to a file, compile it as shown in Getting started, and run it. Each one checks its own results with assert(condition, "message"), which stops the program with the message if the condition is false, so a program that runs to the end and returns 0 did what the comments say.

Variables and functions

# A function lists its parameters as `name: type` and gives its return
# type after the closing parenthesis.
square(x: int): int {
    return x * x;
}

# A parameter may have a default value, used when the caller leaves it out.
greet(name: String = "world"): String {
    return "Hello, " + name + "!";
}

# Adds up 1 + 2 + ... + n with a while loop.
sum_to(n: int): int {
    var total = 0;
    var i = 1;
    while i <= n {
        total += i;
        i = i + 1;
    }
    return total;
}

describe(n: int): String {
    if n > 100 {
        return "big";
    } else {
        if n > 10 {
            return "medium";
        }
    }
    return "small";
}

main(): int {
    var answer = 42;          # the type, int, comes from the value
    double ratio = 0.5;       # or write the type yourself
    var whole = (ratio * 10.0) as int;
    assert(whole == 5, "'as' converts between number types");

    assert(square(answer) == 1764, "square(42)");
    assert(greet() == "Hello, world!", "the default name");
    assert(greet("Pepper") == "Hello, Pepper!", "an explicit name");
    assert(sum_to(4) == 10, "1 + 2 + 3 + 4");

    assert(describe(answer) == "medium", "42 is medium");
    assert(describe(7) == "small", "7 is small");

    print(greet("Pepper"));
    print(sum_to(100));
    return 0;
}

A few things to notice:

  • var declares a variable whose type comes from its initial value: 42 is an int. You can also name the type yourself, as in double ratio = 0.5;. as converts between types, such as from double to int. See Types and variables.
  • A function is declared as name(parameters): return type, and each parameter as name: type. A parameter can have a default value, as name does in greet. A function that returns nothing leaves out the return type. See Functions.
  • if and while do not need parentheses around the condition, but the body always needs braces. Chain conditions with else if. See Control flow.
  • += updates a variable in place, and + joins two strings.
  • print writes one value per line. Called with several arguments, it prints each one on its own line.

Structs

A struct groups related data with the methods that act on it:

struct Point {
    var x: int = 0;
    var y: int = 0;

    # `this.x = 0` as a parameter stores the argument straight into the field.
    construct(this.x = 0, this.y = 0) {}

    length_squared(): int {
        return this.x * this.x + this.y * this.y;
    }

    move_by(dx: int, dy: int) {
        this.x = this.x + dx;
        this.y = this.y + dy;
    }
}

# An extension adds methods to a struct that is declared elsewhere.
extend Point {
    swapped(): Point {
        return Point(this.y, this.x);
    }
}

main(): int {
    var p = Point(3, 4);
    assert(p.length_squared() == 25, "3*3 + 4*4");

    var origin = Point();
    assert(origin.x == 0 && origin.y == 0, "defaults from the constructor");

    # Struct values are references: q and p name the same Point.
    var q = p;
    q.move_by(1, 1);
    assert(p.x == 4 && p.y == 5, "p sees the move made through q");

    var s = p.swapped();
    assert(s.x == 5 && s.y == 4, "swapped is a new Point");
    assert(p.x == 4, "p itself is unchanged");
    return 0;
}
  • Fields are declared like variables, with var name: type, and may have default values.
  • The constructor is named construct. A parameter written as this.x = 0 stores its argument straight into the field x, and = 0 makes it optional, so Point() and Point(3, 4) both work.
  • Methods are functions declared inside the struct. Inside a method, this is the object the method was called on.
  • extend Point { ... } adds methods to a struct from outside its declaration, even to a struct from the standard library.

Struct values are references. var q = p; does not copy the point: p and q refer to the same object, so moving q moves p. Pepper frees an object when the last reference to it goes away, so you never free memory yourself. See Structs and Memory and reference counting.

Collections

The standard library provides growable arrays, strings, and hash maps. You do not need to import them:

main(): int {
    # Array<T> is a growable list. int[] is another way to write Array<int>.
    var scores = Array<int>();
    scores.append(90);
    scores.append(72);
    scores.append(85);
    assert(scores.count() == 3, "three scores");
    assert(scores[0] == 90, "indexing starts at 0");
    scores[1] = 75;
    assert(scores.contains(85), "contains");

    var total = 0;
    for (var score in scores) {
        total += score;
    }
    assert(total == 250, "90 + 75 + 85");

    int[] none = Array<int>();
    assert(none.empty(), "a new array is empty");

    # String holds UTF-8 text.
    var name = "Pepper";
    var greeting = "Hello, " + name;
    greeting += "!";
    assert(greeting == "Hello, Pepper!", "+ and += join strings");
    assert(greeting.length() == 14, "length counts characters");
    assert(greeting.starts_with("Hello"), "starts_with");
    assert(greeting.substr(7, 6) == "Pepper", "substr(start, count)");
    assert(greeting.find("Pepper") == 7, "find returns an offset, or -1");
    assert(greeting[0] == "H", "s[i] is a one-character String");

    # HashMap<K, V> maps keys to values.
    var ages = HashMap<String, int>();
    ages["Ada"] = 36;
    ages.insert("Alan", 41);
    assert(ages.count() == 2, "two entries");
    assert(ages["Ada"] == 36, "look up by key");
    assert(ages.contains("Alan"), "contains");
    assert(ages["Grace"] == 0, "a missing key reads as the default value");
    assert(!ages.contains("Grace"), "reading does not insert");
    ages.remove("Ada");
    assert(ages.count() == 1, "removed");

    # HashSet<K> keeps each key once.
    var seen = HashSet<String>();
    seen.insert("apple");
    seen.insert("apple");
    assert(seen.count() == 1, "duplicates are ignored");
    return 0;
}
  • Array<T> is a list that grows as you append. int[] is a shorter way to write the type Array<int>. Indexing is checked: reading past the end stops the program with an error rather than reading stray memory.
  • String holds UTF-8 text. length() counts characters (code points), and s[i] is the character at index i, as a one-character string. Strings compare by content with ==.
  • HashMap<K, V> maps keys to values. Reading a key that is not there gives the default value of V (0 for numbers) without adding it; use contains to tell the two apart. HashSet<K> keeps each key once.
  • for (var x in collection) visits each element of an array, or each character of a string, in order. See Iteration.

Handling errors with Result

Pepper has no exceptions. A function that can fail returns a Result: either a value, or an error that explains what went wrong.

# Converts a string of decimal digits to an int, or explains why it cannot.
parse_number(text: String): Result<int> {
    if text.empty() {
        return Result<int>.err(Error("expected a number, found nothing"));
    }
    var value = 0;
    for (var c in text) {
        var digit = "0123456789".find(c);
        if digit < 0 {
            return Result<int>.err(Error("'" + c + "' is not a digit"));
        }
        value = value * 10 + (digit as int);
    }
    return Result<int>.ok(value);
}

main(): int {
    var good = parse_number("2026");
    assert(good.is_ok(), "digits parse");
    assert(good.value() == 2026, "value() returns the value");

    var bad = parse_number("12x");
    assert(bad.is_err(), "x is not a digit");
    assert(bad.error().message == "'x' is not a digit", "the error explains why");
    print(bad.error().message);

    assert(parse_number("").ok_or(-1) == -1, "ok_or supplies a fallback");
    return 0;
}
  • Result<int> holds either an int or an Error. Build one with Result<int>.ok(value) or Result<int>.err(Error("message")).
  • is_ok() and is_err() tell you which one you have. value() and error() return what it holds; error().message is the error’s text.
  • value() returns the value, and stops the program if there is none. Use it only when an error would be a bug. ok_or(fallback) returns the value, or fallback if there is none.

See Error handling with Result.

Putting it together: a word counter

The last program combines everything above. It writes a short text file, reads it back, and counts how often each word appears. It is split into two files: counter.pep defines a WordCounter struct, and main.pep uses it.

counter.pep:

# Counts how often each word occurs in some text.
export struct WordCounter {
    var counts: HashMap<String, int>;
    var order: String[];    # each distinct word, in the order first seen
    var total: int;

    construct() {
        this.counts = HashMap<String, int>();
        this.order = Array<String>();
        this.total = 0;
    }

    add(word: String) {
        if !word.empty() {
            if !this.counts.contains(word) {
                this.order.append(word);
            }
            this.counts[word] = this.counts[word] + 1;
            this.total = this.total + 1;
        }
    }

    # Splits `text` into words at spaces and counts each one.
    add_text(text: String) {
        var word = String();
        for (var c in text) {
            if c == " " {
                this.add(word);
                word = String();
            } else {
                word += c;
            }
        }
        this.add(word);
    }

    count(word: String): int {
        return this.counts[word];
    }
}

main.pep:

import [File, Directory, Path] from io;
import "counter";

# Reads the file at `path` and counts its words.
count_file(path: Path): Result<WordCounter> {
    var text = File.read_to_string(path);
    if text.is_err() {
        return Result<WordCounter>.err(Error(text.error().message));
    }
    var counter = WordCounter();
    counter.add_text(text.value());
    return Result<WordCounter>.ok(counter);
}

main(): int {
    # Write a small file to read back.
    var dir = Directory.temp().join("pepper-word-count");
    Directory.create_all(dir);
    var path = dir.join("poem.txt");
    File.write_text(path, "the cat sat on the mat and the dog sat too");

    var result = count_file(path);
    assert(result.is_ok(), "the file we just wrote can be read");
    var counter = result.value();
    for (var word in counter.order) {
        print(word);
        print(counter.count(word));
    }
    assert(counter.total == 11, "eleven words");
    assert(counter.count("the") == 3, "'the' appears three times");
    assert(counter.count("sat") == 2, "'sat' appears twice");
    assert(counter.count("bird") == 0, "'bird' does not appear");

    var missing = count_file(dir.join("missing.txt"));
    assert(missing.is_err(), "a missing file is an error, not a crash");
    print(missing.error().message);

    Directory.remove_all(dir);
    return 0;
}

Compile it by passing the entry file; the compiler finds counter.pep from the import:

pepper main.pep -o word-count
./word-count

It prints each word followed by its count, one value per line, and then the error message for the missing file (its exact text comes from the operating system):

the
3
cat
1
sat
2
...
No such file or directory

What is new here:

  • Imports. import "counter"; makes the declarations that counter.pep marks with export available in main.pep. Anything not exported stays private to its file. import [File, Directory, Path] from io; brings three names in from the standard library’s io module, which, unlike String or Array, must be imported. See Modules and imports and Visibility.
  • Files. Directory.temp() is the system’s temporary directory, and join adds a name to a Path; the name can be a plain String, which converts to a Path implicitly. File has helpers that read or write a whole file at once: File.write_text and File.read_to_string.
  • I/O errors are Results. File.read_to_string returns a Result whose error is an IoError, so reading a missing file is an ordinary error value, not a crash. count_file turns it into its own Result<WordCounter>.
  • Strings character by character. add_text walks the text one character at a time and builds each word with +=, since the String type has no split method yet.

Where to go next

You have now seen most of what you need to write everyday Pepper code. The language guide covers each topic in depth, including features this tour skipped: generics, function types and closures, traits and extensions, atomics, threads, and calling C.

Types and variables

This chapter covers the values every Pepper program is built from: numbers, booleans and strings, the variables that hold them, and the operators that combine them.

Built-in types

Integers

Pepper has signed and unsigned integers of 8, 16, 32 and 64 bits. Each width has a short name (i32) and a descriptive alias (int). The two names are the same type, so you can use whichever reads better and assign freely between them.

BitsSignedUnsigned
8i8, charu8, byte
16i16, shortu16, ushort
32i32, intu32, uint
64i64, longu64, ulong

int is 32 bits and long is 64 bits on every platform. sizeof<T>() gives the size of a type in bytes.

Integer literals are written in decimal (42) or hexadecimal (0xFF). A literal on its own is an int when its value fits in an int, and a long otherwise, so var big = 3000000000; is a long. It takes another type when you store it in a variable of that type (u64 huge = 9000000000000000000;). A hexadecimal literal is a 64-bit bit pattern: u64 all = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF; sets every bit.

# Integer types: widths, signedness, wraparound and conversions.
main(): int {
    # Each width has a short name and a descriptive alias.
    i8 tiny = -100;       # same type as char
    u8 octet = 200;       # same type as byte
    i16 small = -30000;   # same type as short
    u16 port = 65000;     # same type as ushort
    i32 count = 2000000;  # same type as int
    u32 mask = 4000000000; # same type as uint
    i64 big = 9000000000; # same type as long
    u64 huge = 9000000000000000000;  # same type as ulong

    assert(sizeof<char>() == 1 && sizeof<short>() == 2, "8 and 16 bits");
    assert(sizeof<int>() == 4 && sizeof<long>() == 8, "32 and 64 bits");

    # An alias names the same type, so assigning between them is lossless.
    short s = small;
    assert(s == -30000, "short and i16 are one type");

    # Storing a value that does not fit wraps around, as in C.
    u16 wrap = 65535;
    wrap = wrap + 1;
    assert(wrap == 0, "u16 wraps from 65535 to 0");
    i16 overflow = 32767;
    overflow += 1;
    assert(overflow == -32768, "i16 wraps from 32767 to -32768");

    # Widening keeps the value: signed types sign-extend, unsigned zero-extend.
    long widened = small;
    assert(widened == -30000, "i16 widens to long");
    assert((tiny as u8) == 156, "-100 as u8 is 156");
    assert((octet as i8) == -56, "200 as i8 is -56");

    # Narrowing with 'as' keeps the low bits.
    assert((70000 as u16) == 4464, "70000 as u16 keeps the low 16 bits");

    # Hexadecimal literals.
    assert(0xFF == 255, "0xFF is 255");
    assert(count + big > 0 && mask > 0 && huge > 0 && port > 0, "all in range");
    return 0;
}

Storing a value that does not fit in the destination keeps its low bits, as in C: a u16 holding 65535 becomes 0 when you add 1 and store the result, and 70000 as u16 is 4464.

A decimal literal larger than the largest long (9223372036854775807), or a hexadecimal literal with more than 64 bits, is an error:

main.pep:2:13: error: integer literal is too large to be represented in a 64-bit integer
    2 |     var f = 99999999999999999999;
      |             ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

Floating point

float is a 32-bit and double a 64-bit IEEE 754 number (f32 and f64 are aliases). A literal with a decimal point or an exponent, such as 0.25 or 1e3, is a double.

Booleans

bool holds true or false. Comparisons and the logical operators produce a bool. In arithmetic a bool counts as 0 or 1, and print(true) prints 1.

Characters

A character literal is one ASCII character between single quotes: 'a', '"', '#'. Its value is the character’s code, and its type is byte (u8), the type of one byte of a UTF-8 string. Like any byte it widens to the other integer types, so it compares with, and stores into, an int or a char as well:

# Character literals are bytes.
is_lower(c: int): bool {
    return c >= 'a' and c <= 'z';
}

main(): int {
    var letter = 'a';   # a byte holding 97
    assert(letter == 97, "a character literal is its ASCII code");
    assert('"' == 34 and '\n' == 10 and '\'' == 39, "escapes work as in strings");

    # Compare the bytes of a String with character literals.
    var text = "x = 1";
    var bytes = text.c_str();
    assert(bytes[0] == 'x' and bytes[2] == '=', "compare raw bytes");
    assert(is_lower(bytes[0]), "a byte widens to int");

    # Arithmetic works as on any integer.
    int digit = bytes[4] - '0';
    assert(digit == 1, "the digit's value");

    print(letter);
    return 0;
}

The escapes are the same as a string’s (see the table under Strings), plus \xHH for any byte, '\xFF' included. A character literal holds exactly one ASCII character; for anything else use a string:

main.pep:2:13: error: character literal may only contain one character; use a string for more
    2 |     var x = 'ab';
      |             ^~~~
main.pep:2:14: error: character literal must be a single ASCII character; use a string for other characters
    2 |     var x = 'é';
      |              ^

char is an 8-bit signed integer, the same type as i8, used mostly for C strings (__ptr<char>). The ASCII characters have the same value as a char and as a byte; '\xFF' is 255 as a byte but -1 read through a char. Text is a String, and a single character of it is a one-code-point String.

Strings

String is a UTF-8 string. A string literal such as "hello" is a String value, except where it is stored or passed as a __ptr<char>: there it is the literal’s NUL-terminated bytes (see Passing strings). + joins two strings into a new one, == and != compare contents, length() counts code points, size() counts bytes, and s[i] gives the code point at index i as a one-code-point String.

# String literals are String values.
main(): int {
    var language = "Pepper";
    assert(language.length() == 6, "6 code points");

    # + makes a new String; == compares contents.
    var message = "Hello, " + language;
    assert(message == "Hello, Pepper", "concatenation");
    assert(message != language, "different contents");

    # Strings are UTF-8. length() counts code points, size() counts bytes.
    var word = "héllo";
    assert(word.length() == 5, "5 code points");
    assert(word.size() == 6, "6 bytes: 'é' takes 2");
    assert(word[1] == "é", "indexing yields a one-code-point String");

    # A String is a reference: both names see an append.
    var alias = message;
    message.append("!");
    assert(alias == "Hello, Pepper!", "one object, two references");

    print(message);
    return 0;
}

A String is a reference type: var alias = message; makes a second reference to the same object rather than a copy, so a change made with append shows through both names. Memory and reference counting explains how references work, and the String reference lists every method.

A backslash in a string literal starts an escape sequence, as in C and Rust:

EscapeCharacter
\nnewline
\ttab
\rcarriage return
\0the zero byte
\\a backslash
\"a double quote
\'a single quote
\xHHthe ASCII character with hexadecimal code HH, at most \x7F (any byte in a character literal)
\u{H...}the Unicode code point with hexadecimal code H... (1 to 6 digits)
 
print("name:\t\"Pepper\"");   # name:	"Pepper"
print("caf\u{e9}");            # café

Any other character after a backslash is an error:

main.pep:2:13: error: unknown escape sequence '\q'
    2 |     print("a\qb");
      |             ^~

Other built-in types

  • void means “no value”. It is the return type of a function that returns nothing.
  • Array<T> is a growable array of T; int[] is shorthand for Array<int>, and int[][] for Array<Array<int>>. See Array.
  • Optional<T> is a value that may be missing; int? is shorthand for Optional<int>. See Optional values.
  • HashMap<K, V> and HashSet<K> are hash tables. See HashMap and HashSet.
  • Result<TValue, TError> and Error describe operations that can fail. See Error handling with Result.
  • Atomic<T> is a value that threads can update safely. See Atomics and Threads and shared data.

Raw pointers and __null

For talking to C, Pepper has raw pointers, written __ptr<T> (for example __ptr<char> or __ptr<void>), and the null pointer literal __null:

var p: __ptr<void> = __null;
assert(p == __null, "p is null");

Most programs never need them. Calling C covers pointers in full.

Naming conventions

Primitive types and keywords are lowercase: int, float, bool, var, while. Structs, including the ones the runtime provides, are PascalCase: String, Array<T>, HashMap<K, V>, Result<TValue, TError>. Follow the same convention for your own structs. Functions and variables are usually written in snake_case.

Identifiers are made of ASCII letters, digits and underscores, and cannot start with a digit. Avoid names that start with two underscores: the compiler and runtime use them for their own names, such as __ptr and __null.

Variables

Declare a variable with var and let Pepper infer its type from the initial value, or write the type yourself. Prefer var whenever the type is obvious from the value.

# Declaring variables: inferred, explicitly typed, constant, and module-level.

# Module-level variables (globals) live for the whole program. Any thread
# can reach them, so they are const and of a thread-safe type: a number, or
# an Atomic for state that changes.
const max_requests = 10;
const request_count = Atomic<int>(0);
const total_bytes = Atomic<long>(0);

record(bytes: int) {
    request_count.fetch_add(1);
    total_bytes.fetch_add(bytes);
}

main(): int {
    # 'var' infers the type from the initializer.
    var answer = 42;          # int: integer literals are int
    var ratio = 0.25;         # double: floating-point literals are double
    var ready = true;         # bool
    var name = "Pepper";      # String

    # Write the type first, C style...
    int width = 80;
    float scale = 1.5;

    # ...or after the name with a colon.
    var height: int = 24;
    var offset: long = 5000000000;

    # A variable declared without a value starts at zero.
    var hits: int;
    assert(hits == 0, "numbers start at zero");

    # 'const' declares a name that should not be reassigned.
    const limit = 3;

    record(100);
    record(250);
    assert(request_count.load() == 2, "two requests recorded");
    assert(total_bytes.load() == 350, "350 bytes in total");
    assert(request_count.load() < max_requests, "globals are visible in every function");

    assert(answer + width + height == 146, "sum of ints");
    assert(ratio * 4 == 1.0 && scale == 1.5, "floating point");
    assert(ready && name == "Pepper", "bool and String");
    assert(offset > 4000000000 && limit == 3, "long and const");
    return 0;
}

The three forms are:

  • var name = value; infers the type from the value.
  • Type name = value; is the C style, with the type first.
  • var name: Type = value; puts the type after the name.

A variable declared without a value starts out as zero: 0 for numbers, false for bool, and null for a struct type such as String. Give struct variables a value before you use them.

When var infers the type of an arithmetic expression, it takes the type the arithmetic produces under C’s rules (see Arithmetic follows C): var m = 1 + 0.5; is a double holding 1.5, and with u8 small = 250;, var s = small + 10; is an int holding 260.

Constants

const declares a name that you do not intend to change:

const limit = 3;
const ratio: double = 0.5;

Assigning to a const, with =, a compound assignment, ++ or --, is an error:

main.pep:5:5: error: cannot assign to const variable 'limit'
    5 |     limit = 4;
      |     ^~~~~
main.pep:1:7: note: 'limit' declared const here
    1 | const limit = 3;
      |       ^~~~~

Module-level variables

A variable declared outside any function is a module-level (global) variable. It exists for the whole run of the program and every function in the file can read it. Its initializer can be any expression, including a function call, and runs before main. Like other top-level declarations, a global is private to its file unless you mark it export (see Visibility).

Any function can run on any thread, so every thread can reach a global. It must therefore be declared const and have a thread-safe type, such as a number, an Atomic<T>, a ThreadLocal<T>, or a [Shared] struct (see Module-scope variables). A global that changes is an Atomic<T>:

const max_retries = 3;
const requests = Atomic<long>(0);   # changed with requests.fetch_add(1)

Casting with as

value as Type converts a value to another type explicitly, like static_cast in C++:

var ratio = 7.9;
var whole = ratio as int;   # 7
var wide = whole as long;
  • Floating point to integer truncates toward zero: -7.9 as int is -7.
  • Integer to a narrower integer keeps the low bits: 70000 as u16 is 4464.
  • Integer to a wider integer sign-extends a signed source and zero-extends an unsigned one: (-1 as i16) as long is -1, and (65535 as u16) as long is 65535.
  • Casting between unrelated struct types is a compile error (cannot cast to Q).

as binds more tightly than the arithmetic operators, so x * 10 as int means x * (10 as int). Put parentheses around the expression you want to convert: (x * 10) as int. C-style casts such as (int)x do not exist.

Many conversions also happen without as: assigning or passing a number to a variable or parameter of another numeric type converts it, as in C, including narrowing conversions (int n = 2.7; stores 2).

Arithmetic follows C

Arithmetic on numbers behaves exactly as it does in C (the “usual arithmetic conversions”), so code ported from C gives the same results:

  • Integer promotion. Types narrower than int (char, u8, short, u16, and bool) are converted to int before any arithmetic. The sum of two chars is an int, so 100 + 100 gives 200 rather than wrapping at 8 bits.
  • Mixed widths use the wider type: int + long is a long.
  • Signed and unsigned. When both operands have the same width and one is unsigned, the operation is unsigned: comparing int -1 with uint 1 treats -1 as 4294967295, so -1 < 1 is false for those types.
  • Integer and floating point gives floating point: 3 * 0.5 is 1.5.
  • Division of integers truncates toward zero (-7 / 2 is -3), and % takes the sign of the left operand (-7 % 3 is -1).
  • Shifts take the type of the (promoted) left operand. >> keeps the sign for signed types and fills with zeros for unsigned types.
  • Overflow of unsigned arithmetic wraps around. As in C, signed overflow, division by zero, and shifting by the operand’s width or more are not defined; do not rely on what they do.
# Arithmetic follows C's rules for promotion and conversion.
main(): int {
    # Integer division truncates toward zero; % takes the sign of the left side.
    assert(7 / 2 == 3, "7 / 2 is 3");
    assert(-7 / 2 == -3, "-7 / 2 is -3");
    assert(-7 % 3 == -1, "-7 % 3 is -1");

    # Types narrower than int are promoted to int before arithmetic,
    # so the sum of two chars does not wrap at 8 bits...
    char a = 100;
    char b = 100;
    int sum = a + b;
    assert(sum == 200, "char + char is computed as int");

    # ...but storing the result back into a char does.
    char c = a + b;
    assert(c == -56, "200 does not fit in a char");

    # Mixing signed and unsigned of the same width is unsigned, as in C.
    int minus_one = -1;
    uint one = 1;
    assert(!(minus_one < one), "-1 converts to 4294967295 when compared with a uint");
    uint zero = 0;
    assert(zero - 1 == 4294967295, "unsigned arithmetic wraps");

    # Mixing an integer with a floating-point value gives floating point.
    int whole = 3;
    double half = 0.5;
    double product = whole * half;
    assert(product == 1.5, "3 * 0.5 is 1.5");

    # 'as' converts explicitly. Floating point to integer truncates toward zero.
    assert((7.9 as int) == 7, "7.9 as int is 7");
    assert((-7.9 as int) == -7, "-7.9 as int is -7");
    assert(((whole as double) / 2) == 1.5, "divide as double");

    # Assignment converts implicitly, including from double to int.
    int truncated = 2.7;
    assert(truncated == 2, "2.7 stored in an int is 2");

    # Shifts: >> is arithmetic for signed types and logical for unsigned types.
    assert(-8 >> 1 == -4, "signed shift keeps the sign");
    uint bits = 0x80000000;
    assert(bits >> 31 == 1, "unsigned shift fills with zeros");
    return 0;
}

Operators

# Operators on numbers and booleans.
main(): int {
    # Arithmetic.
    assert(2 + 3 * 4 == 14, "* binds tighter than +");
    assert((2 + 3) * 4 == 20, "parentheses group");
    assert(17 % 5 == 2, "remainder");
    assert(-(3) == -3, "negation");

    # Exponentiation is right-associative: 2 ** 3 ** 2 is 2 ** 9.
    assert(2 ** 10 == 1024, "power");
    assert(2 ** 3 ** 2 == 512, "right-associative");

    # Comparison yields a bool.
    var small = 3 < 5;
    assert(small, "3 < 5");
    assert(5 >= 5 && 4 <= 5 && 6 > 5 && 5 == 5 && 5 != 6, "comparisons");
    # On numbers, `===` and `!==` are the same as `==` and `!=`.
    assert(5 === 5 && 5 !== 6, "identity on numbers");

    # Logical operators; 'and' and 'or' are spellings of && and ||.
    var t = true;
    var f = false;
    assert(t && !f, "and, not");
    assert(f || t, "or");
    assert(t and (f or t), "word forms");

    # Bitwise operators.
    assert((6 & 3) == 2, "and");
    assert((6 | 3) == 7, "or");
    assert((6 ^ 3) == 5, "exclusive or");
    assert(~0 == -1, "complement");
    assert(1 << 4 == 16 && 256 >> 2 == 64, "shifts");

    # Compound assignment and increment.
    var n = 5;
    n += 3;   # 8
    n -= 2;   # 6
    n *= 4;   # 24
    n /= 5;   # 4
    n %= 3;   # 1
    n <<= 3;  # 8
    n >>= 1;  # 4
    n &= 6;   # 4
    assert(n == 4, "compound assignment");
    n++;
    ++n;
    n--;
    assert(n == 5, "increment and decrement");

    # The conditional (ternary) operator.
    var sign = n > 0 ? "positive" : "not positive";
    assert(sign == "positive", "ternary");
    return 0;
}
KindOperators
Arithmetic+ - * / % **, unary - and +
Comparison== != < <= > >=
Identity=== !== (two references to the same object; plain equality for numbers and bool)
Logical&& (or and), || (or or), !
Bitwise& | ^ ~ << >>
Assignment= += -= *= /= %= <<= >>= &= |= ^=
Increment++ --, prefix or postfix
Conditionalcondition ? a : b

&& and || short-circuit: the right operand is evaluated only when the left one does not decide the result, so items.count() > 0 && items[0] == 1 never reads items[0] from an empty array.

** raises an integer to an integer power, and is right-associative: 2 ** 3 ** 2 is 2 ** 9. The exponent can be any integer expression. A negative exponent gives the truncated real result: 1 for a base of 1, 1 or -1 for a base of -1, and 0 otherwise (2 ** -1 is 0).

Some operators have limits today:

  • ** works only on integers. A floating-point operand is a compile error (unsupported op Pow).

Operators bind in this order, tightest first:

  1. Postfix: calls f(x), indexing a[i], member access a.b, x++, x--
  2. **
  3. as
  4. Unary +, -, !, ~
  5. Prefix ++, --
  6. *, /, %
  7. +, -
  8. <<, >>
  9. <, <=, >, >=
  10. ==, !=, ===, !==
  11. &
  12. |, ^
  13. &&, and
  14. ||, or
  15. ? :
  16. = and the compound assignments (right-associative)

As in C, & binds more loosely than ==, so write (flags & mask) == mask with the parentheses.

Statements and semicolons

Every simple statement ends with a ;: a variable declaration, an expression, return, break and continue. So does every declaration that does not end in a block: a module variable, a struct field, a type alias, an import, a re-export and an extern function. A statement or declaration that ends in a block (if, while, for, with, a function, a struct) needs none.

Line breaks, spaces and comments mean nothing to the grammar. A statement can span as many lines as it needs, breaking anywhere between tokens, including before an operator or a ., and several statements can share a line.

# Every simple statement ends with a `;`; line breaks mean nothing.
is_small(n: int): bool {
    return n > 0 && n < 10;
}

main(): int {
    var width = 3;
    var height = 4; var depth = 5;

    # A statement may span several lines, breaking before or after an
    # operator.
    var volume = width * height
        * depth;
    assert(volume == 60, "3 * 4 * 5");

    var in_range = is_small(width)
        && is_small(height)
        && !is_small(volume);
    assert(in_range, "3 and 4 are small; 60 is not");

    var words = Array<String>();
    words.append("line");
    words.append("breaks");
    var long_words = 0;
    # A chain of calls may break before each `.`.
    for (var word in words
        .iterate()
        .filter((w) => w.length() > 4)) {  # comments are fine anywhere
        long_words += 1;
    }
    assert(long_words == 1, "one word is longer than four characters");

    # So may a call's arguments.
    assert(volume + words[0].length() == 64,
        "60 + 4");
    return 0;
}

Leaving out a ; is deprecated

Semicolons used to be optional. Code that leaves one out still compiles, with a warning at the place the ; belongs and a fix that adds it. The language server offers it as a quick fix, and pep-fmt adds them all:

main.pep:2:18: warning: omitting ';' is deprecated; end the statement with ';' [deprecated]
    2 |     var total = 1
      |                  ^
      |                  ;

A later version makes a missing ; an error.

Without its ;, a statement runs on for as long as the next line can continue it, since line breaks do not end anything. So

x = y
-z

is x = y - z, and a line that starts with ( calls the expression on the line above. The one exception is a declaration: until a missing ; becomes an error, a declaration without one ends at the end of its line, so var x = 1 followed by y = 2 stays two statements.

Control flow

Pepper has the familiar structured control flow of C-family languages: if and else to choose, while and for to repeat, and break, continue and return to leave early.

if and else

if runs a block when its condition is true, and the optional else block when it is false. The parentheses around the condition are optional; the braces around each block are required, even for a single statement.

# Branching with if and else.
classify(n: int): String {
    if n < 0 {
        return "negative";
    } else if n == 0 {
        return "zero";
    } else {
        return "positive";
    }
}

main(): int {
    var temperature = 25;

    # Parentheses around the condition are optional; braces are required.
    if temperature > 30 {
        print("hot");
    } else {
        print("not hot");
    }

    if (temperature > 20) {
        temperature = 20;
    }
    assert(temperature == 20, "clamped to 20");

    assert(classify(-5) == "negative", "negative");
    assert(classify(0) == "zero", "zero");
    assert(classify(7) == "positive", "positive");

    # For a choice between two values, the ternary operator is shorter.
    var parity = temperature % 2 == 0 ? "even" : "odd";
    assert(parity == "even", "20 is even");

    # && and || stop at the first operand that decides the result, so the
    # count check guards the index.
    var readings = Array<int>();
    var starts_high = false;
    if (readings.count() > 0 && readings[0] > 100) {
        starts_high = true;
    }
    assert(!starts_high, "an empty array does not start high");
    return 0;
}

The condition is usually a bool, but a number also works: any value other than zero counts as true, as in C.

Chains of conditions

else if tests another condition when the ones before it were false. The first true condition’s block runs, and the final else block, if there is one, runs when none was true:

if x > 0 {
    return 1;
} else if x < 0 {
    return -1;
} else {
    return 0;
}

For a choice between two values, the conditional operator condition ? a : b is often shorter than an if.

Guarding a check

&& and || short-circuit, as in C: the right operand is evaluated only when the left one does not already decide the result. A guard can therefore come first in the same condition:

if items.count() > 0 && items[0] > 100 {
    print("starts high");
}

When items is empty, items[0] is never read.

while

while repeats its block as long as the condition is true. The condition is checked before each iteration, so the body may run zero times. As with if, the parentheses are optional and the braces are required.

var countdown = 3;
while countdown > 0 {
    countdown -= 1;
}

for

for runs its block once for each element of an Array, each code point of a String, or each value of any type that follows the iterator protocol:

for (var score in scores) {
    total += score;
}

The loop variable can be declared with var or a type (for (int score in scores), for (var score: int in scores)), and the parentheses are optional. Iteration covers the protocol behind for and how to make your own types iterable.

Pepper has no counting for (init; condition; step) loop and no range syntax. To count, use a while loop with a counter.

with

with enters a scope object for the length of a block and binds what it hands out. The object cleans up when it is destroyed, on every way out of the block:

with (var session = Session("grace")) {
    greet(session.user);
}

Scoped blocks with with covers the protocol and the optional else block.

match

match runs the arm for the case an enum value holds, and the compiler checks that every case is handled:

match (light) {
    Red, Amber => { stop(); }
    Green => { go(); }
}

Enums and match covers patterns and the else arm.

break and continue

break leaves the innermost loop immediately. continue skips the rest of the current iteration and goes on with the next one; in a while loop that means checking the condition again, so update the counter before any continue.

# Looping with while and for, and leaving early with break and continue.
main(): int {
    # while repeats as long as its condition is true.
    var countdown = 3;
    var launched = false;
    while countdown > 0 {
        countdown -= 1;
    }
    launched = countdown == 0;
    assert(launched, "counted down to zero");

    # for visits each element of an Array (or any other iterable).
    var scores = Array<int>();
    scores.append(70);
    scores.append(85);
    scores.append(92);
    var total = 0;
    for (var score in scores) {
        total += score;
    }
    assert(total == 247, "sum of the scores");

    # continue skips to the next iteration; break leaves the loop.
    var odd_sum = 0;
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 100) {
        i += 1;
        if (i % 2 == 0) { continue; }
        if (i > 9) { break; }
        odd_sum += i;
    }
    assert(odd_sum == 25, "1 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 9");

    # break and continue apply to the innermost loop.
    var pairs = 0;
    for (var a in scores) {
        for (var b in scores) {
            if (b == a) { break; }
            pairs += 1;
        }
    }
    assert(pairs == 3, "(85, 70), (92, 70), (92, 85)");
    return 0;
}

Both work in while and for loops and always apply to the innermost loop. There are no labeled breaks: to leave several loops at once, set a flag and test it, or move the loops into a function and return from it.

return

return value; leaves the current function and hands value back to the caller. It works from anywhere in the function, including from inside loops. See Functions.

In a function that returns nothing, a bare return; leaves the function early.

Functions

A function is a named block of code that takes parameters and can return a value. Functions declared at the top level of a file are free functions; functions declared inside a struct are methods, covered in Structs.

Declaring a function

Write the name, the parameters in parentheses as name: Type, and the return type after a colon:

area(width: int, height: int): int {
    return width * height;
}

Pepper also accepts a C-style form, with the return type first and each parameter’s type before its name:

int perimeter(int width, int height) {
    return 2 * (width + height);
}

Both forms declare the same kind of function; pick one style and use it consistently. The first form is the one used throughout this book.

# Declaring and calling functions.

# Parameters are written 'name: Type'; the return type follows the ')'.
area(width: int, height: int): int {
    return width * height;
}

# The C-style form puts the return type first and the type before each name.
int perimeter(int width, int height) {
    return 2 * (width + height);
}

# A function that returns nothing leaves out the return type
# (or writes ': void', or 'void' in front).
# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const log_count = Atomic<int>(0);
log(message: String) {
    log_count.fetch_add(1);
    print("log: " + message);
}

# Functions can call themselves, and can be declared in any order.
factorial(n: int): long {
    if (n <= 1) { return 1; }
    return n * factorial(n - 1);
}

main(): int {
    assert(area(3, 4) == 12, "3 x 4");
    assert(perimeter(3, 4) == 14, "2 x (3 + 4)");
    assert(factorial(20) == 2432902008176640000, "20!");
    assert(is_even(10), "declared after main, still callable");

    log("starting");
    log("done");
    assert(log_count.load() == 2, "logged twice");
    return 0;
}

is_even(n: int): bool {
    return n % 2 == 0;
}

  • Order does not matter. A function can call functions declared later in the file, and can call itself.

  • The program starts at main. main(): int returns the process’s exit code; return 0 for success.

  • Calls must match the declaration. Passing the wrong number of arguments is a compile error:

    main.pep:3:12: error: too many arguments to 'add': expected 2, have 3
    3 |     return add(1, 2, 3);
      |            ^~~

Functions that return nothing

A function that returns no value leaves out the return type. You can also write : void, or void in front in the C-style form:

log(message: String) { print(message); }
log_line(message: String): void { print(message); }
void log_c(String message) { print(message); }

Such a function ends when it reaches the closing brace, or earlier at a bare return;:

log_positive(n: int) {
    if n <= 0 {
        return;
    }
    print(n);
}

A call to such a function has no value, so it cannot be stored, passed, returned, or used as an operand or condition: var x = log("hi"); reports cannot use a value of type 'void' as variable 'x'.

In a function that returns a value, return; is an error:

main.pep:2:5: error: non-void function 'f' should return a value
    2 |     return;
      |     ^~~~~~

Inferred return types

A function without a return type gets one from its return statements. It returns void when no return has a value, and otherwise the type of the first return that has one, so half(x: double) { return x / 2; } returns a double. main with no return type returns an int, and reaching its closing brace exits with 0.

Writing the return type is still clearer for a function that returns a value, and it lets the compiler check each return against it.

Parameters

Parameters are passed by value. For numbers and bool, the function receives its own copy. For a String, an Array or any other struct, the value is a reference, so the function receives another reference to the same object: it can change the object’s contents, and the caller sees the change. Assigning a new object to the parameter does not affect the caller’s variable. Memory and reference counting describes exactly how references are counted when you pass them.

Arguments convert to the parameter’s type the same way assignment does, so you can pass an int where a long or double is expected, and a String where a struct with an [Implicit] constructor from String (such as io.Path) is expected.

Default parameter values

A parameter can have a default value, written = value after its type. A call that leaves the argument out gets the default. Put parameters with defaults at the end of the list, since arguments are matched from the left.

# Default parameter values.

# Parameters with defaults come last; a call may leave them out.
greet(name: String, greeting: String = "Hello", punctuation: String = "!"): String {
    return greeting + ", " + name + punctuation;
}

# Defaults work with the C-style form too.
int clamp(int value, int low = 0, int high = 100) {
    if (value < low) { return low; }
    if (value > high) { return high; }
    return value;
}

main(): int {
    assert(greet("Ada") == "Hello, Ada!", "both defaults");
    assert(greet("Ada", "Hi") == "Hi, Ada!", "one default");
    assert(greet("Ada", "Hi", ".") == "Hi, Ada.", "no defaults");

    assert(clamp(150) == 100, "clamped to the default high");
    assert(clamp(-5) == 0, "clamped to the default low");
    assert(clamp(-5, -10) == -5, "low given");
    return 0;
}

Defaults work the same way for methods and constructors. Pepper has no named arguments: to give a later parameter a value, you pass every parameter before it.

Returning several values

A function returns several values as a tuple, and the caller destructures it:

divide(a: int, b: int): (int, int) {
    return (a / b, a % b);
}

var (quotient, remainder) = divide(17, 5);

Overloading

Functions of one file can share a name when their parameter lists differ, and each call picks the best fit for its arguments. See Overloading.

Generic functions

A function can take type parameters in angle brackets after its name, such as first<T>(items: T[]): T. Generics covers them.

Printing with print

print is a built-in function that writes its arguments to standard output, each on its own line. It accepts any number of arguments, and each one can be a String, an integer, a bool (printed as 1 or 0), or a floating-point number.

# The print builtin writes each argument on its own line.
main(): int {
    print("Hello, world!");     # a String
    print(42);                  # an integer
    print(true);                # a bool prints as 1 or 0
    var name = "Pepper";
    print("Hi, " + name, 3 + 4); # two lines: "Hi, Pepper" and "7"
    return 0;
}

To print several values on one line, build a String with + first.

A float or double prints with six digits after the decimal point, rounded: print(0.5) prints 0.500000.

For checks in your programs, the runtime also provides assert(condition, message), which stops the program with message when condition is false, and panic(message), which always stops it. Both take the message as a String, so it can be built at run time (assert(n > 0, name + " must be positive")); a string literal message costs nothing when the assertion holds. See Built-in functions.

Functions as values

A function’s name used without a call is a value that you can store in a variable, pass to another function, or return, and a closure such as (x: int) => x * 2 is a function written where it is used. See Function types and closures.

Tuples and multiple return values

A tuple groups a fixed number of values, each of its own type, into one value. Its main use is returning several values from a function:

# A function returns several values as a tuple.
divide(a: int, b: int): (int, int) {
    return (a / b, a % b);
}

# Elements can be of any type, and a tuple can hold another tuple.
describe(n: int): (String, (bool, int)) {
    return ("number", (n % 2 == 0, n * n));
}

main(): int {
    # Read elements by position: t.0, t.1, ...
    var t = divide(17, 5);
    assert(t.0 == 3, "the quotient");
    assert(t.1 == 2, "the remainder");

    # Nested tuples chain: d.1.0 is element 0 of element 1.
    var d = describe(4);
    assert(d.0 == "number", "the name");
    assert(d.1.0 && d.1.1 == 16, "even, and its square");

    # A tuple type can be written out; the literal converts to it.
    var wide: (long, double) = (1, 2);
    assert(wide.0 == 1 && wide.1 == 2.0, "converted elements");
    return 0;
}

Tuple types and values

  • A tuple type lists its element types in parentheses: (int, String), (bool, (int, int)). A tuple has at least two elements.
  • A tuple value lists its elements the same way: (a / b, a % b). The compiler infers its type from the elements, so (1, "x") is an (int, String).
  • Where a tuple type is expected (a declared variable, a parameter, a return value), each element converts to its element type, as it would on its own: var wide: (long, double) = (1, 2);.
  • t.0, t.1, … read an element by position, counting from 0. t.1.0 is element 0 of element 1. An element that does not exist is a compile error.
  • Elements are read-only. t.0 = 3 is a compile error; build a new tuple instead: t = (3, t.1);.

Destructuring

var (a, b) = value; declares one variable for each element of a tuple:

struct Point {
    var x: int = 0;
    var y: int = 0;
}

min_max(values: Array<int>): (int, int) {
    var low = values[0];
    var high = values[0];
    for (var v in values) {
        if (v < low) {
            low = v;
        }
        if (v > high) {
            high = v;
        }
    }
    return (low, high);
}

main(): int {
    var values = Array<int>();
    values.append(4);
    values.append(-2);
    values.append(9);

    # Destructure the result into new variables.
    var (low, high) = min_max(values);
    assert(low == -2 && high == 9, "both ends");

    # `_` skips an element; a binding may state its type.
    var (_, top: long) = min_max(values);
    assert(top == 9, "only the maximum");

    # `const` makes every binding constant.
    const (first, last) = (values[0], values[2]);
    assert(first == 4 && last == 9, "constants");

    # Assign to existing places. The right side is evaluated first,
    # so this swaps.
    var a = 1;
    var b = 2;
    (a, b) = (b, a);
    assert(a == 2 && b == 1, "swapped");

    var p = Point();
    (p.x, p.y) = min_max(values);
    assert(p.x == -2 && p.y == 9, "fields are places too");
    return 0;
}
  • The number of names must match the number of elements.
  • _ skips an element.
  • A name may state its type, var (_, top: long) = …; the element converts to it. Without one, the variable takes the element’s type.
  • const (a, b) = … declares constants.
  • (a, b) = value; assigns to places that already exist: variables, fields (p.x) and array elements. The whole right side is evaluated before any assignment, so (a, b) = (b, a) swaps.

Tuples and references

A tuple is a value, like a number. It is not an object on the heap, and copying it copies its elements. An element that is a reference is shared, not copied, exactly as if you had assigned it to a variable:

  • Building a tuple from a variable adds a reference to the object; building it from a function result takes over that result’s reference.
  • A tuple holds its references until it goes out of scope or is replaced, then releases them.
  • Destructuring moves each element into its variable. After var (a, b) = pair();, a holds the only reference to its object, just as it would after var a = make();.

A tuple is thread-safe when all its elements are, so a module-scope const origin: (int, long) = (0, 0); is allowed.

Current limitations

These come in later phases:

  • Tuples cannot be generic type arguments yet (Array<(int, String)>, HashMap<String, (int, int)>), and there is no Tuple<A, B> spelling.
  • Tuples cannot be compared with ==, hashed, or passed to print.
  • Elements cannot be named ((line: int, column: int)).
  • Destructuring does not nest (var (a, (b, c)) = …) and does not work in a for loop (for (var (k, v) in map)).
  • match cannot match tuple patterns.

Function types and closures

Functions in Pepper are values. You can store a function in a variable, a field or an array, pass it to another function, and return it. This lets you choose behavior at run time: a sort order, a callback, a step in a pipeline. A function value is a named function, a method, a constructor, or a closure: an anonymous function written where it is used.

Function types

A function type is written (parameter types) => return type:

TypeMeaning
(int) => inttakes an int, returns an int
(String, int) => booltakes a String and an int, returns a bool
() => voidtakes nothing, returns nothing
(int) => (int) => inttakes an int, returns an (int) => int

=> is right-associative, so the last row is a function that returns a function. A function type can be used anywhere a type can: for variables, parameters, return types, struct fields, generic arguments such as Array<(int) => int>, and type aliases.

Written with shared in front, as in shared () => void, a function type is thread-safe: its values can be passed to other threads. See Sharing function values between threads.

Referring to a named function

The name of a free function, written without a call, is a value of that function’s type. Calling a function value calls the function:

# Function types and references to named functions.
square(x: int): int { return x * x; }
negate(x: int): int { return -x; }

# A parameter can take a function: its type is '(parameter types) => return type'.
apply(f: (int) => int, x: int): int {
    return f(x);
}

twice(f: (int) => int, x: int): int {
    return f(f(x));
}

# Generic functions can take function values too.
apply_twice<T>(f: (T) => T, x: T): T {
    return f(f(x));
}

main(): int {
    # A function's name without a call is a value of its function type.
    var f = square;                 # f: (int) => int
    assert(f(3) == 9, "call through a local");

    var g: (int) => int = negate;   # the type can be written out
    assert(g(4) == -4, "explicit function type");

    # Function variables can be reassigned to another function of the same type.
    f = negate;
    assert(f(6) == -6, "reassigned");

    assert(apply(square, 5) == 25, "pass a function as an argument");
    assert(twice(square, 3) == 81, "(3 * 3) * (3 * 3)");
    assert(twice(negate, 7) == 7, "negating twice");
    assert(apply_twice<int>(square, 2) == 16, "generic");
    return 0;
}
  • var f = square; infers f’s type, (int) => int, from the function. An overloaded name, or an overloaded method (p.move), needs a declared type instead, which picks the member.
  • A function-typed variable can be reassigned to any function of the same type.
  • A parameter of function type, such as f: (int) => int, lets the caller pass in the behavior. Generic functions can take function parameters too.

Methods, constructors and generic functions

Methods, constructors and specializations of generic functions are values too:

# Methods, constructors and generic functions used as values.
struct Point {
    int x;
    int y;
    construct(this.x = 0, this.y = 0) {}

    norm(): int {
        return x + y;
    }

    shifted(dx: int): Point {
        return Point(x + dx, y);
    }
}

larger<T>(a: T, b: T): T {
    return a > b ? a : b;
}

combine(f: (int, int) => int, a: int, b: int): int {
    return f(a, b);
}

main(): int {
    var p = Point(3, 4);

    # A bound method: the method of one object, which it keeps alive.
    var norm = p.norm;
    assert(norm() == 7, "bound method");

    # An unbound method takes the object as its first argument.
    var norm_of: (Point) => int = Point.norm;
    assert(norm_of(Point(1, 1)) == 2, "unbound method");

    # A struct's name is its constructor.
    var make: (int, int) => Point = Point;
    assert(make(5, 6).y == 6, "constructor");

    # A generic function with its type arguments names one specialization.
    assert(combine(larger<int>, 2, 9) == 9, "generic function");

    var steps = Array<(int) => Point>();
    steps.append(p.shifted);
    steps.append(Point(10, 0).shifted);
    assert(steps[1](5).x == 15, "methods in an array");
    return 0;
}
WrittenTypeCalls
p.norm() => intp.norm(), on the object p referred to when the value was made
Point.norm(Point) => intnorm on the object passed as the first argument
Point.origin (a static method)() => PointPoint.origin()
Point(int, int) => Pointthe constructor
Box<int>(int) => Box<int>the constructor of Box<int>
larger<int>(int, int) => intthe specialization larger<int>
  • Bound methods. p.norm keeps a reference to the object, like a closure () => p.norm() that captured p, so the object lives as long as the value does. The expression before the . is evaluated once, when the value is made. Inside a method, write this.norm; a bare norm is not a value. Storing this.norm in a field of this would be a reference cycle, and is an error, as for closures.
  • Traits and extensions. A method declared in an extension works the same way, and so does a method of a trait-typed value (shape.area), which calls through the trait.
  • Generic functions need their type arguments: larger<int>, not larger.
  • A type with a constructor that takes no arguments. Box<int> on its own names the constructor only when the constructor needs at least one argument; otherwise it is a call, Box<int>(). The same goes for a generic function whose parameters all have defaults.

Storing and returning functions

Function values go wherever other values go: struct fields, arrays, module-level variables and return values. A type alias gives a long function type a short name, and can have type parameters of its own.

# Function values in fields, arrays, globals, and return values.
square(x: int): int { return x * x; }
negate(x: int): int { return -x; }
increment(x: int): int { return x + 1; }

struct Transform {
    var op: (int) => int;
    construct(this.op = square) {}
    run(x: int): int { return op(x); }
}

# Choose a function at run time and return it.
pick(squared: bool): (int) => int {
    if squared {
        return square;
    }
    return negate;
}

# A module-level function variable. Every module-level variable is const
# and thread-safe: an Atomic lets it change, and a function type every thread
# can reach is declared shared.
const handler = Atomic<shared (int) => int>(increment);

# A type alias can name a function type, even a generic one.
type Combine<T> = (T, T) => T;
add(a: int, b: int): int { return a + b; }
fold(values: int[], combine: Combine<int>, start: int): int {
    var total = start;
    for (var v in values) {
        total = combine(total, v);
    }
    return total;
}

# A function that returns nothing has the return type void.
const events = Atomic<int>(0);
record(n: int) { events.fetch_add(n); }

main(): int {
    var t = Transform();
    assert(t.run(4) == 16, "the default op squares");
    t.op = negate;
    assert(t.op(4) == -4, "call through a field");

    var pipeline = Array<(int) => int>();
    pipeline.append(increment);
    pipeline.append(square);
    pipeline.append(negate);
    var value = 2;
    for (var step in pipeline) {
        value = step(value);
    }
    assert(value == -9, "-((2 + 1) * (2 + 1))");
    assert(pipeline[1](5) == 25, "call an array element");

    assert(pick(true)(5) == 25, "call a returned function directly");
    var chosen = pick(false);
    assert(chosen(5) == -5, "call a stored returned function");

    assert(handler.load()(1) == 2, "global function value");
    handler.store(square);
    assert(handler.load()(3) == 9, "replaced global");

    var numbers = Array<int>();
    numbers.append(1);
    numbers.append(2);
    numbers.append(3);
    assert(fold(numbers, add, 10) == 16, "10 + 1 + 2 + 3");

    var callback: (int) => void = record;
    callback(2);
    callback(5);
    assert(events.load() == 7, "void function values");
    return 0;
}
  • Fields. A field of function type is called like a method: t.op(4). Inside the struct, op(x) and this.op(x) both call it.
  • Arrays. pipeline[1](5) indexes the array and calls the element.
  • Module-level variables. Every module-level variable is const and thread-safe (see Module-scope variables), so one whose function changes is an Atomic of a shared function type: handler.load()(1) calls the current function and handler.store(square) replaces it.
  • Return values. pick(true)(5) calls the function that pick returns.
  • void results. A function without a return value has a function type ending in => void, such as (int) => void.

Closures

A closure literal is a parameter list, =>, and a body. The body is an expression or a block:

# Closure literals: anonymous functions that capture values.
struct Counter {
    int count;
    construct(this.count = 0) {}
    add(n: int) { count += n; }
}

apply(f: (int) => int, x: int): int {
    return f(x);
}

# A closure can outlive the function that made it: it keeps its own copy
# of 'n'.
make_adder(n: int): (int) => int {
    return (x) => x + n;
}

struct Scaler {
    int factor;
    construct(this.factor = 1) {}

    # Inside a method, a closure can use 'this' and the struct's members.
    scaler(): (int) => int {
        return (x) => x * factor;
    }
}

main(): int {
    # Parameter types written out: the closure's type is (int) => int.
    var double = (x: int) => x * 2;
    assert(double(21) == 42, "expression body");

    # With a function type to fill, the parameter types can be left out.
    var add: (int, int) => int = (a, b) => a + b;
    assert(add(40, 2) == 42, "types from the variable");
    assert(apply((x) => x * x, 5) == 25, "types from the parameter");

    # A block body, with the return type written after the parameters.
    var clamp = (x: int, low: int, high: int): int => {
        if x < low { return low; }
        if x > high { return high; }
        return x;
    };
    assert(clamp(7, 0, 5) == 5, "block body");

    # Captured values.
    var add_five = make_adder(5);
    assert(add_five(10) == 15, "captured parameter");
    var offset = 100;
    var shift = (x: int) => x + offset;
    assert(shift(1) == 101, "captured local");

    # A captured struct is shared, so the closure can change the object.
    var counter = Counter();
    var bump = () => counter.add(1);
    bump();
    bump();
    assert(counter.count == 2, "shared struct");

    var triple = Scaler(3).scaler();
    assert(triple(4) == 12, "captured this");

    # A closure can return a closure.
    var adder: (int) => (int) => int = (a) => (b) => a + b;
    assert(adder(2)(3) == 5, "curried");
    return 0;
}

  • Parameter types. (x: int) => x * 2 writes the parameter type. When the closure goes straight into a slot of function type (a typed variable, a parameter, an assignment, a return, a field), the types come from that slot, so (a, b) => a + b is enough. A parameter of a generic function counts too: passing (x) => x + 1 to apply<T>(f: (T) => T, x: T) with x an int types the closure’s x as int (see Generics). With no slot to take them from, a parameter without a type is an error:

    main.pep:2:14: error: cannot infer the type of parameter 'x'; write it: (x: Type) => ...

  • Return type. It is inferred from the body, or from the slot. To write it, put it after the parameters: (x: int): long => x.

  • Body. An expression body is the value the closure returns. A block body uses return like a function. A closure whose type returns void can have an expression body such as () => counter.add(1); the value is discarded.

  • Calling. A closure is called like any function value, and can be called where it is written: ((x: int) => x + 1)(2).

Captured values

A closure can use the local variables and parameters around it. It keeps a copy of each one it uses, made when the closure is created, so it can outlive the function that created it (make_adder above).

A captured variable must never be assigned, before or after the closure is created, inside the closure or outside it. Then the copy and the variable can never differ:

var count = 0;
var f = () => count;
count = 5;   # error

main.pep:4:5: error: cannot assign to 'count' because a closure captures it
main.pep:3:19: note: captured here; a closure keeps a copy, so the variable must not change

The rule applies to ++, -- and compound assignments too. Only the variable is fixed: a captured struct is a reference to the same object, so the closure can call its methods and change its fields (counter above). To share a changing number, capture a struct that holds it, such as a Counter or an Atomic<int>.

this in methods

A closure written in a method can use this, the struct’s fields and its methods. It captures this (one reference to the object), never a copy of the fields, so it sees the fields’ current values.

Storing such a closure in a field of the same object would make the object and the closure keep each other alive forever (a reference cycle), so that direct case is an error:

main.pep:4:15: error: storing a closure that captures 'this' in field 'measure' of 'this' creates a reference cycle that is never freed

Capture a weak reference to the object instead.

Type checking

A function value must match the type it flows into exactly: the same parameter types in the same order and the same return type. The aliases of a primitive type count as the same type, so (i32) => i32 and (int) => int match. A mismatch in an initializer, an assignment, an argument or a return is a compile error, as is calling a function value with the wrong number of arguments:

square(x: int): int { return x * x; }

main(): int {
    var f: (String) => int = square;   # error
    return f(1, 2);                    # error
}

error: variable 'f' expects a function of type (String) => int, but was given a function of type (int) => int
a function of type (String) => int takes 1 argument(s), but 2 were given

These two errors do not show a source location yet.

Sharing function values between threads

A plain function type such as () => void can hold any function value, including a closure that captures a String or an Array. Such values stay on the thread that made them: their reference counts are plain increments, like those of a struct that is not [Shared] (see What threads can share).

A function type written with shared in front, such as shared () => void or shared (long) => void, is thread-safe. Its reference counts are atomic, and its values hold only thread-safe state, so any thread may copy, call and drop them.
