It goes wherever a thread-safe type is required: the body of a thread (ThreadBody is shared () => void), a field of a [Shared] struct, a module-scope variable, or Atomic<shared (int) => int>.

# shared function types: function values that threads can share.
import async;

const total = Atomic<long>(0);

# A named function holds no state, so it is always thread-safe.
add_one() {
    total.fetch_add(1);
}

# A [Shared] struct's fields must be thread-safe: a function field is shared.
[Shared]
struct Task {
    const weight: long;
    const run: shared (long) => void;
    construct(this.weight = 1, this.run = (n: long) => {}) {}
}

# A plain function type takes any function of the same signature.
apply(f: (long) => long, x: long): long {
    return f(x);
}

main(): int {
    # A closure converts to a shared type when everything it captures is
    # thread-safe: here nothing but the module-scope Atomic.
    var add: shared (long) => void = (n: long) => { total.fetch_add(n); };
    var task = Task(10, add);

    # A thread body is a shared () => void. This closure captures a
    # [Shared] struct.
    var worker = async.Thread.spawn(() => { task.run(task.weight); });
    var other = async.Thread.spawn(add_one);
    worker.join();
    other.join();
    assert(total.load() == 11, "both threads ran");

    # A shared value can be used where a plain one is expected.
    var step: long = 5;
    var add_step: shared (long) => long = (x: long) => x + step;
    assert(apply(add_step, 1) == 6, "shared value passed as plain");

    # A plain closure may capture anything, but stays on this thread.
    var name = "worker";
    var greet = () => name + " done";
    assert(greet() == "worker done", "plain closure");
    return 0;
}

  • What converts to shared. A named function, which holds no state; a method, constructor or generic function value with nothing captured, such as Point.norm or Box<int>; and a closure literal (or a bound method such as p.norm) whose captured values are all thread-safe: numbers, Atomic<T> and other [Shared] structs, weak references to them, and shared function values. The value must be written where the shared type is expected, so that it is made thread-safe from the start.

  • What does not. A closure that captures a value that is not thread-safe is reported at the capture, with a note at the captured variable:

    main.pep:5:46: error: closure passed as 'shared () => void' captures 'name' of type 'String', which is not thread-safe
    5 |     var t = async.Thread.spawn(() => { print(name); });
      |                                              ^~~~
main.pep:4:9: note: capture a thread-safe value instead, such as an 'Atomic<T>' or a '[Shared]' struct
    4 |     var name = "worker";
      |         ^~~~

    A closure in a method that uses this or a member captures this, so it is thread-safe only in a [Shared] struct. A value that already has a plain type (a variable, a parameter, a call result) never becomes shared, even when it holds nothing:

    main.pep:3:32: error: cannot initialize variable 'f' of type 'shared () => void' with a value of type '() => void'
    3 |     var f: shared () => void = g;
      |                                ^

  • From shared to plain. A shared value converts to the plain type of the same signature anywhere: shared (long) => long can be passed to a parameter of type (long) => long. The conversion allocates a small plain function value that holds the shared one and calls it, so the plain copy counts with plain increments while the shared value keeps its atomic ones.

  • Inference. A variable initialized with a closure, such as var f = () => 1;, has a plain type. Write the type to make it shared: var f: shared () => int = () => 1;.

  • shared is special only directly before a function type; elsewhere it is an ordinary name.

Null function values

A function-typed variable or field that has never been assigned is null. Calling it stops the program with the message called a null function value:

var handler: (int) => int;
handler(1);   # panics: called a null function value

Names and shadowing

A local variable, parameter or field with the same name as a free function hides the function inside its scope. Inside a function with a parameter named square, square means the parameter, not the function.

Memory

A function value is a reference, like a struct value, and follows the same reference-counting rules (see Memory and reference counting). Referring to a named function allocates nothing: every reference to square shares one static function object. A method, constructor or generic function value allocates one small object, like a closure; a bound method’s object holds a reference to its receiver.

Creating a closure allocates one object holding the captured values, like constructing a struct. Copying the closure adds a reference to that object, and when the last reference goes, the object releases what it captured.

A plain function value’s counts are plain increments; a shared one’s are atomic, like a [Shared] struct’s (see Sharing function values between threads).

What is not supported yet

  • Generic functions without type arguments and extern functions cannot be referenced as values. larger alone is not a value even where the expected type would pick larger<int>. To use an extern function as a value, wrap it in a closure: (x: int) => abs(x).
  • Generic methods cannot be referenced as values: b.map<long> is not a value. Wrap the call in a closure: (f: (int) => long) => b.map(f).
  • A bare method name inside a method (var f = norm;) is not a value; write this.norm.
  • Closures in trait default methods cannot use this or the members of the struct yet.
  • Closures that capture values as C callbacks. A C function pointer (extern (P) => R) takes a named function or a closure that captures nothing; see Calling C.

Overloading

Several free functions in one file can share a name when their parameter lists differ. Each call picks the one whose parameters fit its arguments best:

# Overloaded free functions: one name, several parameter lists.

import io;

describe(n: long): String { return "a number"; }
describe(x: double): String { return "a fraction"; }
describe(s: String): String { return "some text"; }
describe(s: String, times: int): String { return "text, repeated"; }

# An exact match beats a widening, which beats a conversion; an
# `[Implicit]` constructor comes last.
size(p: io.Path): int { return 1; }
size(n: long): int { return 2; }

main(): int {
    var small: int = 7;
    assert(describe(small) == "a number", "int widens to long");
    assert(describe(2.5) == "a fraction", "exact match");
    assert(describe("hi") == "some text", "a literal promotes to String");
    assert(describe("hi", 3) == "text, repeated", "picked by argument count");

    assert(size(4) == 2, "an integer literal fits a long");
    assert(size("notes.txt") == 1, "only io.Path takes a String, by its [Implicit] constructor");
    return 0;
}

The functions of one name in one file are an overload set, and each is a member of it. Methods of one name in a struct are a set too, and so are its static methods and its operators. Constructors and extension methods do not overload yet (see What is not supported yet).

Declaring overloads

  • The parameter lists must differ in the number or the types of the parameters. Parameter names and default values do not count, and neither does the return type:

    main.pep:2:1: error: functions that differ only in their return type cannot be overloaded
    2 | f(x: int): long { return 2; }
      | ^
main.pep:1:1: note: previous declaration is here
    1 | f(x: int): int { return 1; }
      | ^

    Two functions with the same parameter types and the same return type are a redefinition of 'f'.

  • One file, one set. A file’s own functions hide an imported function of the same name, as before; overloads do not merge across files.

  • One visibility per set. The members are all exported or all private, so a set is imported or hidden as a whole:

    util.pep:2:1: error: overloads of 'scale' must all be exported or all be private
    2 | scale(x: double): double { return x * 3.0; }
      | ^~~~~
util.pep:1:8: note: other overload declared here
    1 | export scale(x: int): int { return x * 2; }
      |        ^~~~~

    Importing the name (import [scale] from util, or the whole file) brings every member, and import [scale as grow] renames the whole set.

  • extern functions do not overload: a C function has one symbol. Wrap it in a Pepper function to give it company.

How a call picks a member

A member is viable when the call has between its required and total number of parameters and each argument converts to its parameter. For each argument, the conversion has a rank, best first:

RankWhat converts
exactthe same type; a string literal to __ptr<char>
promotionan integer widening that loses nothing (int to long, u8 to int; the narrower target ranks better, so a short picks int over long), float to double, an integer literal to any integer type it fits, a struct to a trait it declares, __null to a reference, a string literal to String
conversionany other numeric conversion (bool included), and pointer conversions
implicit constructora value to a struct whose [Implicit] constructor takes it

One viable member is better than another when none of its arguments ranks worse and at least one ranks better, as in C++. The call picks the member that is better than every other viable one. When there is none, the call is an error that lists the candidates:

main.pep:3:22: error: call to 'f' is ambiguous
    3 | main(): int { return f(1); }
      |                      ^
main.pep:1:1: note: candidate function
    1 | f(x: long): int { return 1; }
      | ^
main.pep:2:1: note: candidate function
    2 | f(x: short): int { return 2; }
      | ^

An integer literal fits every integer type that holds its value equally well, so f(1) above is ambiguous; an int member would take it exactly. Two widenings to types of the same width (a ushort to long and ulong) are ambiguous too; convert the argument yourself (f(n as long)). When no member is viable, each note says why:

main.pep:5:12: error: no matching function for call to 'f'
    5 |     return f(s);
      |            ^
main.pep:1:1: note: candidate function not viable: no known conversion from 'String' to 'int' for 1st argument
    1 | f(x: int): int { return 1; }
      | ^
main.pep:2:1: note: candidate function not viable: requires 2 arguments, but 1 was provided
    2 | f(x: int, y: int): int { return 2; }
      | ^

The member picked decides the call’s type, so var x = half(9.0); is a double when half(x: double): double is picked. In the editor, hovering the call shows the member it picked and how many others the set has, and go-to-definition goes to that member.

Generic members

A set can mix generic and non-generic members. A generic member is viable when its type arguments can be inferred from the call, and ranks as an exact match for the arguments that inferred them. When a generic and a non-generic member tie, the non-generic one wins. Writing type arguments (describe<int>(7)) considers only the generic members:

# Generic and non-generic overloads, and overloads called from generic code.

# A non-generic overload wins a tie with a generic one.
describe<T>(value: T): String { return "something"; }
describe(value: int): String { return "an int"; }

# Generic overloads can differ in their number of parameters.
last<T>(a: T): T { return a; }
last<T>(a: T, b: T): T { return b; }

kind(n: int): String { return "number"; }
kind(s: String): String { return "text"; }

# Each specialization resolves `kind` for its own `T`.
label<T>(value: T): String { return kind(value); }

main(): int {
    assert(describe(7) == "an int", "non-generic wins the tie");
    assert(describe(1.5) == "something", "only the generic fits");
    assert(describe<int>(7) == "something", "type arguments pick generic overloads");

    assert(last(1) == 1, "one argument");
    assert(last(1, 2) == 2, "two arguments");

    assert(label(3) == "number", "label<int> calls kind(int)");
    assert(label("x") == "text", "label<String> calls kind(String)");
    return 0;
}

A call inside a generic function or a generic struct’s method whose argument types depend on a type parameter is resolved again for each specialization, so label<String> above calls kind(s: String).

An override such as describe<Point>(p: Point) belongs to the generic member with the same number of type parameters and parameters; it is not a member itself.

Methods and operators

A struct’s methods of one name are an overload set, and a call picks among them the same way, whether it is written m.add(x), this.add(x), or add(x) inside a method. Static methods overload among themselves (Money.of(250)), and operators are methods, so +(Money) and +(int) can coexist:

# Overloaded methods, static methods and operators.

struct Money {
    var cents: long;

    construct(long cents = 0) {
        this.cents = cents;
    }

    # Methods of one name in a struct overload like free functions.
    add(other: Money): Money { return Money(cents + other.cents); }
    add(dollars: int, cents: int): Money { return Money(this.cents + dollars * 100 + cents); }

    # Static methods overload among themselves.
    static of(cents: long): Money { return Money(cents); }
    static of(text: String): Money { return Money(text.size() as long); }

    # Operators are methods, so they overload by operand type too.
    +(other: Money): Money { return add(other); }
    +(more: int): Money { return Money(cents + more); }
}

main(): int {
    var a = Money.of(250);
    var b = a.add(1, 5);                  # add(int, int)
    assert(b.cents == 355, "two ints");
    assert(a.add(b).cents == 605, "a Money");

    var c = a + b + 10;                   # +(Money), then +(int)
    assert(c.cents == 615, "operators");
    assert(Money.of("four").cents == 4, "static of(String)");

    # A method used as a value takes the member its type names.
    var plus: (Money) => Money = a.add;
    assert(plus(a).cents == 500, "method value");
    return 0;
}

  • A static method and an instance method may not share a name, since a bare of(x) inside a method could mean either:

    main.pep:3:12: error: static method 'get' cannot share its name with an instance method of 'S'
    3 |     static get(x: String): int { return 2; }
      |            ^~~
main.pep:2:5: note: previous declaration is here
    2 |     get(x: int): int { return 1; }
      |     ^~~

  • In a generic struct, each specialization resolves its own calls: in Holder<int>, has(x: T) takes an int. Two members that become the same after substitution (has(x: T) and has(x: String) in Holder<String>) are only an error when a call cannot tell them apart.

  • A method as a value (m.add, Money.of) picks its member from the expected function type, like a free function below; without one the error is cannot infer the type of 'f' from overloaded method 'Money.add'.

Extensions and traits

An extension’s methods join the struct’s overload set of their name, and a trait may declare several methods of one name. Each of a trait’s overloads is implemented by the conforming type’s method with the same parameter types, and a call through a value of the trait type picks among the trait’s members the same way:

struct Square {
    var side: int = 0;

    construct(this.side = 0) {}

    area(): int {
        return side * side;
    }
}

# An extension's methods join the struct's overload set of their name.
extend Square {
    area(scale: int): int {
        return area() * scale;
    }
}

# A trait may declare overloads too; each is its own member.
trait Shape {
    area(): int;
    area(scale: int): int;

    # A default joins the conforming type's set of its name.
    describe(): int {
        return area() * 100 + area(2);
    }
}

# Each trait member is implemented by the method with its parameter types.
extend Square with Shape {}

total(shape: Shape): int {
    return shape.area() + shape.area(10);
}

main(): int {
    var square = Square(3);
    assert(square.area(2) == 18, "the extension's overload");
    assert(total(square) == 99, "each call through the trait takes its own member");
    assert(square.describe() == 918, "the default body calls both");
    return 0;
}
  • An extension member with a body method’s parameters is redefinition of 'area', with a note at the method it repeats.
  • A call sees only the extensions visible in its file (see Visibility): a private extension’s overload is never a candidate in another file, so importing a file can add candidates to a call. If an added candidate makes a call ambiguous, the error’s notes point at each candidate, the extension’s included.
  • A trait’s default method is copied into a conforming type that lacks a method with its parameter types, even when the type has other methods of that name; the copy joins their set.
  • A missing overload is named with its parameter types: 'Square' does not implement 'area(int)', required by trait 'Shape'.

Overloaded functions as values

The name of an overload set, written without a call, is a value only where the expected function type picks one member exactly: a typed variable, an argument, a return from a function with a declared return type, or an assignment. A closure whose parameters have no types, passed to an overloaded function, picks the member whose function parameter has its number of parameters:

# An overloaded function used as a value, and closures passed to overloads.

twice(n: int): int { return n * 2; }
twice(s: String): String { return s + s; }

apply(f: (int) => int, n: int): int { return f(n); }

# Overloads that differ in the type of a function parameter.
run(f: (int) => int): int { return f(10); }
run(f: (int, int) => int): int { return f(10, 20); }

main(): int {
    # The declared type picks the overload.
    var doubler: (int) => int = twice;
    assert(doubler(4) == 8, "twice(int)");

    # So does the parameter it is passed to.
    assert(apply(twice, 5) == 10, "twice(int) again");

    # A closure without parameter types picks the overload of its arity.
    assert(run((a) => a + 1) == 11, "one parameter");
    assert(run((a, b) => a + b) == 30, "two parameters");
    return 0;
}

Without an expected type, the compiler asks for one:

main.pep:4:13: error: cannot infer the type of 'g' from overloaded function 'f'
    4 |     var g = f;
      |             ^
main.pep:1:1: note: candidate function
    1 | f(x: int): int { return 1; }
      | ^
main.pep:2:1: note: candidate function
    2 | f(x: long): int { return 2; }
      | ^
main.pep:4:13: note: declare the type, for example 'var g: (int) => int = f;'
    4 |     var g = f;
      |             ^

An expected type that matches no member is address of overloaded function 'f' does not match required type '(bool) => int'.

What is not supported yet

  • Constructors do not overload: a struct has one constructor. Use default arguments, or a static method for each alternative.
  • Unary operators cannot be declared at all yet.
  • extern functions do not overload.
  • Overloads across files do not merge: a file’s own functions of a name hide an imported set of that name.

Structs

A struct groups related values into one named type, and gives that type behavior through methods. Pepper has no classes and no inheritance: a struct holds data, methods and extensions add behavior, and traits describe what a type can do.

Every struct value is a reference to an object, so assigning a struct or passing it to a function shares the object rather than copying it. The memory chapter explains exactly when objects are created and destroyed.

Declaring a struct

Declare a struct with struct, a name, and its fields in braces. Struct names are PascalCase by convention.

# A struct groups related values into one type.
struct Point {
    var x: int = 0;
    var y: int = 0;
}

# Fields can also be declared C-style, with the type first.
struct Counter {
    int count;          # no initializer: starts at zero
    String label = "clicks";
}

main(): int {
    # Without a constructor, `Point()` makes a Point with every field
    # at its initial value.
    var p = Point();
    assert(p.x == 0 && p.y == 0, "fields start at their initializers");

    p.x = 3;
    p.y = 4;
    assert(p.x * p.x + p.y * p.y == 25, "fields can be read and written");

    var c = Counter();
    assert(c.count == 0, "a numeric field without an initializer is zero");
    assert(c.label == "clicks", "a String field can have an initializer");

    # A struct value is a reference: `q` names the same Point as `p`.
    var q = p;
    q.x = 10;
    assert(p.x == 10, "p and q are the same object");
    assert(p === q, "=== tests whether two references name the same object");
    assert(!(p === Point()), "a new Point is a different object");
    return 0;
}

Things to notice:

  • A field is declared like a variable: var x: int = 0; or, C-style, int x = 0;. The initializer is optional.
  • A field without an initializer starts at its type’s default: zero for numbers, false for bool, and null for a struct type such as String or another struct. Reading a field through a null reference crashes the program, so give struct-typed fields a value before you use them.
  • A struct with no constructor can be created with its name and empty parentheses, Point(). There is no new keyword at the call site.
  • p.x reads a field and p.x = 3 writes it.
  • a === b is true when a and b refer to the same object, and a !== b when they do not. See Comparing structs for ==.

A struct can have a field whose type is a struct declared later in the file, a field of its own type, or fields that lead back to it through other structs (a Team holding a Player that holds the Team). A struct-typed field holds a reference, so a linked list or a tree is just a struct that names itself:

# A field can hold the struct's own type: each Link points at the next one.
struct Link {
    var value: int;
    var next: Link;

    construct(this.value = 0) {}
}

# Two structs can refer to each other, in either declaration order.
struct Team {
    var name: String;
    var lead: Player;

    construct(this.name = "") {}
}

struct Player {
    var name: String;
    # A weak back pointer: the team owns its lead, so the player's link
    # back to the team must not keep the team alive (see the memory chapter).
    var team: Weak<Team>;

    construct(this.name = "") {}
}

sum(first: Link): int {
    var total = 0;
    var link: Link = first;
    while (link !== __null) {
        total = total + link.value;
        link = link.next;
    }
    return total;
}

main(): int {
    var list = Link(1);
    list.next = Link(2);
    list.next.next = Link(3);
    assert(sum(list) == 6, "the list holds 1, 2 and 3");
    assert(list.next.next.next === __null, "the last link has no next");

    var team = Team("Owls");
    team.lead = Player("Ada");
    team.lead.team = Weak<Team>(team);
    with (var t = team.lead.team) {
        assert(t.name == "Owls", "the player reaches its team");
    }
    return 0;
}

Objects that refer to each other in a loop keep each other alive, so make back pointers such as a child’s parent a Weak<T> (see Cycles leak).

Constructors

A constructor sets up a new object. Declare it with construct, followed by its parameters and a body. Callers create the object by calling the struct’s name like a function.

struct Rect {
    var width: int;
    var height: int;
    var area: int;

    # `this.width = 1` declares a parameter that is stored straight into the
    # field `width`; `1` is its default when the caller leaves it out.
    construct(this.width = 1, this.height = 1) {
        area = width * height;
    }
}

struct Temperature {
    var celsius: float;

    # Ordinary parameters work too, written like a function's: `name: Type`.
    construct(fahrenheit: float = 32.0) {
        celsius = (fahrenheit - 32.0) * 5.0 / 9.0;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var r = Rect(3, 4);
    assert(r.width == 3 && r.height == 4, "arguments fill the fields");
    assert(r.area == 12, "the constructor body runs after the fields are set");

    var tall = Rect(2);
    assert(tall.width == 2 && tall.height == 1, "omitted arguments use their defaults");

    var unit = Rect();
    assert(unit.area == 1, "every parameter has a default, so Rect() works");

    var boiling = Temperature(212.0);
    assert(boiling.celsius == 100.0, "212 F is 100 C");
    assert(Temperature().celsius == 0.0, "the default is 32 F");
    return 0;
}
  • A parameter written this.width = 1 stores its argument directly into the field width, before the body runs. The = 1 is required: it is the value used when the caller omits that argument.
  • Ordinary parameters are written as in a function, name: Type, with an optional default: fahrenheit: float = 32.0. The C-style form, float fahrenheit = 32.0, is accepted too.
  • Inside the body, fields are in scope by name. Use this.name when a parameter has the same name as a field.
  • A struct has at most one constructor, and declaring a second is an error (redefinition of the constructor of 'S'). Constructors do not overload yet: use default arguments, or a static method for each alternative way to build the object. Likewise, a struct has at most one destructor.

A this.field parameter must have a default. Leaving it out is an error:

struct Point {
    var x: int;
    construct(this.x) {}
}

main.pep:3:21: error: expected '='
    3 |     construct(this.x) {}
      |                     ^

A struct without a constructor accepts no arguments:

main.pep:7:13: error: too many arguments to 'Point': expected 0, have 2
    7 |     var p = Point(1, 2);
      |             ^~~~~

Note: new is the older, deprecated spelling of construct. It still compiles, with a warning that offers the fix (see Deprecations).

Implicit conversions

A constructor marked [Implicit] also converts. Wherever a value flows into a place whose type is the struct, and the value has the constructor’s parameter type, the compiler calls the constructor for you:

struct Celsius {
    var degrees: double;

    # `[Implicit]` lets a `double` become a `Celsius` wherever one is expected.
    [Implicit]
    construct(double degrees) {
        this.degrees = degrees;
    }
}

struct Label {
    var text: String;

    # Without `[Implicit]`, callers write `Label("...")` themselves.
    construct(String text) {
        this.text = text;
    }
}

describe(t: Celsius): String {
    return (t.degrees > 30.0) ? "hot" : "mild";
}

main(): int {
    assert(describe(35.0) == "hot", "an argument converts");
    var today: Celsius = 21.5;            # a typed variable converts
    assert(describe(today) == "mild", "already a Celsius");

    var label = Label("kitchen");         # no conversion: call the constructor
    assert(label.text == "kitchen", "explicit constructor call");

    var n = 12.0;                         # no slot type: stays a double
    assert(n == 12.0, "untyped local");
    return 0;
}

The standard library uses this for io.Path, which converts from a String, so dir.join("logs") and io.File.open("notes.txt") work without writing io.Path(...), and for Error, so Result<int>.err("bad input") works.

  • Where it applies: a variable’s initializer when the variable’s type is written, an assignment, a function, method or constructor argument (operators and a[i] = value included), a call through a function value, and a return from a function with a declared return type.
  • Where it does not: the receiver of a method call ("docs".join("x") does not turn "docs" into a Path), a var whose type is inferred (var n = 12.0; stays a double), and __null, which stays a null reference.
  • One conversion per value. The value must already have the constructor’s parameter type, or reach it by a widening that loses nothing: a smaller integer to a larger one, an integer literal that fits, float to double, or a struct to a trait it declares. Conversions never chain: if Tag converts from Name and Name from String, a String does not become a Tag.
  • Overloads prefer anything else. Among overloads, a member that takes the value as it is, or by any other conversion, beats one that needs an [Implicit] constructor.
  • Each conversion is a constructor call, so it allocates a new object. The VS Code extension shows the call as an inlay hint around the converted value, and hovering the value names the constructor. The converted value is a temporary like any constructor call’s result: it is released after the call it was passed to (see Memory and reference counting).
  • Only a constructor that can be called with one argument may be [Implicit]: one parameter, or a first parameter followed by parameters that all have defaults. It may not convert from the struct itself or, in a generic struct, from a bare type parameter (construct(T value)):
main.pep:2:5: error: 'Implicit' constructor of 'Span' must be callable with one argument
    2 |     [Implicit]
      |     ^~~~~~~~~~

Conversions are opt-in: a constructor without [Implicit] never runs on its own. When a value would fit such a constructor, the type error says so:

main.pep:6:27: error: cannot pass a value of type 'String' as argument 1 of 'show', which has type 'Label'
    6 | main(): int { return show("x"); }
      |                           ^~~
main.pep:3:5: note: 'Label' has a constructor from 'String': call 'Label(...)', or mark the constructor [Implicit]
    3 |     construct(String text) { this.text = text; }
      |     ^~~~~~~~~

Destructors

A destructor runs when an object is destroyed, which happens when the last reference to it goes away. Declare it with destruct and a body, with no parentheses. Use it to release something the object holds that Pepper does not manage for you, such as a file handle from C.

# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const closed = Atomic<int>(0);

struct Connection {
    var name: String;

    construct(this.name = "") {}

    # The destructor runs once, when the last reference to the object goes
    # away. Use it to release whatever the object holds.
    destruct {
        closed.fetch_add(1);
    }
}

main(): int {
    {
        var c = Connection("db");
        assert(closed.load() == 0, "still in use");
    }
    # `c` went out of scope at the closing brace.
    assert(closed.load() == 1, "the destructor ran at the end of the block");
    return 0;
}

You never call a destructor yourself. After it runs, Pepper releases the object’s fields, so a destructor can still read them. The memory chapter lists every point where an object can be destroyed.

Methods

A method is a function declared inside a struct. You call it on an object with a dot, v.length_squared(), and inside it this refers to that object.

struct Vector {
    var x: int = 0;
    var y: int = 0;

    construct(this.x = 0, this.y = 0) {}

    # A method: name, parameters, then the return type after `:`.
    length_squared(): int {
        # Fields can be named directly inside a method...
        return x * x + y * y;
    }

    # ...or through `this`, the object the method was called on.
    dot(other: Vector): int {
        return this.x * other.x + this.y * other.y;
    }

    # Methods can change fields, and can call other methods.
    scale(factor: int) {
        x = x * factor;
        y = y * factor;
    }

    # The return type can also come first, C-style.
    bool is_zero() {
        return length_squared() == 0;
    }

    # A static method belongs to the type, not to one object: there is
    # no `this`. Call it on the type name.
    static unit_x(): Vector {
        return Vector(1, 0);
    }
}

main(): int {
    var v = Vector(3, 4);
    assert(v.length_squared() == 25, "method call");
    assert(v.dot(Vector(1, 1)) == 7, "method with an argument");

    v.scale(2);
    assert(v.x == 6 && v.y == 8, "a method can modify its object");

    assert(Vector().is_zero(), "methods can be called on a temporary");
    assert(Vector.unit_x().x == 1, "static method called on the type");
    return 0;
}
  • Methods are written like functions: name(parameters): ReturnType { ... }. Leave out the return type for a method that returns nothing. The C-style form, bool is_zero() { ... }, works too.
  • Inside a method, the object’s fields and other methods can be named directly (x, length_squared()), or through this (this.x). A parameter or local with the same name as a field hides the field; use this. to reach the field.
  • A method can change the fields of its object. Because struct values are references, the caller sees the change.
  • Calling a method does not copy the object or add a reference to it. The result of a method call is a new reference, like any function result.
  • Several methods can share a name when their parameters differ; each call picks the best fit (see Overloading).

Static methods

A method marked static belongs to the type rather than to one object. It has no this, and you call it on the type name: Vector.unit_x(). Static methods are the usual way to offer named constructors, such as the standard library’s Result<int>.ok(42).

Operators

A struct can define what the operators do for it. Declare a method whose name is the operator, written like any other method: ==(other: Money): bool. The C-style form with the return type first, bool ==(Money other), is accepted too.

struct Money {
    var cents: int = 0;

    construct(this.cents = 0) {}

    # `a == b` calls `a.==(b)`. Without this method, `==` on two structs
    # compares identity, like `===`.
    ==(other: Money): bool {
        return cents == other.cents;
    }

    # `!=` is derived from `==` when the struct does not define it.

    # `a < b` calls `a.<(b)`.
    <(other: Money): bool {
        return cents < other.cents;
    }

    # `a + b` calls `a.+(b)` and returns a new Money.
    +(other: Money): Money {
        return Money(cents + other.cents);
    }

    # `a += b` needs its own method; it is not rewritten to `a = a + b`.
    +=(other: Money): Money {
        cents += other.cents;
        return this;
    }
}

struct Shelf {
    var slots: Array<String>;

    construct() {
        slots = Array<String>();
        slots.append("empty");
        slots.append("empty");
    }

    # An indexer with one parameter handles reads: `shelf[i]`.
    [](index: int): String {
        return slots[index];
    }

    # An indexer with two parameters handles writes: `shelf[i] = value`.
    [](index: int, value: String): String {
        slots[index] = value;
        return value;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var price = Money(250);
    assert(price == Money(250), "== compares cents");
    assert(price != Money(100), "!= compares cents");
    # `===` and `!==` always compare identity: equal cents, different objects.
    assert(price !== Money(250), "!== compares identity");
    var same = price;
    assert(same === price, "=== is true for the same object");

    var total = price + Money(50);
    assert(total.cents == 300, "+ makes a new value");
    assert(price.cents == 250, "+ left its operands alone");

    assert(price < total, "< compares cents");

    total += Money(25);
    assert(total.cents == 325, "+= updated total in place");

    var shelf = Shelf();
    shelf[1] = "books";
    assert(shelf[1] == "books", "indexer write, then read");
    assert(shelf[0] == "empty", "the other slot is unchanged");
    return 0;
}

When the left operand is a struct that declares the operator, a + b becomes the call a.+(b). The right operand is the method’s single parameter, and it can be any type. These binary operators can be defined:

KindOperators
Arithmetic+ - * / % **
Bitwise& | ^ >>
Bitwise shifts<< >>
Comparison and logical== != < <= > >= && ||
Compound assignment+= -= *= /= %= <<= >>= &= |= ^=

Keep these rules in mind:

  • An operator can be declared once per right-operand type (+(Money) and +(int)), and a + b picks by b’s type, as for any overloaded method.
  • Each operator is its own method, with one exception: when a struct defines == but not !=, a != b means !(a == b). += is not rewritten to a = a + b; define each one you want to use.
  • Comparison operators must return bool; any other return type is an error (comparison operator '==' must return 'bool', not 'String'). Logical operators should return bool too. Arithmetic, bitwise and compound assignment operators usually return the struct’s own type; a compound assignment usually changes this and returns it.
  • The left operand decides: a + b uses the method on a’s type. An expression with a number on the left and a struct on the right, such as 2 * v, is not supported.
  • Operators that start with < (<, <=, <<, <<=) can only be declared in the name: Type form, <(other: V): bool: in the C-style form the parser reads bool < as the start of generic arguments.
  • Unary operators (-a, !a, ~a) cannot be overloaded yet.

The result of an operator has the type its method returns, so with -(other: V): int, var r = a - b; is an int.

Comparing structs

Without an == method, a == b on two structs compares identity: it is true only when both refer to the same object, exactly like a === b. The same goes for !=. Define == when two distinct objects with the same contents should count as equal, as String and Array do; != then follows from it. === and !== always compare identity, whatever methods the struct declares, so "a" + "b" == "ab" is true while "a" + "b" === "ab" is false.

Indexers

An indexer lets you write object[index]. Declare it as a method named []:

  • With one parameter, [](index: int): String, it handles reads: shelf[i].
  • With two parameters, [](index: int, value: String): String, it handles writes: shelf[i] = value. The second parameter is the value being stored.

The C-style form, String [](int index), is accepted too.

A struct can have both. With both, compound assignment through an index (counts[i] += 1) reads with the first and writes with the second. Array and String are indexed this way.

Enums and match

An enum is a type with a fixed set of named cases. A value of the enum holds exactly one of them. Use an enum where you would otherwise pass an integer code or a string, so the compiler checks that only the listed cases flow through, and that a match handles each one.

# An enum names a fixed set of cases; a slot of the enum type holds one.
enum Suit {
    Clubs,
    Diamonds,
    Hearts,
    Spades,
}

# A representation sets the stored integer type; a case may give its value.
enum Status: u8 {
    Ok,
    NotFound = 44,
    Failed,
}

struct Card {
    Suit suit;
    int rank = 1;
}

main(): int {
    var card = Card();
    assert(card.suit == Suit.Clubs, "a slot starts at the case whose value is 0");
    card.suit = Suit.Hearts;
    assert(card.suit != Suit.Spades, "cases compare with == and !=");

    # 'as' turns a case into its integer; the reverse is not allowed.
    assert((Suit.Hearts as int) == 2, "cases number from 0");
    assert((Status.Failed as int) == 45, "numbering continues after an explicit value");
    assert(sizeof<Status>() == 1, "Status is stored as a u8");
    return 0;
}

Declaring an enum

enum Name { ... } lists the cases, separated by commas; a comma after the last case is optional. Case names are PascalCase, like types (see naming conventions).

Each case is an integer. The first case is 0 and each case after it is one more than the one before. A case can give its own value with = value, and numbering continues from there:

enum Status: u8 {
    Ok,             # 0
    NotFound = 44,  # 44
    Failed,         # 45
}

A value must be an integer constant: a literal such as -1 or 0x20, or a character such as 'y'. Two cases cannot have the same value.

The type after the colon is the representation, the integer type the enum is stored as. It can be any integer type (u8, short, int, long, and so on). Without one, the enum is stored as an int. Every case value must fit in the representation.

A case can carry a [Target] attribute, so a platform can have cases that others lack. A dropped case does not renumber the others.

Using enum values

Name.Case is a value of the enum type. An enum is a value type, like int: it is copied, not reference counted, and it can be stored in locals, fields, arrays and module-scope constants, and passed to and returned from functions.

  • == and != compare two values of the same enum. No other operator applies to an enum.
  • An enum does not mix with integers or with other enums: int x = Color.Red and Color c = 0 are errors, and so is comparing a Color with a Size.
  • value as int (or any integer type) gives a case’s integer value. The reverse, 1 as Color, is an error, since the integer may not name a case; name the case instead.
  • A slot of an enum type that is not given a value (a field, or a local declared without one) holds the case whose value is 0, or the first case when no case is 0.
  • sizeof<Name>() is the size of the representation.

match

match (value) { ... } runs the arm for the case that value holds:

# 'match' runs the arm of the case a value holds, and must handle every case.
enum Light {
    Red,
    Amber,
    Green,
}

next(light: Light): Light {
    match (light) {
        Red => { return Light.Green; }
        Amber => { return Light.Red; }
        Green => { return Light.Amber; }
    }
    return light;
}

may_go(light: Light): bool {
    match (light) {
        Green => { return true; }
        else => { return false; }
    }
    return false;
}

wait_seconds(light: Light): int {
    match (light) {
        Red, Amber => { return 30; }
        Light.Green => { return 0; }
    }
    return 0;
}

main(): int {
    var light = Light.Red;
    var changes = 0;
    while (!may_go(light)) {
        light = next(light);
        changes += 1;
    }
    assert(changes == 1, "red turns green");
    assert(wait_seconds(Light.Amber) == 30, "one arm handles two cases");
    assert(wait_seconds(light) == 0, "a pattern may name the enum");
    return 0;
}
  • Each arm is one or more patterns, separated by commas, then => and a block. A pattern is a case name (Red) or the case qualified by its enum (Light.Red).
  • An else => { ... } arm, last, handles every case the other arms do not.
  • The arms must handle every case. A missing case is an error that names it, located at match; the language server offers a quick fix that adds an empty arm for each missing case. An else that can never run, because the other arms handle every case, is a warning.
  • Handling a case twice is an error.
  • match is a statement. break, continue and return inside an arm act on the enclosing loop or function, as they would inside an if.

The value is evaluated once, before any arm runs.

Current limits

  • Cases cannot carry values yet (Circle(radius: float)); that is the next step, and it is what lets Optional and Result become enums.
  • Enums cannot have methods, and cannot be generic, yet.
  • match works only on enums, and only as a statement: it does not produce a value, and cannot match integers, characters or strings yet.
  • There is no way to get a case’s name as a string, or to turn an integer back into a case, yet.

Memory and reference counting

Pepper frees memory for you, without a garbage collector and without ownership annotations. Every struct object keeps a count of the references to it, and the object is destroyed the moment that count drops to zero. Because the rules are simple and the same everywhere, you can always tell when an object will be destroyed and its destructor will run.

If you know C++, the whole model fits in one sentence: every struct value behaves exactly like a std::shared_ptr<T> passed by value.

Values and references

Numbers, bool, and the other primitive types are plain values: assigning one copies it, as in C. So is a tuple, whose elements are copied the way each element would be on its own.

Everything else, including your own structs, String, Array, HashMap, and function values, is a reference to an object on the heap. Assigning a reference, passing it to a function, or storing it in a field or an array shares the object. Nothing is copied.

struct Account {
    var balance: int = 0;
}

# The parameter is another reference to the caller's object, so changes to
# its fields are visible to the caller.
deposit(account: Account, amount: int) {
    account.balance += amount;
}

# Rebinding the parameter itself only changes this function's reference.
replace(account: Account) {
    account = Account();
    account.balance = 999;
}

main(): int {
    var mine = Account();
    var yours = mine;               # shares the object; nothing is copied
    yours.balance = 50;
    assert(mine.balance == 50, "assignment shares the object");

    deposit(mine, 25);
    assert(mine.balance == 75, "a function can change the shared object");

    replace(mine);
    assert(mine.balance == 75, "rebinding a parameter does not affect the caller");

    # To get an independent object, make a new one and copy the fields.
    var copy = Account();
    copy.balance = mine.balance;
    copy.balance = 0;
    assert(mine.balance == 75, "the copy is a separate object");
    return 0;
}
  • var yours = mine; makes a second reference to the same Account. Changes through either name are visible through both.
  • A parameter is one more reference to the caller’s object. The function can change the object’s fields, but assigning a new object to the parameter only changes the function’s own reference.
  • To get an independent object, create a new one. Pepper does not copy structs for you; some library types offer a method for it, such as Array’s clone().

A struct-typed variable or field that has not been given a value, such as var next: Account;, is null: it refers to no object. Reading a field or calling a method through a null reference crashes the program. Use === or !== to check whether two references name the same object, or whether one is null (next === __null). For a value that may be absent, prefer Optional<T>, whose type says so.

When objects are destroyed

A reference is held by whatever stores it: a local variable, a parameter, a field, an element of a container, or a temporary value in the middle of an expression. Each of those releases its reference exactly once, when it goes away. When the last reference is released, the object is destroyed: its destructor runs, and then its fields release their own references, which may destroy further objects in turn.

# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const destroyed = Atomic<int>(0);

struct Tracked {
    var name: String;
    construct(this.name = "") {}
    destruct {
        destroyed.fetch_add(1);
    }
}

struct Holder {
    var item: Tracked;
}

make(name: String): Tracked {
    var t = Tracked(name);
    return t;                   # the caller receives the object
}

main(): int {
    # 1. A local is released when its scope ends.
    {
        var a = Tracked("a");
    }
    assert(destroyed.load() == 1, "a was destroyed at the end of its block");

    # 2. While another reference exists, the object stays alive.
    var b = Tracked("b");
    var also_b = b;
    b = Tracked("b2");          # b lets go of the first object...
    assert(destroyed.load() == 1, "...but also_b still holds it");
    also_b = b;                 # the last reference moves away
    assert(destroyed.load() == 2, "the first object is destroyed now");

    # 3. Fields and containers hold references too.
    var holder = Holder();
    {
        var c = Tracked("c");
        holder.item = c;
    }
    assert(destroyed.load() == 2, "holder.item keeps c alive after its scope");
    holder = Holder();          # the old Holder dies and releases c
    assert(destroyed.load() == 3, "releasing the holder released its field");

    var list = Array<Tracked>();
    list.append(Tracked("d"));
    assert(destroyed.load() == 3, "the array holds d");
    list.clear();
    assert(destroyed.load() == 4, "clearing the array released d");

    # 4. Returned objects belong to the caller; unused results are
    #    destroyed right away.
    var e = make("e");
    assert(destroyed.load() == 4, "e is alive in main");
    make("temporary");
    assert(destroyed.load() == 5, "a result nobody keeps is destroyed immediately");
    return 0;
}

Concretely, a reference is released when:

  • A local variable goes out of scope, at the closing brace of the block that declared it, or when the function returns.
  • A variable or field is overwritten. b = Tracked("b2"); releases the object b held before, after taking the new one. Assigning a variable to itself (a = a) changes nothing.
  • A parameter’s function returns. The function held its parameters for as long as it ran.
  • The object that holds a field is destroyed, or a container removes an element (clear(), overwriting list[i], and so on). remove_at() and pop() are the exception: they hand the container’s reference to the caller, so var x = list.remove_at(0); leaves the count unchanged.
  • A temporary is no longer needed. A result that is not stored anywhere, like the one from make("temporary");, is released at the end of the statement. The same goes for a call result passed straight to another function, and for the object in make().name.

Returning a local hands its reference to the caller, so the object survives the function. Calling a method on an object does not add a reference to it: inside the method, this refers to the caller’s object.

Cycles leak

Reference counting cannot free a cycle: objects that refer to each other, directly or through a chain, keep each other’s counts above zero even after nothing else refers to them. Their destructors never run and their memory is never freed.

# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const destroyed = Atomic<int>(0);

struct Person {
    var name: String;
    var friends: Array<Person>;

    construct(this.name = "") {
        friends = Array<Person>();
    }

    destruct {
        destroyed.fetch_add(1);
    }
}

main(): int {
    # Two people who list each other as friends form a cycle: each keeps
    # the other alive, so neither is ever destroyed.
    {
        var ann = Person("Ann");
        var bob = Person("Bob");
        ann.friends.append(bob);
        bob.friends.append(ann);
    }
    assert(destroyed.load() == 0, "the cycle leaked both objects");

    # Break the cycle before letting go, and both are destroyed.
    {
        var cat = Person("Cat");
        var dan = Person("Dan");
        cat.friends.append(dan);
        dan.friends.append(cat);
        dan.friends.clear();    # Dan no longer holds Cat
    }
    assert(destroyed.load() == 2, "without the cycle, both were destroyed");
    return 0;
}

To avoid a cycle, make one direction of it a weak reference (next section), or break the cycle yourself before the last outside reference goes away: clear the container, or overwrite the field, that closes the loop.

A closure holds a reference to every struct it captures, including this, so a closure stored inside an object it captures closes a cycle too. The compiler reports the direct case, a method storing a closure that captures this in a field of this.

Weak references

A Weak<T> points at an object without keeping it alive. Use it for back pointers: a child’s parent, a document’s folder, an observer’s subject. The owner holds strong references down the tree, and everything that points back up holds a Weak<T>, so there is no cycle.

# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const destroyed = Atomic<int>(0);

struct Folder {
    var name: String;
    var files: Array<Document>;

    construct(this.name = "") {
        files = Array<Document>();
    }

    destruct {
        destroyed.fetch_add(1);
    }
}

struct Document {
    var title: String;
    # A weak back pointer: the folder owns its documents, not the other way
    # round, so there is no cycle.
    var folder: Weak<Folder>;

    construct(String title, Folder folder) {
        this.title = title;
        this.folder = Weak<Folder>(folder);
    }

    destruct {
        destroyed.fetch_add(1);
    }

    String path() {
        with (var f = folder) {
            return f.name + "/" + title;
        } else {
            return "(deleted)/" + title;
        }
        return "";
    }
}

main(): int {
    var kept = Document("draft", Folder("none"));
    {
        var home = Folder("home");
        var notes = Document("notes", home);
        home.files.append(notes);
        assert(notes.path() == "home/notes", "the folder is alive");
        kept = notes;
    }
    # The folder went away with `home`; the weak pointer did not keep it.
    assert(kept.path() == "(deleted)/notes", "the folder is gone");
    assert(kept.folder.expired(), "and the weak reference knows it");
    return 0;
}
  • Weak<T>(object) makes the weak reference. It does not change the object’s reference count.
  • A with block reaches the object: with (var f = folder) { ... } binds f to an ordinary strong reference for the length of the block. When the object has already been destroyed, the body is skipped and the else block runs. See Scoped blocks with with.
  • expired() reports whether the object has been destroyed.
  • The object is destroyed on time. When its last strong reference goes, its destructor runs and its fields are released, even if weak references remain. Only the object’s small header stays allocated until the last weak reference is gone.

In C++ terms, a struct value is a std::shared_ptr<T> and Weak<T> wraps the built-in __weak<T>, a std::weak_ptr<T>: Weak<T>(object) is weak_ptr(object), and entering it with with is lock(). A weak reference made while an object is still inside its constructor cannot be upgraded until the constructor returns, as with weak_from_this() in C++.

References and threads

Reference counts are plain, fast increments, so a struct’s objects must stay on one thread. A struct declared [Shared] has atomic counts instead, and its references can be copied and dropped on any thread. The compiler requires such a struct’s fields to be const and thread-safe. See What threads can share.

Function values follow the same split: a plain function type such as () => void has plain counts and stays on one thread, and a shared function type such as shared () => void has atomic counts.

Copying a value with copy

copy(value) makes a copy of a value that shares no object that is not thread-safe with the original, so the copy can go to another thread. A channel copies every value it is sent this way.

var names = Array<String>();
names.append("ada");
var copied = copy(names);
copied[0].append(" lovelace");   # names[0] is still "ada"
  • Every struct object the value reaches is copied once. An object referenced twice is copied once and stays shared inside the copy, and a cycle becomes the same cycle among the copies.
  • Thread-safe objects are not copied: a [Shared] struct or a shared function value in the original is the same object in the copy. For a thread-safe value, copy returns the value itself.
  • A weak reference to an object that was copied points at its copy. A weak reference to an object outside the copied value becomes null.
  • A struct is copied field by field, without running its constructor.

A struct with a destructor or a raw pointer (__ptr<T>) field is not trivially copyable: copying its fields would share what it owns, such as a buffer it frees. Such a struct says how to copy itself by implementing Copyable<T>, with a copy(Copies copies) method that builds the copy and copies each value it holds with copies.copy(value). String, Array, HashMap and HashSet implement it. String.copy() also copies a single String; it replaces the deprecated clone(). Array.clone() is unchanged: it copies the Array but shares its elements.

Copying a value that reaches a struct that is not trivially copyable and does not implement Copyable<T> is an error at the copy call. It names the struct, shows each field on the way to it, says why it cannot be copied, and shows a Copyable<T> to fill in (the editor offers to insert it):

main.pep:14:13: error: cannot copy 'Log' because it contains 'Handle', which is not trivially copyable
   14 |     var c = copy(log);
      |             ^~~~
main.pep:9:5: note: 'Log' reaches it through field 'out'
    9 |     Handle out;
      |     ^~~~~~
main.pep:1:8: note: 'Handle' has a destructor, so copying its fields would release what it owns twice
    1 | struct Handle {
      |        ^~~~~~
note: implement 'Copyable<Handle>' to say how to copy it:
    extend Handle with Copyable<Handle> {
        Handle copy(Copies copies) {
            # Build the copy; copy each struct field with copies.copy(field).
            panic("copying 'Handle' is not implemented");
            return this;
        }
    }

Some types cannot be copied at all: an io.File owns an operating-system handle, so the error says to send its path instead. A plain function value cannot be copied, because it may capture anything; declare it shared. A trait value cannot be copied yet.

Summary

CodeWhat happens to references
var b = a;b takes a new reference to a’s object.
b = c;b takes a reference to c’s object, then releases its old one.
f(a)The parameter takes a reference for the duration of the call.
return a;The caller receives a reference to a’s object.
a.method()No change: this refers to a’s object.
make(); (result unused)The result is released at the end of the statement.
var w = Weak<T>(a);No change to a’s count; w does not keep the object alive.
var c = copy(a);c refers to new objects; a’s count does not change.
end of a blockEvery local declared in the block releases its reference.

Generics

Generics let you write a struct or a function once and use it with many types. Add type parameters in angle brackets after the name, then use them like any other type name. The standard library’s Array<T>, HashMap<K, V>, and Result<TValue, TError> are all ordinary generic structs written this way.

Pepper generics work like C++ templates: each distinct set of type arguments, such as Stack<int> or Stack<String>, produces its own copy of the code, compiled for those exact types. There is no runtime cost for using them.

Generic structs

# `T` is a type parameter: each use such as `Stack<int>` or
# `Stack<String>` makes a separate, fully typed version of the struct.
struct Stack<T> {
    var items: Array<T>;

    construct() {
        items = Array<T>();
    }

    push(item: T) {
        items.append(item);
    }

    pop(): T {
        return items.pop();
    }

    peek(): T {
        # Inside the struct, T can be used like any other type name.
        var top: T = items.last();
        return top;
    }

    is_empty(): bool {
        return items.empty();
    }
}

# Several parameters are separated by commas.
struct Entry<K, V> {
    var key: K;
    var value: V;
    construct(K key, V value) {
        this.key = key;
        this.value = value;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var numbers = Stack<int>();
    numbers.push(1);
    numbers.push(2);
    assert(numbers.peek() == 2, "peek looks at the top");
    assert(numbers.pop() == 2, "last in, first out");
    assert(numbers.pop() == 1, "then the first");
    assert(numbers.is_empty(), "empty again");

    var words = Stack<String>();
    words.push("hello");
    assert(words.pop() == "hello", "the same code works for Strings");

    var e = Entry<String, int>("answer", 42);
    assert(e.key == "answer" && e.value == 42, "two type parameters");
    return 0;
}
  • Name the type parameters after the struct’s name: struct Stack<T>, struct Entry<K, V>. Inside the struct, fields, parameters, return types, and locals can all use them.
  • Name the type arguments whenever you use the struct: Stack<int>(), var s: Stack<String>;.
  • Stack<int> and Stack<String> are different types.

Note: Inside a generic struct, var top = items.last(); cannot infer a type that involves a type parameter yet, and fails with unknown type name 'T'. Write the type out: var top: T = items.last();.

Generic functions

A free function takes type parameters the same way:

larger<T>(a: T, b: T): T {
    if (a > b) {
        return a;
    }
    return b;
}

struct Circle {
    var radius: int = 1;
    area(): int { return 3 * radius * radius; }
}

struct Square {
    var side: int = 2;
    area(): int { return side * side; }
}

# The body can use anything the type argument provides: this works for
# any T that has an `area()` method returning a number.
total_area<T>(shapes: Array<T>): int {
    var sum = 0;
    for (var shape in shapes) {
        sum += shape.area();
    }
    return sum;
}

main(): int {
    # Type arguments come from the arguments, or can be written out.
    assert(larger(3, 9) == 9, "ints");
    assert(larger<float>(2.5, 1.5) == 2.5, "floats");

    var circles = Array<Circle>();
    circles.append(Circle());
    circles.append(Circle());
    assert(total_area(circles) == 6, "circles");

    var squares = Array<Square>();
    squares.append(Square());
    assert(total_area<Square>(squares) == 4, "squares");
    return 0;
}

Pepper infers the type arguments from the arguments, so larger(3, 9) calls larger<int>. You can always write them out, larger<float>(2.5, 1.5), and must when the arguments do not determine every type parameter, for example one that appears only in the return type:

main.pep:4:13: error: cannot infer the type arguments of 'make' from its arguments; write them: make<T>(...)
    4 |     var b = make();
      |             ^~~~
main.pep:1:1: note: declared here
    1 | make<T>(): Box<T> { return Box<T>(); }
      | ^~~~

Inference works through closures in both directions. In transform(words, (w) => w.size()), where transform<T, U>(items: Array<T>, f: (T) => U), words gives T, which types the closure’s parameter w, and the closure’s result gives U.

Because each use is compiled for its concrete types, the body may use any operator, field, or method that the type arguments provide. total_area<T> calls shape.area(), so it works with any T that has an area() method returning a number, and using it with a type that lacks one is a compile error. Pepper does not yet let you state such requirements up front with a trait.

Generic methods

A method can have type parameters of its own, in a struct, a generic struct, an extension, or a trait’s default method. They are inferred at each call like a function’s, or written after the method name:

struct Box<T> {
    T value;
    construct(T v) { value = v; }

    # A method with a type parameter of its own: `U` is chosen per call.
    map<U>(f: (T) => U): Box<U> {
        return Box<U>(f(value));
    }
}

main(): int {
    var b = Box<int>(4);

    # `U` comes from what the closure returns...
    var tens = b.map((x) => x * 10);
    assert(tens.value == 40, "tens");

    var text = tens.map((x) => "forty");
    assert(text.value == "forty", "text");

    # ...or is written after the method name.
    var wide = b.map<long>((x: int): long => x + 1);
    assert(wide.value == 5, "wide");
    return 0;
}
  • Write the type arguments with no space before < or after >: b.map<long>(f). With a space, a.b < c is a comparison.
  • A generic method cannot be called through a trait value, since each of its uses is compiled separately. Call it on the concrete type.
  • The iterator adapters map and filter are generic default methods of Iterator<T>; see Iteration.

Default type arguments

A type parameter can have a default, written Name=Type. When a use leaves that argument out, the default fills it in:

struct Measurement<TValue, TUnit=String> {
    var value: TValue;
    var unit: TUnit;
}

main(): int {
    # `Measurement<float>` fills TUnit with its default, String.
    var length = Measurement<float>();
    length.value = 2.5;
    length.unit = "m";
    assert(length.unit == "m", "TUnit defaulted to String");

    # Any argument can still be given explicitly.
    var count = Measurement<int, int>();
    count.unit = 7;
    assert(count.unit == 7, "TUnit given as int");

    # The standard library's Result uses a default the same way:
    # Result<int> means Result<int, Error>.
    var ok = Result<int>.ok(42);
    assert(ok.value() == 42, "Result with the default error type");
    return 0;
}

Arguments are matched to parameters in order, so put the parameters that have defaults last. The standard library declares struct Result<TValue, TError=Error>, so Result<int> means Result<int, Error>. See Error handling with Result.

Overriding a generic function for one type

You can give a generic function a separate definition for a specific type argument. Write the concrete type where the type parameter would go:

# The generic version handles every type...
describe<T>(value: T): String {
    return "something";
}

# ...and these definitions replace it for two specific types.
describe<int>(value: int): String {
    return "an int";
}

describe<String>(value: String): String {
    return "a String";
}

main(): int {
    assert(describe<float>(1.5) == "something", "the generic version");
    assert(describe<int>(7) == "an int", "the int override");
    assert(describe<String>("hi") == "a String", "the String override");

    # The standard library's hash<String> is such an override: it hashes
    # a String by its content instead of by identity.
    assert(hash<String>("pepper") == hash<String>("pepper"), "content hash");
    return 0;
}

A call whose type argument matches an override, such as describe<int>(7), uses the override; every other call uses the generic version. The standard library uses this to give hash<String> a content-based hash, while hash<T> hashes other structs by identity.

An override can name a built-in type (int, bool, String, and so on) or a struct, such as describe<Point>. It may be declared in any file that can see both the generic function and the struct: here main.pep overrides shapes.pep’s describe<T> for its own Point, and the generic code in shapes.pep uses the override too:

# A generic function, and code that calls it for any type.
export describe<T>(v: T): int {
    return 0;
}

export describe_twice<T>(v: T): int {
    return describe<T>(v) * 2;
}

import "shapes";

struct Point {
    var x: int;
    var y: int;
}

# Overrides shapes.pep's describe<T> for Point, from another file.
describe<Point>(p: Point): int {
    return p.x + p.y;
}

main(): int {
    var p = Point();
    p.x = 3;
    p.y = 4;
    assert(describe<Point>(p) == 7, "the override");
    assert(describe_twice<Point>(p) == 14, "generic code in shapes.pep uses it too");
    assert(describe<int>(5) == 0, "other types keep the generic version");

    # The standard library's generics, such as hash<T>, can be overridden
    # for a struct the same way.
    return 0;
}

A function whose type parameters all name types the file can see (its own, the ones it imports, under any name such as P after import [Point as P] from geo, and the standard library’s) is an override when the file can also see a generic function of that name and number of type parameters (its own, an imported one, or the standard library’s); otherwise a struct’s name is just the name of a type parameter. The generic may be spelled by any name the file has for it (show<Point> after import [describe as show]). A type argument must name a struct, not a type alias or a trait. A generic and another function of the same name in one file are overloads, unless they have the same parameters, or the generic’s type parameters all name structs the file can see without a generic of that name to override: either is an error, since it usually means an override whose type argument the file cannot see, or of a generic the file’s own function of that name hides.

To add methods to a generic struct from outside its declaration, use an extension.

Type aliases

A type alias gives an existing type another name. Use one to shorten a long type that you write often, such as HashMap<String, Array<int>>, or to say what a value means, such as Score instead of int.

Declaring an alias

Write type, the new name, =, and the type it stands for:

# An alias is another name for an existing type.
type Score = int;
type Names = Array<String>;
type Handler = (int) => int;

struct Player {
    var name: String;
    var best: Score = 0;        # aliases work in fields...
}

type Team = Player;

double_it(x: int): int {
    return x * 2;
}

# ...and in parameters and return types.
apply(h: Handler, x: Score): Score {
    return h(x);
}

main(): int {
    var s: Score = 21;
    var plain: int = s;         # Score and int are the same type
    assert(apply(double_it, plain) == 42, "function type alias");

    var names = Names();        # construct through the alias
    names.append("Ada");
    assert(names.count() == 1, "Names is Array<String>");

    var p = Team();
    p.best = s;
    var q: Player = p;          # Team and Player are the same type
    assert(q.best == 21, "struct alias");
    return 0;
}

An alias is only a spelling, not a new type. The compiler replaces every use of the alias with its target before it checks anything else, so Score and int are interchangeable: you can assign one to the other, pass one where the other is expected, and error messages name the target type.

You can use an alias anywhere a type can appear: local and module-level variables, parameters, return types, struct fields, generic arguments, and casts. An alias of a struct also works where the struct’s name would: to construct it (Team()) and to call its static methods.

Generic aliases

An alias can have type parameters of its own, including defaults, just like a generic struct:

# Generic aliases take their own type parameters.
type Predicate<T> = (T) => bool;
type Lookup<V> = HashMap<String, V>;
type Outcome<T, E=Error> = Result<T, E>;

is_even(x: int): bool {
    return x % 2 == 0;
}

count_matching(values: Array<int>, test: Predicate<int>): int {
    var n = 0;
    for (var v in values) {
        if (test(v)) {
            n += 1;
        }
    }
    return n;
}

parse_digit(text: String): Outcome<int> {
    if (text == "7") {
        return Outcome<int>.ok(7);
    }
    return Outcome<int>.err(Error("not a digit I know"));
}

main(): int {
    var values = Array<int>();
    values.append(1);
    values.append(2);
    values.append(4);
    assert(count_matching(values, is_even) == 2, "Predicate<int> is (int) => bool");

    var ages = Lookup<int>();   # HashMap<String, int>
    ages.insert("Ada", 36);
    assert(ages.contains("Ada"), "Lookup<int> is HashMap<String, int>");

    # Outcome<int> fills E with its default: Result<int, Error>.
    assert(parse_digit("7").value() == 7, "ok");
    assert(parse_digit("x").is_err(), "err");
    return 0;
}

The arguments at the use site fill the alias’s parameters in order, and omitted ones take their defaults. Outcome<int> becomes Result<int, Error>, and Lookup<int> becomes HashMap<String, int>. Aliases are especially handy for function types, which are long to write out: Predicate<int> reads better than (int) => bool.

Aliases across files

Like other top-level declarations, an alias is private to the file that declares it. Mark it export to let files that import it use it; see Visibility and Modules and imports.

Recursive aliases

An alias must not refer to itself, directly or through other aliases. The compiler reports the cycle at the alias, with a note at each alias it passes through:

type Celsius = Degrees;
type Degrees = Celsius;

main.pep:1:6: error: type alias 'Celsius' refers to itself
    1 | type Celsius = Degrees;
      |      ^~~~~~~
main.pep:2:6: note: through type alias 'Degrees', declared here
    2 | type Degrees = Celsius;
      |      ^~~~~~~

Traits and extensions

Pepper has no inheritance. Instead, you add behavior to a type with an extension, and describe a set of behavior that several types share with a trait. Extensions can be written anywhere, so you can add methods to a library type, such as String, from your own code.

A trait is also a type: a value of type Shape holds any struct that has the Shape trait, and calls its methods (see Traits as types).

Extensions

An extend block adds methods to a struct declared elsewhere, whether in the same file, another file, or the standard library:

struct Temperature {
    var celsius: float = 0.0;
    construct(this.celsius = 0.0) {}
}

# `extend` adds methods to a struct declared elsewhere.
extend Temperature {
    fahrenheit(): float {
        return celsius * 9.0 / 5.0 + 32.0;
    }

    static freezing(): Temperature {
        return Temperature(0.0);
    }

    bool ==(Temperature other) {
        return celsius == other.celsius;
    }
}

# Library types can be extended too.
extend String {
    exclaim(): String {
        var s = String();
        s.append(this);
        s.append("!");
        return s;
    }
}

# One specialization of a generic library type can be extended, and its
# methods can loop over `this`.
extend Array<int> {
    total(): int {
        var sum = 0;
        for (var x in this) {
            sum += x;
        }
        return sum;
    }
}

struct Box<T> {
    var value: T;
}

# Extending the generic struct adds the method to every Box<...>.
extend Box<T> {
    get(): T {
        return value;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var t = Temperature(100.0);
    assert(t.fahrenheit() == 212.0, "extension method");
    assert(Temperature.freezing() == Temperature(0.0), "static method and operator from an extension");

    assert("hello".exclaim() == "hello!", "String extension");

    var numbers = Array<int>();
    numbers.append(2);
    numbers.append(3);
    assert(numbers.total() == 5, "Array<int> extension loops over this");

    var b = Box<int>();
    b.value = 5;
    assert(b.get() == 5, "Box<int>");
    var s = Box<String>();
    s.value = "five";
    assert(s.get() == "five", "Box<String>");
    return 0;
}
  • An extension can hold anything a struct body can hold except fields, a constructor, and a destructor: methods, static methods, operators, and indexers.
  • Methods in an extension behave exactly like methods declared in the struct. They can use this and name the struct’s fields directly, and they are called the same way.
  • A type can have any number of extensions.
  • An extension method may share its name with the struct’s methods if its parameter types differ: it joins their overload set.
  • An extension in another file is usable only where that file (or its module) is imported, and only if it is exported; the standard library’s extensions are usable everywhere. See Visibility.

Extending generic structs

extend Box<T>, using the struct’s own type parameter name, adds the methods to every specialization: Box<int>, Box<String>, and so on. The example above does this.

extend Box<int> targets one specialization: its methods exist only on Box<int>, and calling one on a Box<String> is an error (no member named 'doubled' in 'Box<String>').

A library type’s specialization can be extended the same way. The example’s extend Array<int> adds total(), which loops over this with for.

An extension cannot add fields; declare every field in the struct itself.

Traits

A trait names a set of methods. Declare one with trait and method signatures ending in ;. Then declare that a type has the trait with extend Type with Trait, and provide the methods in that extension:

# A trait names a set of methods that a type provides.
trait Shape {
    area(): int;
    name(): String;
}

struct Square {
    var side: int = 1;
    construct(this.side = 1) {}
}

# `extend ... with` declares that Square has the Shape trait, and adds the
# trait's methods to it.
extend Square with Shape {
    area(): int {
        return side * side;
    }

    name(): String {
        return "square";
    }
}

# A trait can be generic, and can declare static methods; `this` as a type
# stands for the type that has the trait.
trait From<T> {
    static this from(T value);
}

extend Square with From<int> {
    static Square from(int side) {
        return Square(side);
    }
}

main(): int {
    var sq = Square(3);
    assert(sq.area() == 9, "trait method called on the struct");
    assert(sq.name() == "square", "second trait method");
    assert(Square.from(4).area() == 16, "static method from a generic trait");
    return 0;
}
  • A trait can have type parameters, like From<T>.
  • A trait can declare static methods. In a trait, this used as a type stands for the type that has the trait.
  • extend Square with Shape, Printable { ... } attaches several traits at once.
  • A trait can declare several methods of one name with different parameter types; the type implements each with the method whose parameter types match (see Overloading).
  • The methods in the extension are ordinary methods of the struct, so you call them on a Square as usual.

The compiler checks that the type has every method the trait declares, either in the extension or in the struct itself. A missing method is an error at the extension, with a note at the trait’s method:

main.pep:9:8: error: 'Square' does not implement 'area', required by trait 'Shape'
    9 | extend Square with Shape {
      |        ^~~~~~
main.pep:2:5: note: 'area' declared here
    2 |     area(): int;
      |     ^~~~

Default method bodies

A trait method can have a body. A type that has the trait and does not define that method gets the trait’s body as its own method; a type that defines the method keeps its own. Inside a default body, the trait’s type parameters and this stand for the types named in the with clause:

trait Shape {
    area(): int;
    sides(): int { return 4; }
}

extend Square with Shape {
    area(): int { return side * side; }
}

Here Square(3).sides() returns 4.

A default method can have type parameters of its own (see Generic methods). The runtime’s Iterator<T> uses this for its map<U> and filter adapters, which every type that declares Iterator<T> gets.

A default body sees only what its trait declares: a bare call to one of the trait’s methods calls it on this, and any other bare name means what it means where the trait is declared (a top-level function or variable), even when a conforming type happens to have a method or field of that name. Call through this explicitly only for the trait’s own methods.

Known problem: a default body that uses a string literal does not work yet: it fails to compile (not a struct) or the program crashes. Write such a method in each extension instead.

Traits as types

A trait’s name is also a type. A variable, parameter, field, return value, or collection element of type Shape holds any struct that declares Shape, and calling a method on it runs that struct’s own method:

# A trait used as a type holds any struct that declares the trait.
trait Shape {
    area(): int;
    name(): String;
}

struct Square {
    var side: int = 1;
    construct(this.side = 1) {}
}

extend Square with Shape {
    area(): int { return side * side; }
    name(): String { return "square"; }
}

struct Rect {
    var width: int = 1;
    var height: int = 1;
    construct(this.width = 1, this.height = 1) {}
}

extend Rect with Shape {
    area(): int { return width * height; }
    name(): String { return "rect"; }
}

# One function accepts every Shape; each call runs the value's own method.
measure(shape: Shape): int {
    return shape.area();
}

main(): int {
    # A Square converts to a Shape wherever a Shape is expected.
    var s: Shape = Square(3);
    assert(measure(s) == 9, "square through a trait parameter");
    assert(s.name() == "square", "method of the concrete type");
