    # Collections can hold different types that share a trait.
    var shapes = Array<Shape>();
    shapes.append(Square(2));
    shapes.append(Rect(2, 5));
    var total = 0;
    for (var shape in shapes) {
        total = total + shape.area();
    }
    assert(total == 14, "sum of areas");

    # Two trait values are equal when they refer to the same object.
    var same: Shape = s;
    assert(same == s, "same object");
    return 0;
}

  • Conversion is implicit. A Square becomes a Shape wherever a Shape is expected: an initializer, an assignment, an argument, a return value.

  • Conformance is declared. Only a type that declares the trait with extend Type with Trait converts. Having the right methods is not enough:

    main.pep:11:20: error: 'Circle' does not conform to trait 'Shape'
   11 |     var s: Shape = Circle();
      |                    ^~~~~~
main.pep:6:8: note: declare it with 'extend Circle with Shape'
    6 | struct Circle {
      |        ^~~~~~

  • A trait value is a shared reference to the struct, like any other struct value (see Memory). When the last reference goes away, whether it was a Shape or a Square, the struct’s own destructor runs.

  • Only instance methods are callable through the trait. Static methods, generic methods, and methods whose signature uses this as a type, need the concrete type; calling one through a trait value is an error.

  • Methods must match the trait’s signature. An implementation with different parameter or return types is an error. The one exception is a trait method that returns a trait type: the implementation may return a struct that declares that trait, as iterate() does in Iteration.

  • == and != on trait values compare identity: they are equal when they refer to the same object.

What traits do not do yet:

  • Generic parameters cannot require a trait. There is no syntax yet for saying that T must have Shape; a generic function like total_area<T> accepts any T with an area() method.
  • A trait value cannot convert back to its struct, or to another trait.
  • Atomic<Shape> is not supported. A trait value is two pointers wide and cannot be swapped atomically.
  • Weak<Shape> is not supported. Take the weak reference to the struct type instead.

Iteration

A for loop visits each element of a collection in turn. You can loop over an Array, over the code points of a String, and over any type of your own that follows the iterator protocol.

Looping over an array

for (var x in source) { ... } runs the body once per element, with x bound to the element:

struct Item {
    int value;
    construct(this.value = 0) {}
}

make_items(): Array<Item> {
    var items = Array<Item>();
    items.append(Item(20));
    items.append(Item(22));
    return items;
}

main(): int {
    var nums = Array<int>();
    nums.append(1);
    nums.append(2);
    nums.append(39);

    # The basic loop: x takes each element in order.
    var total = 0;
    for (var x in nums) {
        total = total + x;
    }
    assert(total == 42, "sum of the elements");

    # The loop variable may be typed, and the parentheses are optional.
    long wide = 0;
    for (long x in nums) {
        wide = wide + x;
    }
    for var x: int in nums {
        wide = wide - x;
    }
    assert(wide == 0, "typed loop variables");

    # break and continue work as in a while loop.
    var odd = 0;
    for (var x in nums) {
        if (x % 2 == 0) {
            continue;
        }
        if (x > 10) {
            break;
        }
        odd = odd + x;
    }
    assert(odd == 1, "only 1 is odd and below 10");

    # A struct element is shared: changing a field changes the element,
    # but rebinding the loop variable does not touch the array.
    var items = make_items();
    for (var item in items) {
        item.value = item.value * 10;
        item = Item(99);
    }
    assert(items[0].value == 200, "first element was updated");
    assert(items[1].value == 220, "second element was updated");

    # Iterating a temporary keeps it alive for the whole loop.
    var sum = 0;
    for (var item in make_items()) {
        sum = sum + item.value;
    }
    assert(sum == 42, "temporary array stays alive");

    # The loop is shorthand for driving the iterator yourself.
    var it = nums.iterate();
    var manual = 0;
    while (it.next()) {
        manual = manual + it.current();
    }
    assert(manual == 42, "the same elements, by hand");

    # Modifying the array after the loop is fine.
    nums.append(100);
    assert(nums.count() == 4, "appended after the loop");
    return 0;
}

The example shows each part of the loop:

  • The loop variable can be declared with var or with a type: for (var x in nums), for (long x in nums), and for (var x: int in nums) all work.
  • The parentheses are optional, as with if and while: for var x in nums { ... }. The braces are required.
  • break and continue work as they do in a while loop.
  • The loop variable is a copy, like any var. Assigning a new value to it does not change the collection. For a struct element, though, the copy is another reference to the same object (see Memory and reference counting), so item.value = ... does change the element.
  • A temporary source stays alive. The loop holds a reference to what it iterates, so for (var item in make_items()) is safe.

Looping over a string

A String yields its code points one at a time. Each one is a String holding a single code point, the same value that indexing gives you (word[i]):

main(): int {
    var word = "héllo ✓";

    # A String yields one code point at a time, as a one-code-point String.
    var count = 0;
    var copy = "";
    for (var c in word) {
        assert(c.length() == 1, "each piece is one code point");
        copy.append(c);
        count = count + 1;
    }
    assert(count == 7, "7 code points");
    assert(copy == word, "the pieces rebuild the string");

    # Each piece is the same value as indexing the string.
    var i = 0;
    for (var c in word) {
        assert(c == word[i], "same as word[i]");
        i = i + 1;
    }

    # An empty string runs the body zero times.
    for (var c in "") {
        assert(false, "never runs");
    }
    return 0;
}

The iterator protocol

The for loop is not tied to Array and String. It works with any type that has the right methods, described by two traits in the runtime (runtime/iterator.pep, documented in the Iterators reference):

export trait Iterator<T> {
    bool next();     # advance; true if there is a current element
    T current();     # the element next() just moved to
}

export trait Iterable<T> {
    iterate(): Iterator<T>;
}
  • An iterator steps through elements. next() moves to the next element and returns true, or returns false when there are no more (and keeps returning false after that). current() returns the element next() moved to.
  • An iterable is a collection that hands out a fresh iterator from iterate(). Array<T> returns an ArrayIterator<T> and String returns a StringIterator.

for (var x in source) { body } behaves exactly like this:

{
    var it = source.iterate();   # or `var it = source;` when source is itself an iterator
    while (it.next()) {
        var x = it.current();
        { body }
    }
}

The loop chooses by the methods the source’s type has, like a C++ range-based for: it uses iterate() if the type has one, and otherwise uses the source itself as the iterator if it has both next() and current(). A type with neither is a compile error:

main.pep:4:19: error: type 'Foo' is not iterable: implement Iterable<T> (iterate()) or Iterator<T> (next() and current())
    4 |     for (var x in f) {
      |                   ^

HashMap and HashSet do not implement the protocol yet, so they cannot be used in a for loop.

Adapters

Iterator<T> has two default methods that wrap an iterator in another one. Each one works lazily, one element at a time as the loop asks for it:

  • map(transform) yields transform(x) for each element x; its element type is whatever transform returns.
  • filter(keep) yields only the elements for which keep(x) is true.

Both return iterators, so they chain, and a for loop can use the result directly:

struct Person {
    String name;
    int age;
    construct(this.name = "", this.age = 0) {}
}

main(): int {
    var people = Array<Person>();
    people.append(Person("Ada", 36));
    people.append(Person("Bo", 12));
    people.append(Person("Cyd", 40));

    # filter keeps the adults, map turns each one into a name.
    var names = Array<String>();
    for (var name in people.iterate().filter((p) => p.age >= 18).map((p) => p.name)) {
        names.append(name);
    }
    assert(names.count() == 2, "two adults");
    assert(names[0] == "Ada", "Ada first");
    assert(names[1] == "Cyd", "then Cyd");

    # Adapters work on any Iterator, including a String's.
    var kept = 0;
    for (var c in "hello".iterate().filter((c) => c != "l")) {
        kept += 1;
    }
    assert(kept == 3, "h, e, o");
    return 0;
}

Every type that declares Iterator<T> gets them: ArrayIterator<T> (from Array.iterate()), StringIterator, and your own iterators once you write extend YourType with Iterator<T> {}. The adapter holds a reference to the iterator it wraps, so a chain built from a temporary stays alive while the loop runs. They are generic methods (see Generics), so they are not callable through an Iterator<T> trait value.

Writing your own iterator

To make your own type work in a for loop, give it next() and current() (an iterator), or give it an iterate() method that returns such a type (an iterable):

# An iterator: next() advances, current() returns the element.
struct Countdown {
    int remaining;
    int now;

    construct(this.remaining = 0) {}

    bool next() {
        if (remaining == 0) {
            return false;
        }
        now = remaining;
        remaining = remaining - 1;
        return true;
    }

    int current() {
        return now;
    }
}

# Declaring the trait documents the intent; the loop goes by the methods.
extend Countdown with Iterator<int> {}

# An iterable: iterate() hands out a fresh iterator for each loop.
struct Evens {
    int limit;

    construct(this.limit = 0) {}

    EvensIterator iterate() {
        var it = EvensIterator();
        it.limit = limit;
        it.now = -2;
        return it;
    }
}

struct EvensIterator {
    int limit;
    int now;

    bool next() {
        now = now + 2;
        return now < limit;
    }

    int current() {
        return now;
    }
}

extend EvensIterator with Iterator<int> {}
extend Evens with Iterable<int> {}

# A generic function can loop over any type that supports the protocol.
sum<C>(C values): int {
    var total = 0;
    for (var v in values) {
        total = total + v;
    }
    return total;
}

main(): int {
    var total = 0;
    for (var n in Countdown(3)) {
        total = total + n;
    }
    assert(total == 6, "3 + 2 + 1");

    # A loop over an iterator in a variable uses that object up.
    var c = Countdown(2);
    for (var n in c) {
        total = total + n;
    }
    assert(!c.next(), "the countdown is exhausted");

    # An iterable starts a new pass every time.
    var evens = Evens(10);
    assert(sum<Evens>(evens) == 20, "0 + 2 + 4 + 6 + 8");
    assert(sum<Evens>(evens) == 20, "a second pass sees the same elements");

    var nums = Array<int>();
    nums.append(30);
    nums.append(12);
    assert(sum<Array<int>>(nums) == 42, "arrays work too");
    return 0;
}

Some things to keep in mind:

  • Declaring the trait is optional. The loop looks only at which methods a type has. Writing extend Countdown with Iterator<int> {} documents your intent, and the compiler then checks that Countdown has both next() and current() (see Traits and extensions). Declaring it also lets the type be used as an Iterator<int> value, and a for loop over such a value calls the methods through the trait. It also gives the type the adapters map and filter.
  • Looping over an iterator uses it up. When the source is itself an iterator stored in a variable, the loop drives that same object, so it is exhausted afterwards. An iterable starts a new, independent pass every time you loop over it.
  • Generic code can loop over a type parameter; each specialization checks the protocol for its own type (see Generics).
  • Calling current() out of turn panics in the runtime’s iterators: before the first next() you get ArrayIterator.current() before next(), and after next() returned false you get ArrayIterator.current() past the end. Your own iterators should follow the same rule.

Modifying a collection while you loop over it

Changing an Array or a String while a loop is iterating over it stops the program with a panic, because the iterator could otherwise skip or repeat elements, or read past the end:

var nums = Array<int>();
nums.append(1);
nums.append(2);
for (var x in nums) {
    nums.append(x);   # panics: Array modified during iteration
}

Every method that changes an Array counts, including storing an element with nums[i] = v. For a String, append and grow count, and the message is String modified during iteration. The check happens on the iterator’s next step, so modifying the collection after the loop is finished is fine.

To change elements as you go, loop over an index with while and store through it, or collect the changes in a second array and apply them after the loop.

Loop scope

The loop variable and anything declared in the loop body exist only inside the loop. Each pass gets a new loop variable, and struct values declared in the body are released at the end of every pass, whether the pass ends normally or by continue, break, or return. Leaving the loop early also releases the hidden iterator, so a destructor on a custom iterator runs at that point.

Using the loop variable or a body local after the loop is an error, the same as for any name that is out of scope:

main.pep:6:12: error: use of undeclared identifier 'i'
    6 |     return i;
      |            ^

The loop variable, or a local in the loop body, may have the same name as a variable declared before the loop. It hides the outer variable inside the loop, and the outer variable keeps its value afterwards.

Scoped blocks with with

A with block enters a scope object, binds what it hands out, and cleans up when the object is destroyed. It plays the role of Python’s with and C#’s using, but the cleanup is ordinary destruction: it runs through the same reference counting as every other object (see Memory and reference counting).

# A module-scope counter: const, and changed through an Atomic.
const open_sessions = Atomic<int>(0);

# A scope object: enter() hands out what the block works with, and the
# destructor calls exit() when the last reference goes away.
struct Session {
    var user: String;

    construct(this.user = "") {
        open_sessions.fetch_add(1);
    }

    destruct {
        this.exit();
    }

    Session enter() {
        return this;
    }

    exit() {
        open_sessions.fetch_sub(1);
    }
}

extend Session with Scoped<Session> {}

# enter() may return a null reference: then the body is skipped and the
# `else` block runs.
struct Lookup {
    var found: Session;

    construct(Session session) {
        found = session;
    }

    Session enter() {
        return found;
    }

    exit() {}
}

extend Lookup with Scoped<Session> {}

first_user(): String {
    with (var session = Session("ada")) {
        # `return` leaves the block too: the session still closes.
        return session.user;
    }
    return "";
}

main(): int {
    with (var session = Session("grace")) {
        assert(open_sessions.load() == 1, "open inside the block");
        assert(session.user == "grace", "bound to what enter() returned");
    }
    assert(open_sessions.load() == 0, "closed at the end of the block");

    assert(first_user() == "ada", "the value was returned");
    assert(open_sessions.load() == 0, "closed on return as well");

    var hits = 0;
    with (var s = Lookup(Session("linus"))) {
        hits += 1;
    } else {
        hits -= 100;
    }
    with (var s = Lookup(__null)) {
        hits -= 100;
    } else {
        hits += 1;
    }
    assert(hits == 2, "the body ran for a session, the else block for null");
    return 0;
}

The header

with (var name = scope) { ... } else { ... }
with (Type name = scope) { ... }
with (scope) { ... }
  • name is bound to scope.enter(), not to scope itself. A Session can hand out itself; a Weak<T> hands out a strong reference to the object it points at, or null once that object is gone.
  • Without a binding, the block still holds the result of enter() until it ends. An enter() that returns nothing (declared without a return type) has nothing to bind, and binding it is an error.
  • The parentheses are optional, as with if, while and for. The braces are required.
  • name cannot be reassigned inside the block.

Skipping the body

When enter() returns a struct reference that is null, the body is skipped and the optional else block runs instead. else is only allowed when enter() returns a struct type, since nothing else can be null.

The Scoped<T> protocol

A type works in a with block when it has the methods of the Scoped<T> trait (runtime/scoped.pep, documented in the Scoped reference):

export trait Scoped<T> {
    T enter();   # what the header binds; null skips the body
    exit();      # the cleanup; call it from the destructor
}

A with block goes by the methods a type has, not by the trait: a type without enter() is a compile error in a with header. Declaring the trait (extend Session with Scoped<Session> {}) documents the intent, and the compiler then checks that the type has both enter() and exit().

When exit() runs

The block keeps two locals: the scope object and the bound value. Both are released on every path out of the block (the end of the body, return, break and continue), the bound value first. Implementations call exit() from their destructor:

destruct {
    this.exit();
}

so exit() runs when the scope object’s last reference is released:

  • A scope object created in the header (with (var s = Session("grace"))) is released by the block, so exit() runs at the end of the block.
  • A scope object that something else also holds (a field, another local) lives on after the block, and exit() waits for its last owner. This is the same rule as any other object.
  • A copy of the bound value that outlives the block (stored in a field, returned) keeps that value alive, but not the scope object.

A panic stops the program without running destructors, so exit() does not run then.

Error handling with Result

An operation that can fail returns a Result: either Ok, holding the value you asked for, or Err, holding an error that says what went wrong. The caller checks which one it got before using the value. Pepper has no exceptions, so Result is how a function reports a failure the caller is expected to handle. For a failure that should stop the program, such as a broken invariant, use assert or panic instead.

Result and Error are part of the runtime, so you can use them without an import. The Result reference lists every member.

Creating and checking results

Result<TValue, TError> works like Rust’s Result<T, E>. You build one with the static functions ok and err, and ask which it is with is_ok() and is_err():

# A function that can fail returns a Result instead of a plain value.
parse_digit(String s): Result<int> {
    if (s == "0") { return Result<int>.ok(0); }
    if (s == "1") { return Result<int>.ok(1); }
    if (s == "2") { return Result<int>.ok(2); }
    return Result<int>.err(Error("not a digit: " + s));
}

main(): int {
    var good = parse_digit("2");
    assert(good.is_ok(), "2 parses");
    assert(!good.is_err(), "is_err is the opposite of is_ok");
    assert(good.value() == 2, "value() returns the value");
    assert(good.value() == 2, "the Value field holds it too");

    var bad = parse_digit("x");
    assert(bad.is_err(), "x does not parse");
    assert(bad.error().message == "not a digit: x", "the error explains why");

    # ok_or returns the value, or the fallback for an Err. It never panics.
    assert(good.ok_or(-1) == 2, "Ok gives its value");
    assert(bad.ok_or(-1) == -1, "Err gives the fallback");

    # Result<int> is short for Result<int, Error>.
    Result<int, Error> spelled_out = good;
    assert(spelled_out.value() == 2, "the same type");

    # A default-constructed Result is an Err with a default error.
    var empty = Result<int>();
    assert(empty.is_err(), "Result() is an Err");

    # An operation with no value to return uses void.
    var done = Result<void>.ok();
    var failed = Result<void>.err(Error("disk full"));
    assert(done.is_ok() && failed.is_err(), "Result<void> works like any other");
    return 0;
}

The pieces:

MemberWhat it does
Result<T>.ok(value)Creates an Ok result holding value.
Result<T>.err(error)Creates an Err result holding error.
is_ok(), is_err()Report which kind the result is.
value()Returns the value; panics with Result.value on an Err value if the result is an Err.
error()Returns the error; panics with Result.error on an Ok value if the result is Ok.
ok_or(fallback)Returns the value, or fallback if the result is an Err. Never panics.

TError defaults to Error, so Result<int> means Result<int, Error>, and values of the two spellings can be assigned to each other freely (the compiler does generate a separate specialization for each spelling). Error is a struct with one field, String message: Error("boom").message is "boom".

Use value() when an Err would be a bug in your program, since it stops the program. When an Err is an expected outcome, test with is_ok() or is_err(), or supply a fallback with ok_or.

Check before you read

Pepper has no union types, so a Result stores both a value and an error in private fields, and only one of them is meaningful. value() and error() check which one you asked for and panic on the wrong one, so call value() only after checking is_ok(), and error() only after checking is_err().

Results with no value

An operation that has nothing to return on success, such as deleting a file, uses void as its value type, like Result<(), E> in Rust. A void type argument erases what it types, so Result<void> holds no value, ok() takes no argument, and value() returns nothing: calling it only checks that the result is Ok. Using that call as a value, as in var x = done.value();, is a compile error, as it is for any function that returns void.

var done = Result<void>.ok();
var failed = Result<void>.err(Error("disk full"));
done.value();   # panics if `done` is an Err

Custom error types and propagation

Any type can be the error type. A struct lets you carry details the caller can act on, not just a message. To pass an error up to your own caller, check the result and return early with its error; to change the error’s type on the way, build a new error from the old one:

# A custom error type: any type can be the second type argument.
struct ParseError {
    String input;
    int position;

    construct(this.input = "", this.position = 0) {}
}

digit_value(String c): int {
    var digits = "0123456789";
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 10) {
        if (c == digits[i]) {
            return i;
        }
        i = i + 1;
    }
    return -1;
}

parse_number(String s): Result<int, ParseError> {
    if (s.length() == 0) {
        return Result<int, ParseError>.err(ParseError(s, 0));
    }
    var value = 0;
    var position = 0;
    for (var c in s) {
        var d = digit_value(c);
        if (d < 0) {
            return Result<int, ParseError>.err(ParseError(s, position));
        }
        value = value * 10 + d;
        position = position + 1;
    }
    return Result<int, ParseError>.ok(value);
}

# Propagate an error: check it, and return early with the same error.
add_numbers(String a, String b): Result<int, ParseError> {
    var x = parse_number(a);
    if (x.is_err()) {
        return Result<int, ParseError>.err(x.error());
    }
    var y = parse_number(b);
    if (y.is_err()) {
        return Result<int, ParseError>.err(y.error());
    }
    return Result<int, ParseError>.ok(x.value() + y.value());
}

# Convert an error into another type on the way out.
check_total(String a, String b, int limit): Result<void> {
    var sum = add_numbers(a, b);
    if (sum.is_err()) {
        var e = sum.error();
        return Result<void>.err(Error("bad number '" + e.input + "'"));
    }
    if (sum.value() > limit) {
        return Result<void>.err(Error("total is over the limit"));
    }
    return Result<void>.ok();
}

main(): int {
    var sum = add_numbers("40", "2");
    assert(sum.value() == 42, "40 + 2");

    var bad = add_numbers("40", "4x2");
    assert(bad.is_err(), "the second number is bad");
    assert(bad.error().input == "4x2", "the error names the input");
    assert(bad.error().position == 1, "and where it went wrong");

    assert(check_total("1", "2", 10).is_ok(), "3 is within the limit");
    var over = check_total("10", "20", 10);
    assert(over.error().message == "total is over the limit", "30 is not");
    var wrong = check_total("1", "", 10);
    assert(wrong.error().message == "bad number ''", "an empty number is an error");
    return 0;
}

Pepper does not yet have a shorthand for propagation (such as Rust’s ? operator), and match does not work on a Result yet, so each fallible call is checked with if as shown above.

Results from the io module

The io module is a good model of a library built on Result. Every operation that can fail returns an IoResult<T>, a type alias for Result<T, IoError>, and operations with nothing to return use IoResult<void>. IoError holds the platform’s error code and a message, and has predicates such as is_not_found() and is_permission_denied() for deciding what to do about the failure:

import [File, Directory, Path, IoResult, IoError] from io;

# IoResult<T> is Result<T, IoError>: the same methods, a richer error.
read_setting(Path path): String {
    var text = File.read_to_string(path);
    if (text.is_err()) {
        if (text.error().is_not_found()) {
            return "default";
        }
        return "error: " + text.error().message;
    }
    return text.value();
}

main(): int {
    var dir = Directory.temp().join("pepper-results-io-example");
    var created = Directory.create_all(dir);
    assert(created.is_ok(), "created the folder");

    var path = dir.join("setting.txt");
    assert(read_setting(path) == "default", "a missing file gives the default");

    IoResult<void> written = File.write_text(path, "blue");
    assert(written.is_ok(), "wrote the file");
    assert(read_setting(path) == "blue", "the file's contents");

    assert(Directory.remove_all(dir).is_ok(), "cleaned up");
    return 0;
}

See io: errors and platform types for the full list of IoError predicates.

Optional values

A value that may be missing has the type Optional<T>, written T? for short: either Some, holding a T, or None, holding nothing. It plays the role of Rust’s Option<T> and Swift’s Optional. Use it instead of a null reference or a sentinel such as -1 or "": the type tells the caller that the value can be absent, and reading it goes through a method that checks.

Optional is part of the runtime, so you can use it without an import. The Optional reference lists every member.

# A lookup that may find nothing returns an Optional instead of a sentinel.
struct Person {
    String name;
    int age;

    construct(this.name = "", this.age = 0) {}
}

find_adult(people: Array<Person>): Optional<Person> {
    for (var p in people) {
        if (p.age >= 18) { return Optional<Person>.some(p); }
    }
    return Optional<Person>.none();
}

# `ulong?` is shorthand for `Optional<ulong>`.
index_of(text: String, wanted: String): ulong? {
    var i: ulong = 0;
    while (i < text.length()) {
        if (text[i] == wanted) { return Optional<ulong>.some(i); }
        i = i + 1;
    }
    return Optional<ulong>.none();
}

main(): int {
    var people = Array<Person>();
    people.append(Person("ada", 12));
    assert(find_adult(people).is_none(), "no adult yet");

    people.append(Person("grace", 45));
    var adult = find_adult(people);
    assert(adult.is_some(), "grace is an adult");
    assert(adult.value().name == "grace", "value() returns the Person");

    # Works for types that have no null, too.
    assert(index_of("pepper", "e").value() == 1, "first 'e' is at 1");
    assert(index_of("pepper", "z").is_none(), "no 'z'");

    # value_or supplies a fallback instead of panicking.
    assert(index_of("pepper", "z").value_or(99) == 99, "fallback for None");

    # Optional<T>() is None, like Optional<T>.none().
    var nothing = Optional<int>();
    assert(nothing.is_none(), "Optional() is None");

    # An optional slot that is not given a value starts as None.
    var later: String?;
    assert(later.is_none(), "unassigned optionals are None");
    return 0;
}

The T? shorthand

T? means Optional<T> wherever a type is written: x: int?, find(key: String): Node?, Array<Node?>, a field Token? leaf;. The ? applies to everything before it, so int[]? is an optional array, and Array<int?> is an array of optional values.

A C-style declaration inside a function body, such as Node? n = m;, is read as a declaration only when a name follows the ? and then =, ; or the end of the line; a ? b : c stays a conditional expression.

Optional slots start as None

A variable or field of an optional type that is not given a value starts as None, not null:

struct Node {
    Token? leaf;   # None until assigned
}
var count: int?;   # None

Creating and checking optionals

MemberWhat it does
Optional<T>.some(value)Creates a Some optional holding value.
Optional<T>.none(), Optional<T>()Create a None optional.
is_some(), is_none()Report which kind the optional is.
value()Returns the value; panics with Optional.value on a None value if the optional is None.
value_or(fallback)Returns the value, or fallback if the optional is None. Never panics.

Use value() when a None would be a bug in your program, since it stops the program. When a missing value is an expected outcome, test with is_some() or is_none() first, or supply a fallback with value_or.

Optional<T> works for every T, including types that have no null at all, such as int or ulong. For a struct type, a Some optional holds a reference to the object, like any other struct slot (see Memory and reference counting).

Optional or Result

An Optional<T> says only that a value is missing. When the caller needs to know why, return a Result instead, whose Err carries an error.

Current limits

  • An optional is checked with is_some() or is_none() and read with value() or value_or; match does not work on it yet. Optional will become an enum with None and Some(value) cases once enum cases can carry values; its methods stay the same.
  • The runtime’s own APIs still use null references or default values for “missing” (a HashMap returns the default value for a missing key, for example).

Visibility

Visibility decides which declarations other files may use. Every top-level declaration is private to the file that declares it unless you mark it with export. This lets a file keep its helpers to itself and offer a small, clear surface to the files that import it.

Exporting declarations

Put export in front of a declaration to make it usable from other files that import this one (see Modules and imports for how to import). You can export every kind of top-level declaration:

  • functions, including extern declarations of C functions
  • structs
  • type aliases
  • traits
  • extensions
  • module-scope variables, which are always const and thread-safe (see Module-scope variables)

Here is a library file with a mix of exported and private declarations, and a program that uses it:

# Everything here is private to lib.pep unless it says `export`.

export struct Point {
    var x: int;
    var y: int;
}

export type Points = Point[];

export trait Describe {
    describe(): String;
}

# A module-scope variable is `const` and thread-safe: change it through
# an Atomic.
export const points_made = Atomic<int>(0);

export origin(): Point {
    return make(0, 0);
}

export at(x: int, y: int): Point {
    return make(x, y);
}

# Private: only lib.pep can call make().
make(x: int, y: int): Point {
    var p = Point();
    p.x = x;
    p.y = y;
    points_made.fetch_add(1);
    return p;
}

# An exported extension: its methods are usable wherever Point is.
export extend Point with Describe {
    describe(): String {
        return "point";
    }
}

# A private extension: only lib.pep can call p.norm().
extend Point {
    norm(): int {
        return x * x + y * y;
    }
}

export norm_of(p: Point): int {
    return p.norm();
}

# `export` goes after attributes, directly before the declaration.
[Target(OS(Windows))] export platform_name(): String { return "Windows"; }
[Target(!OS(Windows))] export platform_name(): String { return "not Windows"; }

import "lib";

main(): int {
    var p = at(3, 4);             # exported function
    var o: Point = origin();      # exported struct, used as a type
    Points list = Points();       # exported type alias
    list.append(p);
    list.append(o);

    assert(p.x == 3, "fields are as visible as their struct");
    assert(p.describe() == "point", "method from an exported extension");
    assert(norm_of(p) == 25, "an exported function may use private helpers");
    assert(points_made.load() == 2, "exported module variable");
    assert(platform_name().length() > 0, "exported, with an attribute");
    return 0;
}

main.pep can call at, origin, and norm_of, use Point and Points as types, and read the counter in points_made, an exported const Atomic<int>. It cannot use make or call p.norm(). Trying to is an error that tells you how to fix it:

import "lib";

main(): int {
    var q = make(1, 2);
    return 0;
}

main.pep:4:13: error: 'make' is private to lib.pep; declare it with 'export' to use it from another file
    4 |     var q = make(1, 2);
      |             ^~~~
lib.pep:27:1: note: declared here
   27 | make(x: int, y: int): Point {
      | ^~~~

The same applies to private structs, type aliases, traits, and variables: naming one from another file reports 'name' is private to file.pep.

Members of structs and extensions

Fields and methods declared inside a struct’s body are reached through the struct, so they share the struct’s visibility: if you can use the struct, you can use all of its members.

Methods added by an extension share the extension’s visibility instead. In the example, norm() is added by a private extension, so only lib.pep can call it, even though Point itself is exported:

main.pep:4:14: error: 'norm' is declared in a private extension in lib.pep; declare the extension with 'export' to use it from another file
    4 |     return p.norm();
      |              ^~~~
lib.pep:42:5: note: declared here
   42 |     norm(): int {
      |     ^~~~

An exported extension’s methods are usable where its file is imported, not everywhere: a file sees the extensions it declares itself, the exported extensions of the files it imports, the exported extensions of every file of a module it imports (import geo reaches geo/ext.pep’s), and those of the runtime, which every file sees. Here measure.pep adds manhattan() to Point, and main.pep imports it to call it:

import "point";

# An exported extension: files that import measure.pep can call these.
export extend Point {
    manhattan(): int {
        return abs(x) + abs(y);
    }
}

# Private to this file.
abs(v: int): int {
    if (v < 0) { return -v; }
    return v;
}

import "point";
import "measure";                      # brings Point's manhattan() into scope

main(): int {
    var p = Point();
    p.x = 3;
    p.y = -4;
    assert(p.manhattan() == 7, "an extension from an imported file");
    return 0;
}

Without import "measure";, the call is an error. If no file of the program imports measure.pep, the method does not exist at all (no member named 'manhattan' in 'Point'); if another file does, the error says which import is missing:

main.pep:8:14: error: 'manhattan' is declared in an extension in measure.pep, which this file does not import; add an import for it
    8 |     assert(p.manhattan() == 7, "an extension from an imported file");
      |              ^~~~~~~~~
measure.pep:5:5: note: declared here
    5 |     manhattan(): int {
      |     ^~~~~~~~~

Two kinds of call are checked where they take effect rather than here:

  • A method of a trait you can see (s.area() where area is a method of a trait Shape you declare, import, or get from the standard library): the call may go through a value of that trait, which works whatever file declares the conformance. A file can return a Shape whose struct and conformance stay private, and callers still call area on it.
  • A call on a receiver whose type is not known in this file: this.m() in a trait’s default method, or x.m() (or x.f().m()) where x is a parameter typed by a type parameter, the function’s or its struct’s (measure<X>(x: X)). Such a call resolves where the generic is used, with that file’s imports.

When the extension is in a file of another module, the error names the module to import instead (import its module 'geo'), since a file of another module is reached only through its module.

Inside a struct’s methods (and a trait’s default methods), a bare call to a name that an extension you cannot see adds as a method is an error too, rather than a call to the top-level function of that name: the compiler would otherwise bind it to the method. Rename one of them, or import the extension’s file if the method is what you meant. That import may point back at a file that imports this one (a struct’s file importing the file that extends it); such cycles are allowed.

Extending a struct does not make a private struct visible to other files.

Visibility is checked before types are known, so members are matched by name: a call x.m() is accepted if any struct body visible to the file (its own, or exported) or any extension visible to it, as above, declares a member named m.

Where export goes

export goes directly before the declaration’s keyword or name, after any attributes:

[Target(OS(Windows))] export platform_name(): String { return "Windows"; }

Writing it before the attributes is a parse error:

main.pep:2:8: error: unexpected '['
    2 | export [Target(OS(Windows))] f(): int { return 1; }
      |        ^

export controls visibility between Pepper files only. Making a function callable from C with C linkage is a separate matter, handled by the [Export] attribute described in Calling C.

The runtime’s exports

The runtime’s public surface (String, Array, HashMap, HashSet, Atomic, Result, Error, hash, assert, panic, and the rest of the standard library reference) is exported the same way, and is visible in every file without an import. The runtime’s own helpers are private, so calling one, such as the integer formatter dec, reports 'dec' is private to followed by the path of the runtime’s internals.pep.

Each file has its own namespace

A private declaration belongs to its file alone, so two files may each declare a private name without clashing. Here main.pep and lib.pep each have their own Counter and make, and each file’s code reaches its own:

# lib.pep's private helpers: no other file can see them, and no other
# file's names can clash with them.
struct Counter { var n: int; }

make(): Counter {
    var c = Counter();
    c.n = 10;
    return c;
}

export lib_total(): int {
    return make().n;
}

import "lib";

# main.pep has its own private Counter and make(), unrelated to lib.pep's.
struct Counter { var n: int; var step: int; }

make(): Counter {
    var c = Counter();
    c.n = 1;
    c.step = 2;
    return c;
}

main(): int {
    var c = make();                    # main.pep's make()
    assert(c.n + c.step == 3, "main's own Counter");
    assert(lib_total() == 10, "lib's make() is its own");
    return 0;
}

This holds across modules too: two modules may each have a util.pep with a private helper. Files are told apart by their path inside their module (io/file.pep), not by their file name alone.

Exported names are different: they share one namespace across the whole program, because importers reach them by name. Two files may not export the same name, even from modules reached through different qualifiers: exported 'f' is also declared in b/module.pep. A private name may repeat an exported one from another file; in the file that declares it, the file’s own declaration is the one that name refers to.

A few kinds of declaration are not private in this sense:

  • extern declarations name a C function, which is the same function in every file. Several files may declare the same extern.
  • Concrete overrides of a generic function, such as hash<int> alongside hash<T>, take the generic function’s name: they replace its version for those types wherever it is called. So each override may be declared in only one file (override 'hash<int>' is also declared in b.pep), and overriding one the runtime already provides, such as hash<String>, is also an error. An override must be for a generic function you can see: overriding another file’s private generic function reports that it is private. An override may be for a struct, such as hash<Point> (see Generics).
  • Declarations that a [Target(...)] attribute drops for the platform being compiled (see Platform selection) are never compiled. This is why the first lib.pep above can declare platform_name twice.

Modules and imports

As a program grows, you split it into files, and group related files into modules. An import makes another file’s or module’s exported declarations available in the importing file. What a file exports is covered in Visibility.

Importing a file

The simplest import names another file, as a string, relative to the importing file. The .pep extension is optional:

# Every import comes before the file's declarations.
import "util";                       # util.pep's exports, unqualified
import "util" as u;                  # the same exports, as u.name
import [thrice as triple] from "util";
import "helpers/text";               # a file in a subfolder

main(): int {
    assert(twice(base) == 20, "unqualified names from util.pep");
    assert(u.thrice(2) == 6, "qualified by the alias");
    assert(triple(3) == 9, "renamed by the import list");
    assert(shout("hi") == "hi!", "from helpers/text.pep");

    # An imported function is a value like any other.
    var f = u.twice;
    assert(f(4) == 8, "function reference through a qualifier");
    return 0;
}

util.pep sits next to main.pep, and text.pep is in a helpers folder:

export const base = 10;

export twice(n: int): int {
    return n * 2;
}

export thrice(n: int): int {
    return n * 3;
}

export shout(s: String): String {
    return s + "!";
}

An import comes in one of these forms:

FormWhat it makes available
import "util";Every export of util.pep, unqualified: twice(base).
import "util" as u;Every export, qualified by the name you choose: u.twice(1).
import [thrice as triple, twice] from "util";Only the listed exports, unqualified, each under its own name or the one after as.
import geometry;A module’s exports, qualified by the module’s name: geometry.Point.
import geometry as g;A module’s exports, qualified by g.
import [area, Point as Pt] from geometry;The listed exports of a module, unqualified.

The semicolon after an import is optional.

An import binds names only in the file that contains it. The exports of a file that this file did not import are not visible to it, even when another file of the program imports them. Here geometry/module.pep imports shapes.pep, but main.pep imports only the module:

import geometry;
main(): int { return area(1, 2); }

main.pep:2:22: error: 'area' is exported by deps/geometry/shapes.pep, which this file does not import; add an import for it
    2 | main(): int { return area(1, 2); }
      |                      ^~~~
deps/geometry/shapes.pep:3:8: note: declared here
    3 | export area(w: int, h: int): int {
      |        ^~~~

Imports come first

Every import, and every export ... from, comes before all of a file’s declarations and export name re-exports:

main(): int { return 0; }
import geometry;

main.pep:2:1: error: imports must come before all declarations
    2 | import geometry;
      | ^~~~~~

Modules

A module is a folder that contains a module.pep file. The module is named after its folder, and its exports are exactly what its module.pep exports. The other files in the folder are the module’s own files: module.pep imports them and chooses what to pass on to the module’s users.

This example has a geometry module next to main.pep:

modules-folders/
    main.pep
    geometry/
        module.pep      # the module's interface
        shapes.pep      # a file of the module
        solids/
            module.pep  # a module nested inside geometry

# A file of the geometry module. Its exports are visible to the files of
# the module that import it, not to the module's importers.
export area(w: int, h: int): int {
    return w * h;
}

export struct Box<T> {
    var value: T;
}

# A module nested in the geometry folder.
export volume(w: int, h: int, d: int): int {
    return w * h * d;
}

# The geometry module: what this file exports is the module's interface.
import "shapes";
import "solids" as solids;

export struct Point {
    var x: int;
    var y: int;
}

export make(x: int, y: int): Point {
    var p = Point();
    p.x = x;
    p.y = y;
    return p;
}

reset_count(): int {
    return 0;
}

export area as rect_area;           # shapes.pep's area, renamed
export Box;                         # shapes.pep's Box, under its own name
export reset_count as reset;        # a private function, exported as reset
export solids.volume as box_volume; # from the nested module

import "geometry" as g;
import [rect_area, Point as Pt] from "geometry";

# Qualified names work in types, too.
corner(): g.Point {
    return g.make(1, 1);
}

main(): int {
    var p: g.Point = g.make(3, 4);
    assert(p.x == 3, "qualified function and type");

    Pt q = corner();
    assert(q.y == 1, "the struct under its import-list name");

    assert(g.rect_area(2, 3) == 6, "re-exported under a new name");
    assert(rect_area(2, 5) == 10, "imported unqualified");
    assert(g.reset() == 0, "a private function, re-exported");
    assert(g.box_volume(2, 3, 4) == 24, "re-exported from a nested module");

    var b = g.Box<int>();
    b.value = 7;
    var boxes: g.Box<int>[] = g.Box<int>[]();
    boxes.append(b);
    assert(boxes[0].value == 7, "a generic struct through a qualifier");
    return 0;
}

main.pep imports the folder with a string, import "geometry", because the module lives next to it. A string import names either a file (geometry.pep) or a module folder (geometry/) relative to the importing file. A module imports a module nested in its own folder the same way: geometry/module.pep imports "solids".

Re-exports

export name as new_name; exports name to importers under new_name, and export name; exports it under its own name. The name may be declared in the file (even privately) or imported from another file or module, qualified or not. This is how a module.pep publishes what its other files define.

The semicolon is required. Without it, export Box followed by a declaration would read as an exported C-style declaration whose type is Box.

The exports of the module’s other files are not the module’s exports. In the example, users of geometry can call g.rect_area, but not g.area:

main.pep:2:22: error: 'area' is not exported by 'geometry' (deps/geometry/module.pep)
    2 | main(): int { return geometry.area(1, 2); }
      |                      ^~~~~~~~~~~~~

Re-exporting a whole file or module

A module.pep that only passes on its files’ exports would repeat every name. export ... from passes them on in one line, in the same shapes as the import ... from forms:

FormWhat importers get
export * from "rect";Every export of rect.pep, each under its own name.
export * from geometry;Every export of the module geometry.
export [perimeter as outline, Side] from "measure";Only the listed exports, each under its own name or the one after as.
 
# The shapes module: its interface is its files' exports, passed on whole or
# by name.
export * from "rect";                               # every export of rect.pep
export [perimeter as outline, Side] from "measure"; # two, one renamed

import "shapes";

main(): int {
    var r = make_rect(3, 4);
    assert(area(r) == 12, "from export * of rect.pep");
    assert(outline(r) == 14, "from the export list, renamed");

    var s = Side();
    s.length = 5;
    assert(s.length == 5, "a struct from the export list");
    return 0;
}

  • It re-exports; it doesn’t import. export * from "rect"; makes no name visible in the file that contains it. A file that also uses those names imports them as usual, with import "rect";.

  • Only exports travel. Private declarations stay behind, and extensions stay visible only as they always are.

  • Collisions are errors. If two re-exports, or a re-export and one of the file’s own exports, publish one name for different declarations, the compiler reports it at the export ... from that adds the second one. List the names you want instead, or rename one with as:

    shapes/module.pep:2:1: error: 'area' is exported twice by module 'shapes'
    2 | export * from "measure";
      | ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
shapes/module.pep:1:1: note: also exported here
    1 | export * from "rect";
      | ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

  • Like imports, export ... from lines come before every declaration and take attributes such as [Target(...)]. A deprecated declaration stays deprecated through every re-export.

A module’s files stay inside it

A string import from a file of a module must name a file of the same module, or a module folder. Reaching into another module’s files, or out of your own module, is an error:

main.pep:1:8: error: "geometry/shapes.pep" belongs to another module; import that module's folder instead
    1 | import "geometry/shapes";
      |        ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

geometry/bad.pep:1:8: error: "../main.pep" is outside this file's module; a module imports only its own files
    1 | import "../main";
      |        ^~~~~~~~~

A file outside any module, such as your program’s main.pep, may import files in its own folder or below, except files inside a module folder there.

Files of one module may import each other, even in a cycle.

Dependencies and search paths

A module that is not part of your program’s own folders, such as a library you depend on, is imported by name rather than by string:

import geometry;

main(): int {
    var p: geometry.Point = geometry.make(3, 4);
    return p.x - 3;
}

An identifier import looks for a folder geometry/module.pep on the search paths: first each directory you pass with -I, in order, then the runtime directory. If geometry lives in a deps folder, compile with:

pepper main.pep -I deps -o main

An identifier import never looks next to the importing file. A module that is not found is reported with the places the compiler looked:

main.pep:1:8: error: cannot find module 'nowhere': no nowhere/module.pep in deps, /home/you/pepper/runtime
    1 | import nowhere;
      |        ^~~~~~~

The standard library’s io module is found this way, in the runtime directory: import io; then io.File, or import [File, Path] from io;.

The runtime prelude and --import

The runtime itself is not a module: its files are loaded into every program, and their exports, such as String, Array, and Result, are visible everywhere without an import.

--import NAME adds a module, or a file NAME.pep, found on the search paths to that prelude, so every file sees its exports without importing it. A name that resolves to neither is an error:

error: --import no_such_module: cannot find module 'no_such_module' (no no_such_module/module.pep) or file no_such_module.pep in /home/you/pepper/runtime

A prelude module may redefine an export of the runtime, such as panic: it is loaded ahead of the runtime and its definition wins. It must declare the name itself (export panic(...)); re-exporting another function under a runtime export’s name is an error, since the runtime’s own files would keep calling their version.

How imported names behave

  • Qualified names work wherever a name does: in expressions (g.rect_area(1, 2), or a module variable such as u.base), in types (var p: g.Point, g.Box<int>[], a return type g.Point), and in generic constructions (g.Box<int>()). A generic construction is recognized only for a qualifier imported earlier in the file; elsewhere a.b < c, d > (e) is two comparisons.
  • Local names shadow imports. A local variable, parameter, generic parameter, or struct member hides a qualifier or an imported name of the same name.
  • A file may not import a name it also declares at top level: 'make' is imported but also declared in this file.
  • An import list wins. A name listed in import [...] takes priority over the same name from a whole-file import. When two whole-file imports provide the same name differently, using that name is an error because it is ambiguous.
  • An imported name keeps its original identity. An imported name is compiled as the name of the declaration it denotes, so a local with that declaration’s own name may not be in scope where you use it. If g.rect_area is shapes.pep’s area, calling g.rect_area(1, 2) inside a function with a parameter named area is an error that asks you to rename one of them.

Each file has its own namespace for its private declarations, so files and modules may reuse private names freely. Exported names share one namespace, so two modules may not export the same name, even though users reach them through different qualifiers. See Each file has its own namespace.

Platform-specific imports

An import or a re-export may carry attributes like any declaration. With a [Target(...)] attribute it applies only on matching platforms and is ignored elsewhere (see Platform selection):

[Target(OS(Linux))] import linux;
[Target(OS(Linux))] export linux_open as open;
[Target(OS(Windows))] export * from "windows";

Atomics

An atomic value can be read and updated by several threads at once without a data race: every operation on it happens as one indivisible step. Pepper provides this through Atomic<T>, the equivalent of C++ std::atomic<T>.

Threads themselves, and the rules for what they can share, are in Threads and shared data.

Atomic<T>

Atomic<T> is part of the runtime, so you can use it without an import. The Atomic reference documents every method.

main(): int {
    var a = Atomic<int>(41);
    assert(a.load() == 41, "the initial value");

    a.store(1);
    assert(a.load() == 1, "store replaces the value");

    # The fetch_ operations return the value from before the change.
    assert(a.fetch_add(5) == 1, "fetch_add returns the old value");
    assert(a.load() == 6, "1 + 5");
    assert(a.fetch_sub(2) == 6, "fetch_sub returns the old value");
    assert(a.load() == 4, "6 - 2");

    var flags = Atomic<uint>(12);          # 0b1100
    flags.fetch_and(10);                   # 0b1000
    assert(flags.load() == 8, "fetch_and");
    flags.fetch_or(1);                     # 0b1001
    assert(flags.load() == 9, "fetch_or");
    flags.fetch_xor(9);                    # 0b0000
    assert(flags.load() == 0, "fetch_xor");

    # compare_exchange stores only if the current value is the expected one.
    assert(a.compare_exchange(4, 10), "4 matched, so 10 is stored");
    assert(!a.compare_exchange(4, 20), "10 does not match 4");
    assert(a.load() == 10, "the failed exchange changed nothing");

    # exchange always stores, and returns what was there.
    assert(a.exchange(3) == 10, "exchange returns the old value");
    assert(a.load() == 3, "and stores the new one");

    assert(a.is_lock_free(), "an int fits a native atomic instruction");
    return 0;
}
MethodWhat it does
Atomic<T>(value)Creates an atomic holding value (zero, or null for a struct type, if you leave it out).
load()Reads the value.
store(v)Replaces the value with v.
compare_exchange(expected, desired)Stores desired only if the value is currently expected; returns whether it did.
exchange(v)Stores v and returns the value it replaced.
fetch_add(v), fetch_sub(v)Adds or subtracts v; returns the value from before. Overflow wraps around.
fetch_and(v), fetch_or(v), fetch_xor(v)Combines the value bitwise with v; returns the value from before.
is_lock_free()Reports whether the operations use native atomic instructions.

Each operation compiles to a native atomic instruction. load has acquire ordering, store has release ordering, and compare_exchange and the fetch_ operations are acquire-release. exchange is a compare_exchange retry loop.

Some details differ from C++:

  • compare_exchange does not update expected when it fails. Call load() to get the current value before you retry.
  • The comparison is bitwise. For a floating-point T, 0.0 and -0.0 are different values. For a struct T, it compares references (whether both are the same object), not the objects’ contents.
  • The fetch_ operations are for integer types. Calling one on a floating-point or struct T compiles, but stops the program when it runs.
  • is_lock_free() is true for any number type of 8 bytes or less, and for any struct type.
  • A struct T must be thread-safe. Atomic is a [Shared] struct, so its T must be a [Shared] struct too (or a number, pointer or function type). Atomic<String> is a compile error. See What threads can share.

Common patterns

# An Atomic can only hold a thread-safe struct: one declared [Shared], whose
# fields are const and thread-safe.
[Shared]
struct Config {
    const version: int;
    construct(this.version = 0) {}
}

# A module-scope Atomic that every function can share.
const next_id = Atomic<long>(0);

new_id(): long {
    return next_id.fetch_add(1) + 1;
}

# The compare-and-swap retry loop: build any read-modify-write update.
double_it(Atomic<int> value) {
    var current = value.load();
    while (!value.compare_exchange(current, current * 2)) {
        current = value.load();
    }
}

main(): int {
    assert(new_id() == 1, "first id");
    assert(new_id() == 2, "second id");

    var n = Atomic<int>(21);
    double_it(n);
    assert(n.load() == 42, "doubled");

    # Copies of an Atomic share one slot, because Atomic is a struct.
    var same = n;
    same.store(7);
    assert(n.load() == 7, "both names see the store");

    # An Atomic can hold a struct reference.
    var first = Config(1);
    var current = Atomic<Config>(first);
    assert(current.load().version == 1, "load returns the reference");

    # compare_exchange compares references: the same object, not equal contents.
    var lookalike = Config(1);
    var second = Config(2);
    assert(!current.compare_exchange(lookalike, second), "a different object");
    assert(current.compare_exchange(first, second), "the same object");
    assert(current.load().version == 2, "swapped");

    var old = current.exchange(first);
    assert(old.version == 2, "exchange hands back the old reference");
    return 0;
}
  • A shared counter. fetch_add(1) hands out unique, increasing numbers, even when many threads call it at once.
  • A compare-and-swap loop. To apply any update that the fetch_ operations do not cover, load the current value, compute the new one, and compare_exchange; if another thread changed the value in between, the exchange fails and you try again with a fresh load().
  • Sharing an Atomic. Atomic is a [Shared] struct, so copying one copies a reference: every copy shares the same slot, on any thread. To share a value, pass the Atomic around or keep it in a module-scope variable.
  • Atomic struct references. An Atomic<T> whose T is a [Shared] struct type holds a counted reference, and follows the same rules as any other reference (see Memory and reference counting): store and a successful compare_exchange take a reference to the new value and release the one they replace, and load and exchange give you your own reference. The swap in the slot is itself atomic, so concurrent stores never leak or release a value twice. When the Atomic itself is destroyed, it releases the reference it holds.

Atomics and threads

Atomic<T> is how threads share a value that changes. Keep it in a module-scope variable, or in a const field of a [Shared] struct, and every thread sees the same slot. Threads and shared data shows it together with Thread.spawn.

Threads and shared data

The async module starts and manages operating-system threads. Pepper keeps threads safe by controlling what they can share: only thread-safe values can be reached from more than one thread, and the compiler checks the types that claim to be.

Starting and joining threads

import async;

const finished = Atomic<int>(0);

# A thread body is a function that takes nothing and returns nothing.
count_to_a_million() {
    var me = async.Thread.current();
    assert(me.name() == "counter", "a spawned thread sees its own Thread");
    var total: long = 0;
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 1000000) {
        total = total + 1;
        i = i + 1;
    }
    finished.fetch_add(1);
}

main(): int {
    var main_thread = async.Thread.current();
    assert(main_thread.name() == "main", "the main thread is named main");

    var first = async.Thread.spawn(count_to_a_million, "counter");
    var second = async.Thread.spawn(count_to_a_million, "counter");
    assert(first.id() != second.id(), "every thread has its own id");

    first.join();
    second.join();
    assert(first.is_finished(), "join waits for the body to return");
    assert(finished.load() == 2, "both bodies ran");

    # How many threads can run at once, and on how many cores.
    assert(async.logical_cpus() >= async.physical_cpus(), "hyperthreads count once in physical_cpus");
    return 0;
}
  • Thread.spawn(body, name) starts a new thread that runs body, a function of type ThreadBody (an alias for shared () => void), and returns its Thread. The name is optional.
  • join() waits for the body to return. After join(), the joining thread sees everything the other thread did. A thread can be joined once.
  • Thread.current() returns the calling thread’s Thread. For a spawned thread it is the same object that spawn returned. The main thread’s is named main.
  • id() returns the thread’s ThreadId, a number that no other thread in the program has. Ids come from the runtime, starting at 1, and are not the operating system’s thread ids. A ThreadId can be compared and used as a HashMap key.
  • is_finished() reports whether the body has returned. is_joinable() reports whether join() may be called.
  • Thread.sleep(milliseconds) pauses the calling thread.
  • Dropping every reference to a Thread without joining it detaches the thread. It keeps running, and its resources are freed when it finishes.
  • A panic or failed assert on any thread ends the whole program.

The body can be a named function or a closure. A closure passes data to the thread through its captures:

var total = Atomic<long>(0);
var step: long = 5;
var worker = async.Thread.spawn(() => { total.fetch_add(step); });
worker.join();

The body is a shared function value, so the closure may capture only thread-safe values (numbers, Atomic<T>, [Shared] structs, shared function values). The compiler checks each capture and reports one that is not thread-safe where the closure uses it:

main.pep:5:46: error: closure passed as 'shared () => void' captures 'name' of type 'String', which is not thread-safe
    5 |     var t = async.Thread.spawn(() => { print(name); });
      |                                              ^~~~
main.pep:4:9: note: capture a thread-safe value instead, such as an 'Atomic<T>' or a '[Shared]' struct
    4 |     var name = "worker";
      |         ^~~~

Every module-scope variable is const and thread-safe, so any thread may use one (see below).

The Thread reference documents every method.

Processor counts

  • async.logical_cpus() returns how many threads the machine can run at the same moment. A core with hyperthreading (SMT) counts two or more times. This is a good default size for a pool of CPU-bound worker threads.
  • async.physical_cpus() counts each physical core once. On Linux it reads the kernel’s CPU topology, on macOS hw.physicalcpu, and on Windows GetLogicalProcessorInformationEx. Inside a virtual machine it counts the cores the hypervisor presents, because the guest cannot see the host’s hardware.

Per-thread values with ThreadLocal

ThreadLocal<T> is a variable with a separate value on every thread:

import [Thread, ThreadLocal] from async;

# One variable, a separate value on every thread.
const calls = ThreadLocal<int>();
const totals = Atomic<long>(0);

record_call() {
    calls.set(calls.get() + 1);
}

work() {
    record_call();
    record_call();
    record_call();
    totals.fetch_add(calls.get());   # 3 on every thread
}

main(): int {
    record_call();
    var a = Thread.spawn(work);
    var b = Thread.spawn(work);
    a.join();
    b.join();
    assert(totals.load() == 6, "each thread counted its own calls");
    assert(calls.get() == 1, "the main thread's value is its own");
    return 0;
}
  • get() returns the calling thread’s value, or the default of T (zero, or null for a struct) until that thread calls set.
  • set(value) replaces only the calling thread’s value.
  • is_set() and clear() test for and remove the calling thread’s value.
  • When a thread exits, its value is released.

A ThreadLocal is usually a module-scope variable. Its T does not need to be thread-safe, because each value stays on the thread that stored it. The ThreadLocal reference has the details.

What threads can share

Every struct value is a counted reference (see Memory and reference counting). Updating a count from two threads at once can free an object too early or never, so by default a struct’s counts are plain, fast increments, and its objects must stay on one thread.

A struct declared [Shared] has atomic reference counts, so references to one object can be copied and dropped on any thread:

import async;

# A struct that threads can share: declared [Shared], every field const and
# thread-safe.
[Shared]
struct Settings {
    const port: int;
    const retries: int;
    construct(this.port = 0, this.retries = 0) {}
}

# Atomic<T> of a [Shared] struct: threads read the current settings, and one
# of them can replace them.
const current = Atomic<Settings>(Settings(8080, 3));
const seen = Atomic<long>(0);

read_settings() {
    var settings = current.load();
    seen.fetch_add(settings.port);
}

main(): int {
    var readers = Array<async.Thread>();
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 4) {
        readers.append(async.Thread.spawn(read_settings));
        i = i + 1;
    }
    for (var reader in readers) {
        reader.join();
    }
    assert(seen.load() == 4 * 8080, "every reader saw the settings");

    current.store(Settings(9090, 5));
    assert(current.load().port == 9090, "replaced");
    return 0;
}

The compiler checks every [Shared] struct:

  • Every field must be const. It is set once, by the constructor, and never assigned again, so threads cannot race on it. To change shared state, give the struct a thread-safe member such as an Atomic<T>.

  • Every field must have a thread-safe type. These are the thread-safe types:

    • numbers and bool;
    • raw pointers (__ptr<T>) to thread-safe types;
    • shared function types, such as shared () => void (see Function types);
    • [Shared] structs, and weak references to them;
    • tuples whose elements are all thread-safe.

    String, Array and other structs that are not [Shared] are not thread-safe, and neither is a plain function type such as () => void:

    main.pep:3:14: error: field 'F' of shared struct 'H' has type '() => void', which is not thread-safe
    3 |     const F: () => void;
      |              ^~~~~~~~~~
note: declare the function type 'shared () => void' to share it between threads

A generic [Shared] struct is checked for each set of type arguments. Atomic<T> is [Shared], so Atomic<int> and Atomic<Settings> are fine, and Atomic<String> is an error that points at the code that names it:

main.pep:2:13: error: shared struct 'Atomic<String>' requires thread-safe type arguments; 'String' is not thread-safe
    2 |     var a = Atomic<String>();
      |             ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~
runtime/atomic.pep:40:17: note: required by field 'Data' declared here
runtime/string.pep:33:15: note: 'String' is not declared [Shared]

A field that is not const, or that has a type that is not thread-safe, is reported at the field:

main.pep:3:9: error: field 'Count' of shared struct 'Counter' must be 'const'
    3 |     int Count;
      |         ^~~~~
note: threads change a shared struct's state through a thread-safe member such as 'Atomic<T>'

Thread, ThreadLocal<T> and Atomic<T> are [Shared] themselves, and so are the synchronization types below: Channel<T>, Mutex<T>, Condition<T>, Once and Lazy<T>.

Module-scope variables

Every module-scope variable is implicitly shared, exported or not: any function can use it, and any function can run on any thread. So it follows the same two rules as a field of a [Shared] struct:

  • It must be const. To change the value, give it a thread-safe type such as Atomic<T>, or a ThreadLocal<T> for a value per thread.
  • It must have a thread-safe type from the list above. A String, an Array, a struct that is not [Shared], a trait value, or a plain function value cannot be a module-scope variable; keep it in a local, or in a ThreadLocal<T>.
const max_workers = 8;                  # a number
const jobs_done = Atomic<long>(0);      # changed with fetch_add
const settings = Settings(8080, 3);     # a [Shared] struct
const scratch = ThreadLocal<String>();  # a String per thread
const double: shared (int) => int = (x: int) => x * 2;  # a shared function

A variable that breaks a rule is reported at its name or type:

main.pep:1:5: error: module-scope variable 'hits' must be 'const'
    1 | var hits = 0;
      |     ^~~~
note: every thread can reach a module-scope variable; change its state through a thread-safe type such as 'Atomic<T>' or 'ThreadLocal<T>'

main.pep:2:15: error: module-scope variable 'origin' has type 'Plain', which is not thread-safe
    2 | const origin: Plain = Plain();
      |               ^~~~~
main.pep:1:8: note: 'Plain' is not declared [Shared]
    1 | struct Plain { var x: int; }
      |        ^~~~~

Module-scope variables are initialized before main runs, in the order they are declared, and each file’s after those of the files it imports and of the runtime. So an initializer may use the variables of the files it imports.

Channels

A Channel<T> carries values from threads that send to threads that receive, with any number of each, like a Go channel:

import async;

# The main thread sends numbers to a pool of workers, and the workers send
# their squares back on a second channel.
const jobs = async.Channel<long>(16);    # bounded: send waits while 16 are queued
const results = async.Channel<long>();   # unbounded: send never waits

square_numbers() {
    # The loop ends once `jobs` is closed and empty.
    for (var n in jobs) {
        results.send(n * n);
    }
}

# A `shared` function value is thread-safe, so a channel can carry work.
const tasks = async.Channel<async.ThreadBody>();
const ran = Atomic<long>(0);

run_tasks() {
    for (var task in tasks) {
        task();
    }
}

main(): int {
    var workers = Array<async.Thread>();
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 4) {
        workers.append(async.Thread.spawn(square_numbers));
        i = i + 1;
    }
    var n: long = 1;
    while (n <= 100) {
        jobs.send(n);
        n = n + 1;
    }
    jobs.close();   # no more work: each worker's loop ends
    for (var worker in workers) {
        worker.join();
    }
    results.close();

    var total: long = 0;
    for (var square in results) {
        total = total + square;
    }
    assert(total == 338350, "the sum of the first 100 squares");

    # receive() returns a Result: an error once the channel is closed and
    # empty. A closed channel refuses new values.
    assert(results.receive().is_err(), "nothing left");
    assert(!results.send(1), "closed");

    var runner = async.Thread.spawn(run_tasks);
    tasks.send(() => { ran.fetch_add(1); });
    tasks.send(() => { ran.fetch_add(10); });
    tasks.close();
    runner.join();
    assert(ran.load() == 11, "the runner ran both tasks");
    return 0;
}
  • Channel<T>(capacity) makes a channel that holds at most capacity values; send waits while it is full. Channel<T>() (capacity 0) is unbounded, and send never waits.
  • send(value) queues a value and returns true, or returns false if the channel is closed. try_send(value) does not wait: it also returns false when a bounded channel is full.
  • receive() waits for the next value and returns a Result<T>: the value, or an error once the channel is closed and empty. try_receive() returns an error right away when nothing is waiting, and receive_for(milliseconds) when the time runs out.
  • close() ends the stream and wakes every waiting sender and receiver. Values already queued are still received. Only close() closes a channel: dropping it, or ending the threads that use it, does not.
  • for (var v in channel) receives until the channel is closed and empty. Several threads can loop over one channel; each value goes to one of them.
  • Values from one sender arrive in the order they were sent.
  • count() and is_closed() report the channel’s state, which other threads may change right after.

send copies each value with copy, so the receiver gets a value that nothing on the sending thread references. A String, an Array or a struct of them can be sent, and changing the original after send does not change what was sent:

import async;

# A plain struct: no destructor and no raw pointer, so `copy` copies it field
# by field. Its Strings and Array are not thread-safe, but a Channel copies
# every value it is sent.
struct Order {
    String customer;
    Array<String> items;
    Order next;

    construct(this.customer = "") {
        items = Array<String>();
    }
}

const orders = async.Channel<Order>();
const letters = Atomic<long>(0);

count_letters() {
    for (var order in orders) {
        for (var item in order.items) {
            letters.fetch_add(item.length() as long);
        }
    }
}

main(): int {
    # copy() makes a new object for everything that is not thread-safe.
    var tea = Order("ada");
    tea.items.append("tea");
    var copied = copy(tea);
    copied.items[0].append(" and cake");
    assert(tea.items[0] == "tea", "the original is unchanged");

    # Objects referenced twice stay shared in the copy, and cycles are kept.
    var a = Order("a");
    var b = Order("b");
    a.next = b;
    b.next = a;
    var pair = copy(a);
    assert(pair.next.next === pair, "the copy has the same cycle");
    a.next = __null;
    pair.next = __null;

    # send() copies, so the order can change after it is sent.
    var worker = async.Thread.spawn(count_letters);
    var order = Order("grace");
    order.items.append("coffee");
    orders.send(order);
    order.items.append("biscuits");
    orders.close();
    worker.join();
    assert(letters.load() == 6, "the worker saw only what was sent");
    return 0;
}

A thread-safe value (a number, a [Shared] struct, a shared function value such as a ThreadBody) is not copied but passed by reference, which makes a channel of ThreadBody values a work queue. A type that copy cannot copy is an error where the Channel is named:

main.pep:9:17: error: cannot copy 'Array<Log>' because it contains 'File', which cannot be copied
    9 |     var lines = async.Channel<Array<Log>>();
      |                 ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
note: 'Array<Log>' copies its values of type 'Log'
main.pep:5:5: note: 'Log' reaches it through field 'out'
    5 |     io.File out;
      |     ^~~~~~~
runtime/io/file.pep:244:15: note: 'File' owns an operating-system handle and cannot be copied; send its path, and open the file again on the receiving thread

The Channel reference documents every method.

Mutexes and conditions

A Mutex<T> owns a value that one thread at a time can use. The way to reach the value is a with block:

import async;

# Each balance is an Atomic, but moving money changes both, and no thread
# may see it in neither account. A Mutex makes the pair of updates one step.
[Shared]
struct Accounts {
    const checking: Atomic<long>;
    const savings: Atomic<long>;
    construct(long checking = 0, long savings = 0) {
        this.checking = Atomic<long>(checking);
        this.savings = Atomic<long>(savings);
    }
}

const accounts = async.Mutex<Accounts>(Accounts(1000, 0));
const bad_totals = Atomic<long>(0);

move_to_savings() {
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 1000) {
        with (var a = accounts.lock()) {
            a.checking.fetch_sub(1);
            a.savings.fetch_add(1);
        }   # unlocked here, on every path out of the block
        i = i + 1;
    }
}

audit() {
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 1000) {
        with (var a = accounts.lock()) {
            if (a.checking.load() + a.savings.load() != 1000) {
                bad_totals.fetch_add(1);
            }
        }
        i = i + 1;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var mover = async.Thread.spawn(move_to_savings);
    var auditor = async.Thread.spawn(audit);
    mover.join();
    auditor.join();
    assert(bad_totals.load() == 0, "the auditor never saw money missing");

    # try_lock does not wait: the body runs if the lock was free, and the
    # else block runs if another thread held it.
    with (var a = accounts.try_lock()) {
        assert(a.savings.load() == 1000, "everything was moved");
    } else {
        assert(false, "no other thread holds the lock now");
    }
    return 0;
}
  • with (var v = mutex.lock()) { ... } waits until no other thread holds the lock, binds the value, and unlocks when the block ends by any path: the end of the body, return, break or continue. Everything a thread did while it held the lock is visible to the next thread that takes it.
  • try_lock() does not wait. With with (var v = mutex.try_lock()) { ... } else { ... }, the body runs if the lock was free and the else block runs if it was not.
  • A Mutex is not re-entrant: calling lock() again on the thread that holds it panics instead of deadlocking. is_held() reports whether the calling thread holds it.
  • A panic while a Mutex is held ends the program, so a Mutex is never left “poisoned”.

T must be thread-safe for now (see Limitations). A Mutex is for keeping several changes together, such as two Atomic fields of a [Shared] struct that must move as a unit, or state behind a raw pointer. For one number, Atomic<T> is simpler.

A Condition<T> lets a thread that holds a Mutex sleep until another thread changes the value. Get one with mutex.condition():

import async;

# A baker makes pies and eaters take them. The eaters sleep on a Condition
# until there is a pie, or until the bakery closes.
[Shared]
struct Bakery {
    const pies: Atomic<long>;
    const closed: Atomic<int>;
    construct() {
        pies = Atomic<long>(0);
        closed = Atomic<int>(0);
    }
}

const bakery = async.Mutex<Bakery>(Bakery());
const changed = bakery.condition();
const eaten = Atomic<long>(0);

eat() {
    while (true) {
        with (var b = bakery.lock()) {
