            # Wait in a loop: a wakeup can be spurious, and another eater
            # may take the pie first.
            while (b.pies.load() == 0 && b.closed.load() == 0) {
                changed.wait();   # unlocks, sleeps, locks again
            }
            if (b.pies.load() == 0) {
                return;   # closed and nothing left; the block unlocks
            }
            b.pies.fetch_sub(1);
        }
        eaten.fetch_add(1);
    }
}

main(): int {
    var eaters = Array<async.Thread>();
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 3) {
        eaters.append(async.Thread.spawn(eat));
        i = i + 1;
    }
    i = 0;
    while (i < 50) {
        with (var b = bakery.lock()) {
            b.pies.fetch_add(1);
            changed.notify_one();
        }
        i = i + 1;
    }
    with (var b = bakery.lock()) {
        b.closed.store(1);
        changed.notify_all();
    }
    for (var eater in eaters) {
        eater.join();
    }
    assert(eaten.load() == 50, "every pie was eaten once");
    return 0;
}
  • wait() must run while the calling thread holds the Mutex, normally inside its with block. It unlocks, sleeps until notified, and locks again before returning. It panics if the thread does not hold the Mutex.
  • A thread can wake without a notification (a spurious wakeup), and another thread may get to the value first, so always wait in a loop that re-checks what it waits for.
  • wait_for(milliseconds) gives up after that long and returns false.
  • notify_one() wakes one waiting thread, and notify_all() wakes them all.

The Mutex reference documents Mutex, MutexGuard and Condition.

Once and Lazy

Once runs a function exactly once, however many threads ask, and Lazy<T> builds a value the first time a thread asks for it:

import async;

[Shared]
struct Settings {
    const workers: long;
    construct(this.workers = 1) {}
}

const builds = Atomic<long>(0);

load_settings(): Settings {
    builds.fetch_add(1);
    return Settings(async.logical_cpus() as long);
}

# Built by the first thread that calls get(), not before main runs.
const settings = async.Lazy<Settings>(load_settings);

# Runs initialize() once, however many threads call it.
const setup = async.Once();
const setups = Atomic<long>(0);

initialize() {
    setups.fetch_add(1);
}

work() {
    setup.call(initialize);
    assert(settings.get().workers >= 1, "every thread sees the settings");
}

main(): int {
    assert(!settings.is_built(), "nothing is built before first use");
    var workers = Array<async.Thread>();
    var i = 0;
    while (i < 4) {
        workers.append(async.Thread.spawn(work));
        i = i + 1;
    }
    for (var worker in workers) {
        worker.join();
    }
    assert(builds.load() == 1, "the settings were built once");
    assert(setups.load() == 1, "initialize ran once");
    assert(setup.is_done(), "done");
    return 0;
}
  • once.call(body) runs body if no call has run one yet. Threads that call it while the body runs wait for it, and every caller sees what the body did. is_done() reports whether it has run.
  • Lazy<T>(initializer) takes a shared () => T function. The first get() runs it; later calls, from any thread, return the same value. is_built() reports whether it has run.
  • A Lazy<T> is the usual way to give a module-scope variable a value that is expensive to build, or that cannot be built before main runs. Its T must be thread-safe, since every thread gets the same value.

The Once reference has the details.

Limitations

  • Mutex<T> and Lazy<T> hold only thread-safe values for now, so a String or an Array crosses threads only through a Channel, which copies it.
  • copy cannot copy a trait value yet, so a Channel of a trait type, or of an Array of one, is an error.
  • There is no reader-writer lock, no select over several channels, and no barrier or semaphore. A Mutex is not re-entrant.
  • Deadlocks are not detected, except locking a Mutex twice on one thread, which panics.

Calling C

Pepper programs can call functions written in C: the C standard library, your operating system’s API, or any other library with a C interface. You declare the C function’s prototype with extern, make sure the library that defines it is linked, and then call it like any Pepper function.

Declaring a C function

An extern declaration is a C prototype, written the way C writes it: the return type, the name, and the parameter types, each followed by a parameter name. It has no body.

extern ulong strlen(char* s);
extern int strcmp(char* a, char* b);
extern void* malloc(ulong size);
extern void free(void* p);

After that, strlen("hello") calls the C library’s strlen. The compiler does not check your prototype against any C header, so it must match the real C function exactly. A wrong parameter or return type compiles, and then passes or reads the wrong bits when the program runs.

Match C types with Pepper types of the same size:

CPepper
char, signed char, unsigned charchar or i8, u8 or byte
short, unsigned shortshort or i16, ushort or u16
int, unsigned intint or i32, uint or u32
long long, unsigned long long, int64_t, size_t (64-bit)long or i64, ulong or u64
float, doublefloat, double
T*T* in the prototype

Pepper’s long is always 64 bits. C’s long is 64 bits on Linux and macOS but 32 bits on Windows, so a C function that takes long needs a different prototype per platform (see [LinkName] below for an example).

Some things to know about extern declarations:

  • The prototype uses C syntax only. The name(parameter: type): type form that Pepper functions use is a parse error on an extern, and __ptr<T> as a parameter type is an error too: write char*, not __ptr<char>.
  • An extern is private to its file, like every declaration. Write export extern ... to let files that import this one call it (see Visibility).
  • Several files may declare the same extern, since every declaration names the same C function. The runtime already declares malloc, free and a few others privately, and your program can declare them again.
  • An extern is linked by name. If no linked library defines it, the program compiles and then fails to link, with the linker reporting an undefined symbol. See Linking for how to add libraries.

Passing strings

Pepper’s String is a reference-counted object, not a C string, so a String value cannot be passed where C expects char*.

  • A string literal passed to an extern is passed as a NUL-terminated C string, with no allocation: strlen("hello") is 5. The same is true wherever a literal flows into a __ptr<char>, __ptr<u8>, or __ptr<void> in Pepper code (a variable, field, argument, or return value), so String("hello") copies the literal’s five bytes.
  • A String value is passed with c_str(), which returns the String’s NUL-terminated buffer as a __ptr<char>. The pointer belongs to the String: it is valid until the String is destroyed or grows (for example with append or +=). An empty String() has no buffer, so its c_str() is __null; check before you pass it to a C function that does not accept NULL.
  • To turn a C string back into a String, pass the pointer to the constructor: String(p) copies a NUL-terminated C string, and String(p, size) copies size bytes. If you already know the number of code points, pass it as a third argument: String(p, size, String.length_of(p, size)). The constructor copies the bytes, so you still own (and may free) the C buffer. A C string is never passed or stored where a String is expected without this copy: that is an error.
# Passing strings to C, and turning C strings back into Strings.

extern ulong strlen(char* s);
extern int strcmp(char* a, char* b);
extern char* strchr(char* s, int c);

main(): int {
    # A string literal passed to an extern is a NUL-terminated C string.
    assert(strlen("hello") == 5, "a literal is passed as a C string");
    assert(strcmp("abc", "abc") == 0, "equal C strings compare as 0");

    # So is one stored in a __ptr<char>, or passed to the String
    # constructor, which copies it.
    __ptr<char> greeting = "hello";
    assert(strlen(greeting) == 5, "a literal in a __ptr<char> is a C string");
    assert(String("hello").size() == 5, "String(literal) copies its bytes");

    # A String value goes through c_str().
    var name = "Pepper";
    name += " and C";
    assert(strlen(name.c_str()) == name.size(), "c_str() is the String's bytes");

    # A char* result is a __ptr<char>. Copy it into a String with the
    # constructor: the byte size and the number of code points.
    var rest = strchr(name.c_str(), 32);   # 32 is ' '
    assert(rest != __null, "strchr found a space");
    var size = strlen(rest);
    var copy = String(rest, size, String.length_of(rest, size));
    assert(copy == " and C", "the copy holds the tail of the C string");
    return 0;
}

Passing a String value directly to an extern’s char* parameter is an error:

main.pep:4:19: error: cannot pass 'String' to parameter 1 of extern 'strlen' of type 'char*'; pass its 'c_str()'
    4 |     return strlen(name) as int;
      |                   ^~~~

The reverse is an error too: a char* (or any raw pointer, void* included) is never a String, so passing one where a String is expected, assigning it to a String variable, or returning it as one is rejected. Copy it into a String with String(p) first:

main.pep:3:19: error: cannot pass a value of type '__ptr<char>' as argument 2 of 'assert', which has type 'String'; use 'String(...)' to copy a C string

Variadic functions

A C function with a variable argument list, such as printf or snprintf, ends its prototype with ... after at least one named parameter:

extern int snprintf(char* buffer, ulong size, char* format, ...);

The call uses the C calling convention for variadic functions, which differs from a plain call on some targets (such as macOS on Apple silicon). Each argument past the named ones gets C’s default argument promotions: integers narrower than int widen to int, and float widens to double. Match your format string to the promoted type: %d for a u8, %f for a float, and %lld (not %ld) for a long, because C’s long is 32 bits on Windows.

... must come last and must follow a named parameter: extern int f(...); is a parse error.

# Calling a C function that takes a variable argument list.

extern void* malloc(ulong size);
extern void free(void* p);

# On Windows, snprintf lives in legacy_stdio_definitions.
[Target(OS(Windows))] [Link("legacy_stdio_definitions")]
extern int snprintf(char* buffer, ulong size, char* format, ...);
[Target(!OS(Windows))]
extern int snprintf(char* buffer, ulong size, char* format, ...);

main(): int {
    var buffer = malloc(64) as __ptr<char>;
    u8 small = 200;     # widened to int
    float half = 0.5;   # widened to double
    long big = 5000000000;
    var written = snprintf(buffer, 64, "%d %.1f %lld %s", small, half, big, "ok");
    var text = String(buffer, written as ulong, String.length_of(buffer, written as ulong));
    free(buffer);
    assert(text == "200 0.5 5000000000 ok", "snprintf formatted every argument");
    return 0;
}

On Windows, the C runtime defines snprintf and its relatives inline in its headers, so the linkable symbols live in a separate library, legacy_stdio_definitions. The example links it with [Link] on Windows only.

Pointers

C code deals in raw pointers, and Pepper has a raw pointer type for talking to it: __ptr<T>, such as __ptr<void>, __ptr<char> or __ptr<__ptr<int>>. A void* returned by an extern is a __ptr<void> in Pepper, and a __ptr<T> can be passed to an extern that takes a C pointer.

A pointer supports these operations:

OperationMeaning
p[i]reads or writes the element i places from p; its type is T, so var v = p[i] declares a T
p + nthe address n elements after p (n bytes for __ptr<void>)
p == __null, p != __nullcompares with the null pointer
p == q, p != qcompares two addresses (a pointer also compares with an integer address)
p as ulong, p as longthe address as an integer
p as __ptr<U>the same address, seen as pointing at U

__null is the null pointer. sizeof<T>() gives the size of a type in bytes, which you need to allocate memory for C.

T* spelling is for extern prototypes only, since they mirror C. Everywhere else (parameters, return types, struct fields, locals, globals, type aliases, generic arguments and casts) you write __ptr<T>, and T* is a compile error:

clear(p: void*, n: ulong) { }

main.pep:1:10: error: C pointer syntax `void*` is only allowed in extern declarations; use `__ptr<void>`
    1 | clear(p: void*, n: ulong) { }
      |          ^~~~

# Raw pointers: __ptr<T> in Pepper code, T* in extern prototypes.

extern void* malloc(ulong size);
extern void free(void* p);
extern void* memset(void* p, int value, ulong size);

struct Buffer {
    var data: __ptr<u8> = __null;
    var size: ulong = 0;
}

make_buffer(size: ulong): Buffer {
    var b = Buffer();
    b.data = malloc(size) as __ptr<u8>;
    b.size = size;
    memset(b.data, 0, size);
    return b;
}

sum(values: __ptr<int>, count: int): int {
    var total = 0;
    var i = 0;
    while (i < count) {
        total = total + values[i];
        i = i + 1;
    }
    return total;
}

# `var` takes the element type from the pointer: `big` is a ulong, so a value
# above 2^32 reads back whole.
first(values: __ptr<ulong>): ulong {
    var big = values[0];
    return big;
}

main(): int {
    # Indexing reads and writes elements of T.
    var numbers = malloc(4 * sizeof<int>()) as __ptr<int>;
    numbers[0] = 10;
    numbers[1] = 20;
    numbers[2] = 30;
    numbers[3] = 40;
    assert(sum(numbers, 4) == 100, "indexing reads what was written");

    var wide = malloc(sizeof<ulong>()) as __ptr<ulong>;
    wide[0] = 5000000000;
    assert(first(wide) == 5000000000, "var v = p[i] has the pointee's type");
    free(wide);

    # p + n moves by n elements, not n bytes.
    var third = numbers + 2;
    assert(third[0] == 30, "numbers + 2 points at the third element");
    assert((third as ulong) - (numbers as ulong) == 8, "two ints are eight bytes");

    # __ptr<void> strides by bytes; cast to see the memory another way.
    var raw = numbers as __ptr<void>;
    var bytes = raw as __ptr<u8>;
    assert(bytes[4] == 20, "the second int starts at byte 4 (little-endian)");
    free(raw);

    # Pointers can live in struct fields, and compare with __null.
    var b = make_buffer(16);
    assert(b.data != __null, "malloc returned memory");
    assert(b.data[15] == 0, "memset cleared it");
    free(b.data);
    b.data = __null;

    # A pointer to pointers: indexing yields the inner pointer, and indexing
    # again reads or writes through it.
    var rows = malloc(2 * sizeof<__ptr<int>>()) as __ptr<__ptr<int>>;
    var row = malloc(sizeof<int>()) as __ptr<int>;
    rows[1] = row;
    rows[1][0] = 7;
    assert(rows[1][0] == 7, "rows[1][0] reads through the row pointer");
    assert(row[0] == 7, "rows[1] is the row pointer");
    free(row);
    free(rows);
    return 0;
}

Pointers are not reference-counted and nothing checks them: indexing past the end of an allocation, using memory after free, or forgetting to free it behaves exactly as it would in C. Keep raw pointers inside small functions or structs that own the memory, and give the rest of your program ordinary Pepper types.

Passing a Pepper function to C

A Pepper function value is a reference-counted object, not a C function pointer. For C, Pepper has a separate type, extern (P...) => R: the address of a function that C calls with the C calling convention. Declare a callback parameter of a C function with it, and pass a named function, or a closure that captures nothing:

# `extern (P...) => R` is a C function pointer type: qsort calls `compare`
# with pointers to two elements.
extern void qsort(void* base, ulong count, ulong size, extern (void*, void*) => int compare);

ascending(a: __ptr<void>, b: __ptr<void>): int {
    __ptr<int> x = a;
    __ptr<int> y = b;
    return x[0] - y[0];
}

main(): int {
    __ptr<int> numbers = _pep_malloc(4 * sizeof<int>());
    numbers[0] = 30;
    numbers[1] = 10;
    numbers[2] = 40;
    numbers[3] = 20;

    # A named function converts to the C function pointer type.
    qsort(numbers, 4, sizeof<int>(), ascending);
    assert(numbers[0] == 10 && numbers[3] == 40, "ascending");

    # So does a closure that captures nothing; its parameter types come
    # from the C function pointer type.
    qsort(numbers, 4, sizeof<int>(), (a, b) => {
        __ptr<int> x = a;
        __ptr<int> y = b;
        return y[0] - x[0];
    });
    assert(numbers[0] == 40 && numbers[3] == 10, "descending");

    # A C function pointer is a value like a number: store it, call it.
    var compare: extern (__ptr<void>, __ptr<void>) => int = ascending;
    assert(compare(numbers, numbers + 1) > 0, "40 sorts after 30");

    _pep_free(numbers);
    return 0;
}

  • What converts. A named free function, a static method (Order.descending), or a closure literal that captures nothing, whose parameter and return types match the C function pointer type exactly. A closure literal takes its parameter types from the type, as it does from any function type.

  • Nothing captured. C calls the function with its arguments alone, so a closure that captures a value, or a bound method (p.get), cannot convert:

    main.pep:4:43: error: closure passed as C function pointer type 'extern (void*, void*) => int' captures 'bias'; a C function pointer cannot hold captured values
    4 |     qsort(_pep_malloc(4), 0, 4, (a, b) => bias);
      |                                           ^~~~
main.pep:3:9: note: pass what the callback needs through the C function's context argument instead
    3 |     var bias = 1;
      |         ^~~~

    Most C APIs that take a callback also take a void* context argument that they hand back to it; pass what the callback needs through that. A function value held in a variable does not convert either, since it may be a closure.

  • C types only. C knows nothing of reference counts, so the parameter and return types must be numbers, bool, pointers, or other C function pointer types. A struct is an error: C function pointer type 'extern (String) => int' cannot use 'String'; C passes only numbers, 'bool' and pointers. Write pointers as void* in an extern declaration and as __ptr<void> elsewhere, as for any pointer.

  • A value like a number. A C function pointer has no reference count. Store it in a variable, field or module-scope const, return it, pass it to other threads, or call it: compare(a, b) calls through it with the C calling convention. Calling a null one (from C) panics. It converts to void* but not to a Pepper function type; wrap it in a closure that calls it, (x) => f(x), to use it as one.

__function_pointer(name) also gives a function’s address, as an untyped __ptr<void>. It takes a free function, or a static method of the struct whose method uses it. Inside a generic struct the static method is the one for the current type arguments, which is how ThreadLocal<T> gives the operating system a destructor for its T. Prefer a C function pointer type where the signature is known, since the compiler then checks it.

Binding to a different symbol name

[LinkName("symbol")] binds an extern to a C symbol whose name differs from the declared one. Pepper code calls the extern by its declared name, and the object file refers to symbol. Use it to give a C function a clearer name, to avoid clashing with a Pepper name, or, together with [Target], to bind one Pepper name to a different symbol on each platform:

# [LinkName] binds a Pepper name to a C symbol with a different name.

# llabs takes a 64-bit long long on every platform.
[LinkName("llabs")] extern long absolute(long value);

# C's long is 64 bits on Linux and macOS but 32 bits on Windows, so
# the 64-bit labs is only right away from Windows.
[Target(OS(Windows))] [LinkName("llabs")] extern long abs64(long value);
[Target(!OS(Windows))] [LinkName("labs")] extern long abs64(long value);

main(): int {
    assert(absolute(-7) == 7, "absolute calls llabs");
    assert(abs64(-5000000000) == 5000000000, "abs64 calls a 64-bit abs");
    return 0;
}

Real uses of the per-platform form include macOS on Intel, where the 64-bit-inode version of stat is named stat$INODE64:

[Target(OS(Mac) && Arch(x64))] [LinkName("stat$INODE64")] extern int stat(char* path, void* buf);
[Target(OS(Mac) && Arch(aarch64))] extern int stat(char* path, void* buf);

LinkName takes exactly one non-empty string, and an extern may carry at most one. Anything else is an error, such as:

main.pep:1:34: error: LinkName on extern 'pep_abs' takes exactly one string: [LinkName("sym")]
    1 | [LinkName("a", "b")] extern long pep_abs(long v);
      |                                  ^~~~~~~

Linking the library

A C function is only callable if the library that defines it is linked into your program. The C library is always linked. For anything else, attach [Link("name")] to the extern or pass -l name to the compiler. The Linking chapter covers both.

[Target(OS(Windows))] [Link("gdi32")] extern ulong GetStockObject(int kind);

Calling Pepper from C

Pepper builds executables, and there is no supported way yet for C code to call Pepper functions. The parser accepts an [Export] attribute on a function, intended to give it a C interface with object references passed as opaque handles, but the compiler does not act on it yet: [Export] currently has no effect.

Do not confuse [Export] with the export keyword, which makes a declaration visible to other Pepper files (see Visibility).

Platform selection

Some code only makes sense on one platform: a Windows API call, a Linux-specific constant, a type whose size differs between operating systems. The [Target(...)] attribute keeps a declaration or a block only when you compile for a matching platform, so one source file can hold every platform’s version side by side.

The target is always the platform you compile on: Pepper does not cross-compile yet.

Writing a condition

Target takes one boolean condition built from two predicates:

  • OS(...) is true on the named operating system: Windows, Mac (or MacOS), or Linux.
  • Arch(...) is true on the named processor architecture: x64 or aarch64.

Values are matched case-insensitively, so OS(windows) works too. Combine predicates with && (or and), || (or or), !, and parentheses:

[Target(OS(Windows))]                     # Windows
[Target(!OS(Windows))]                    # Linux and macOS
[Target(OS(Linux) && Arch(x64))]          # Linux on x64 only
[Target(OS(Windows) || OS(Mac))]          # Windows and macOS
[Target(!(OS(Windows) || OS(Mac)))]       # Linux

An item may carry several attributes. They are combined with “and”: the item is kept only when every Target on it matches. Other attributes on the same item, such as [Link(...)], do not affect the selection.

What it applies to

Put [Target(...)] before any top-level declaration: a function, an extern, a struct, a trait, an extension, a type alias, a module-scope variable, an import, or a re-export. It also works on the methods inside a struct, trait or extension. When two declarations share a name, give them conditions that never match on the same platform, so exactly one survives:

# One name, a different declaration per platform.

# Type aliases: a Windows HANDLE is a pointer, a POSIX descriptor an int.
[Target(OS(Windows))] type Handle = __ptr<void>;
[Target(!OS(Windows))] type Handle = int;

# Module-scope variables (const and thread-safe, like every global).
[Target(OS(Windows))] const max_path = 260;
[Target(!OS(Windows))] const max_path = 4096;

# Functions.
[Target(OS(Windows))]
platform_name(): String { return "Windows"; }

[Target(OS(Mac))]
platform_name(): String { return "macOS"; }

[Target(OS(Linux))]
platform_name(): String { return "Linux"; }

# Structs, and methods inside them.
[Target(Arch(x64))]
struct Cpu {
    var name: String = "x64";
}

[Target(Arch(aarch64))]
struct Cpu {
    var name: String = "aarch64";
}

struct Word {
    [Target(Arch(x64) || Arch(aarch64))]
    bits(): int { return 64; }
}

main(): int {
    var name = platform_name();
    print(name);
    print(Cpu().name);
    assert(Word().bits() == 64, "every supported target is 64-bit");

    [Target(OS(Windows))] {
        assert(sizeof<Handle>() == 8, "a HANDLE is a pointer");
        assert(name == "Windows", "the Windows function was kept");
    }
    [Target(!OS(Windows))] {
        assert(sizeof<Handle>() == 4, "a descriptor is an int");
        assert(max_path == 4096, "the POSIX variable was kept");
    }
    return 0;
}

The attribute goes before export, not after it: [Target(OS(Windows))] export f(). Writing export [Target(OS(Windows))] f() is a parse error.

Blocks inside a function

Inside a function body, attach [Target(...)] to a block { ... }. The block is kept or removed like a declaration. Blocks may nest, and may appear inside if and loop bodies.

This is the usual way to wrap C functions that differ per platform: declare each platform’s extern with its own condition, and call each one from a block with the same condition.

# A portable wrapper over a C function that has a different name on
# each platform. kernel32 is linked by default on Windows, and getpid is
# in the C library everywhere else.

[Target(OS(Windows))] extern uint GetCurrentProcessId();
[Target(!OS(Windows))] extern int getpid();

process_id(): long {
    [Target(OS(Windows))] {
        return GetCurrentProcessId() as long;
    }
    [Target(!OS(Windows))] {
        return getpid() as long;
    }
}

main(): int {
    var id = process_id();
    print(id);
    assert(id > 0, "a running process has a positive id");
    return 0;
}

What happens to code that does not match

Code whose condition does not match is removed before type checking. That is what makes the examples above work: a call to GetCurrentProcessId, which exists only on Windows, is fine in a block that is removed everywhere else. Removed code is not checked for errors beyond parsing, so a typo in it goes unnoticed until you build on the platform that keeps it.

It still has to parse, and every Target condition is validated on every platform, even one inside removed code. A misspelled value is always an error, never a silent “no match”:

[Target(OS(Darwin))] f(): int { return 1; }

main.pep:1:1: error: unknown OS value `Darwin` (expected Windows, Mac, MacOS, Linux) in [Target(OS(Darwin))]
    1 | [Target(OS(Darwin))] f(): int { return 1; }
      | ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

Other mistakes the compiler reports:

  • Target takes exactly one condition: [Target(OS(Windows), Arch(x64))] is an error; write [Target(OS(Windows) && Arch(x64))].
  • Only OS(...) and Arch(...) may appear inside it. A bare name ([Target(Windows)]), another call ([Target(Foo(x))]), not in place of !, or a nested Target is an error.
  • OS(...) and Arch(...) are only valid inside Target(...), and the brackets hold one attribute: [OS(Windows)] and [Target(OS(Windows)) && Export] are parse errors.
  • Struct fields cannot carry [Target(...)]. Select the whole struct instead, as Cpu does in the first example.

Linking

The Pepper compiler turns your program into a native executable in two steps: it compiles all of your files, and the runtime, into one object file, and then links that object file with the system libraries it needs. When you call C functions, linking is where their definitions come from, so this chapter explains what is linked by default and how to add more.

What the compiler links

The compiler uses clang as its link driver, rather than calling the platform’s linker directly, because clang already knows each platform’s startup files, C library and SDK locations. It uses the clang from the LLVM installation the compiler was built with when there is one, and otherwise the first clang on your PATH.

Every program is linked with:

  • Linux: the C library, which clang adds.
  • macOS: libSystem, which contains the C library, plus the SDK and platform-version flags. clang adds all of these, using the Xcode Command Line Tools.
  • Windows: the Microsoft C runtime (the UCRT, vcruntime and the CRT startup library), which clang adds, plus kernel32, user32 and shell32, which the Pepper compiler adds. clang finds the Visual Studio and Windows SDK libraries itself, so you do not need a Developer Command Prompt.

Any library file named lib<name>.a in the runtime directory is linked too.

Anything else must be linked explicitly. On Linux, that includes the math library (m) for functions such as pow, and pthread on older systems.

Linking a library from the source: [Link]

Attach [Link("name")] to an extern declaration to link the library that defines it. The compiler passes -lname to the linker, just like -l on the command line. Name the library the way the linker does: m for libm, gdi32 for gdi32.lib.

# [Link("name")] on an extern links the library that defines it.

# gdi32 is not one of the libraries Windows programs link by default.
[Target(OS(Windows))] [Link("gdi32")] extern ulong GetStockObject(int kind);

# pthread_self is in libpthread on older Linux systems, in libc on newer
# ones, and in libSystem on macOS; linking pthread works on all of them.
[Target(!OS(Windows))] [Link("pthread")] extern ulong pthread_self();

# The math library is separate from the C library on Linux.
[Target(OS(Linux))] [Link("m")] extern double pow(double base, double exponent);
[Target(!OS(Linux))] extern double pow(double base, double exponent);

main(): int {
    [Target(OS(Windows))] {
        # 5 is NULL_BRUSH, a stock object that always exists.
        assert(GetStockObject(5) != 0, "gdi32 was linked");
    }
    [Target(!OS(Windows))] {
        assert(pthread_self() != 0, "pthread was linked");
    }
    assert(pow(2.0, 10.0) == 1024.0, "the math library was linked");
    return 0;
}
  • The library is linked only if the extern survives platform selection. Combining [Link] with [Target], as above, links a library only on the platforms that need it.
  • Each library is linked once, however many externs name it.
  • The library is linked even when nothing calls the extern.

Putting [Link] in the source keeps the knowledge of which library a function comes from next to its declaration, so nobody building your program has to remember a command-line flag.

Linking a library from the command line: -l

The -l option links a library for one build:

pepper main.pep -l m -o main

Repeat it for more libraries (-l m -l pthread). There is no option to add a library search directory yet, so a library must be one the linker finds on its own: a system library, or one in a directory the linker searches by default.

When linking fails

If a function you declared with extern is not defined by any linked library, compilation succeeds and the link fails. The compiler prints what the linker said, which names the missing symbol:

error: Link failed: clang exited with exit status: 1
/usr/bin/ld: .pep/build/main.units/main.o: in function `main':
main:(.text+0x25ba): undefined reference to `no_such_function'
clang: error: linker command failed with exit code 1 (use -v to see invocation)

The object the linker names is the one for the file that made the call, in .pep/build/<name>.units/. Symbols of your own functions appear as the compiler spells them for the linker: a name that is not just letters, digits, _ and . is written _P followed by the name with other characters as _ and two hex digits, so Array<int>.count appears as _PArray_3cint_3e.count. extern functions keep their C names.

To fix it, check the spelling of the extern against the C function (or use [LinkName] when the names differ), then add the library that defines it with [Link] or -l.

If the compiler cannot run clang at all, the error says Link failed: cannot run clang. Install clang (on Linux, from your package manager) or the Xcode Command Line Tools on macOS.

Warnings from the linker are passed through unchanged, even when the link succeeds.

Output files

With no -o, the executable is named after the first source file, without an extension, in the directory where you run the compiler. Windows will not run a file without .exe, so pass -o main.exe there. The object files and other intermediate files go in .pep/build/ under the current directory, or in the directory you pass to --build: one object per file of the program in <name>.units/, and <name>.o, which runs the program’s global initializers. Unchanged objects come from the build cache in .pep/cache/ (--no-cache skips it).

Compiler errors

When the compiler cannot build your program, it tells you what is wrong and exactly where, in the same format as clang. This chapter shows how to read an error and lists some common ones.

Reading an error

Here is a program with a mistake in it:

struct Point {
    var x: int;
    var y: int;
}

main(): int {
    var p = Point();
    var total = p.x + p.z;
    return total;
}

Compiling it prints:

main.pep:8:25: error: no member named 'z' in 'Point'
    8 |     var total = p.x + p.z;
      |                         ^

An error has three parts:

  1. The header: the file, line and column, then error: and the message. Editors and terminals recognize file:line:column and can jump to it.
  2. The source line the error is on, with its line number.
  3. A caret (^) under the exact token you need to change, with ~ under the rest of the token when it is longer than one character. Here, the member name z, not the whole statement.

Messages follow clang’s style: they start in lowercase, put names and types in single quotes ('z', 'Point'), and say what to do when there is an obvious fix. The fixed program is:

# The program from "Reading an error", after the fix.

struct Point {
    var x: int;
    var y: int;
}

main(): int {
    var p = Point();
    p.x = 3;
    p.y = 4;
    var total = p.x + p.y;
    assert(total == 7, "x + y");
    return 0;
}

Notes

Some errors are explained by a second place in your code. The compiler shows that place as a note: after the error, in the same format. When one file calls a function that another file did not export, the note points at the declaration:

main.pep:4:12: error: 'helper' is private to lib.pep; declare it with 'export' to use it from another file
    4 |     return helper();
      |            ^~~~~~
lib.pep:1:1: note: declared here
    1 | helper(): int { return 1; }
      | ^~~~~~

When a brace is never closed, the error is at the end of the file, and the note shows the brace that opened the block:

main.pep:5:1: error: expected '}'
    5 | 
      | ^
main.pep:1:13: note: to match this '{'
    1 | main(): int {
      |             ^

One error at a time

The compiler stops at the first error it finds and exits with status 1, so fix that error and compile again. It never produces an executable from a program with an error.

On a terminal, errors are colored like clang’s. Set the NO_COLOR environment variable to turn colors off. Output that goes to a file or a pipe is never colored.

Warnings do not stop the build, and the compiler prints them whether or not it also finds an error. Besides the linker’s own warnings, which the compiler passes through (see Linking), the only warnings today are deprecations.

Deprecations

A use of a declaration marked [Deprecated("message")] warns at the name, with the message and a note at the declaration. When the attribute names a replacement, the warning shows the rename under the code, and the language server offers it as a quick fix. See Deprecation.

main.pep:9:15: warning: 'clone' is deprecated: use 'copy' instead [deprecated]
    9 |     var b = a.clone();
      |               ^~~~~
      |               copy
main.pep:4:5: note: 'clone' declared here
    4 |     clone(): Counter { return this.copy(); }
      |     ^~~~~

Syntax of the language itself that is on its way out keeps compiling for a while too, with a warning that names its replacement and shows the fix under the code it replaces:

main.pep:3:5: warning: `new` is deprecated; constructors are spelled `construct`, matching `destruct` [deprecated]
    3 |     new(this.x = 1) {}
      |     ^~~
      |     construct
DeprecatedReplacement
new(...) constructorconstruct(...)
io.Path.join_str(String)io.Path.join(Path), which accepts a String
String.clone()String.copy()
io.Path.is_dir()io.Path.is_directory()
io.File.set_len(ulong)io.File.set_length(ulong)
HashMap.cap(), HashSet.cap()capacity()
A statement or declaration without its ;End it with ; (see Statements and semicolons)

Errors in the editor

The Pepper language server, used by the VS Code extension, reports the same errors at the same places while you type. It underlines the token the caret would point at, and shows each note as related information that you can click to jump to.

Common errors

These are some errors you are likely to meet, with the exact text the compiler prints.

A name that was never declared, often a typo:

main.pep:3:16: error: use of undeclared identifier 'nope'
    3 |     return a + nope;
      |                ^~~~

main.pep:2:12: error: call to undeclared function 'frob'
    2 |     return frob(1);
      |            ^~~~

A type name that does not exist, often a misspelled type or a missing import:

main.pep:2:12: error: unknown type name 'Frob'
    2 |     var x: Frob = 1;
      |            ^~~~

A field or method the struct does not have:

main.pep:6:7: error: no member named 'move' in 'Point'
    6 |     p.move(1);
      |       ^~~~

The wrong number of arguments. When the function has default parameters, the message gives the range:

main.pep:4:12: error: too few arguments to 'add': expected 2, have 1
    4 |     return add(1);
      |            ^~~

main.pep:3:12: error: too many arguments to 'f': expected at most 2, have 3
    3 |     return f(1, 2, 3) + f();
      |            ^

A call no overload fits, or that two fit equally well. Each note is a candidate, and says why it does not fit (see Overloading):

main.pep:3:22: error: call to 'f' is ambiguous
    3 | main(): int { return f(1); }
      |                      ^
main.pep:1:1: note: candidate function
    1 | f(x: long): int { return 1; }
      | ^
main.pep:2:1: note: candidate function
    2 | f(x: short): int { return 2; }
      | ^

A syntax error. The parser says what it expected at the first place the code stopped making sense:

main.pep:2:14: error: expected ')'
    2 |     if (true {
      |              ^

main.pep:2:13: error: expected expression
    2 |     var x = ;
      |             ^

A value of the wrong type. Numbers convert among themselves, as in C, but a number is never a struct and one struct type is never another, except that a struct converts to a trait it declares, and a value converts to a struct whose constructor from its type is [Implicit]. A raw pointer is never a struct either: a __ptr<char> is not a String (copy it with String(p)), and a String is not a __ptr<char> (pass its c_str()). The check applies wherever a value flows into a typed place: a variable’s initializer, an assignment, an argument and a return:

main.pep:2:13: error: cannot initialize variable 'x' of type 'int' with a value of type 'String'
    2 |     int x = "hello";
      |             ^~~~~~~

main.pep:3:12: error: cannot pass a value of type 'i32' as argument 1 of 'g', which has type 'String'
    3 |     return g(5);
      |            ^

main.pep:3:19: error: cannot pass a value of type '__ptr<char>' as argument 2 of 'assert', which has type 'String'; use 'String(...)' to copy a C string
    3 |     assert(false, p);
      |                   ^

main.pep:2:12: error: cannot return a value of type 'String' from 'f', which returns 'int'
    2 |     return "a";
      |            ^~~

A struct used as a trait it does not declare (see Traits as types):

main.pep:11:20: error: 'Circle' does not conform to trait 'Shape'
   11 |     var s: Shape = Circle();
      |                    ^~~~~~
main.pep:6:8: note: declare it with 'extend Circle with Shape'
    6 | struct Circle {
      |        ^~~~~~

A value that is not thread-safe given to another thread (see Threads): here a thread body, a shared function value, that captures a String:

main.pep:5:46: error: closure passed as 'shared () => void' captures 'name' of type 'String', which is not thread-safe
    5 |     var t = async.Thread.spawn(() => { print(name); });
      |                                              ^~~~
main.pep:4:9: note: capture a thread-safe value instead, such as an 'Atomic<T>' or a '[Shared]' struct
    4 |     var name = "worker";
      |         ^~~~

The same name declared twice in one scope:

main.pep:3:9: error: redefinition of 'n'
    3 |     var n = 2;
      |         ^
main.pep:2:9: note: previous definition is here
    2 |     var n = 1;
      |         ^

Looping over something that cannot be iterated (see Iteration):

main.pep:3:19: error: type 'int' is not iterable: implement Iterable<T> (iterate()) or Iterator<T> (next() and current())
    3 |     for (var i in 5) {
      |                   ^

An import that names nothing (see Modules and imports):

main.pep:1:8: error: cannot find "nope.pep" or the module folder "nope" next to this file
    1 | import "nope";
      |        ^~~~~~

Errors while the program runs

Some problems can only be found while the program runs, such as an array index out of range or a failed assert. These are not compiler errors: the program prints a message and stops immediately (it aborts, so it exits with a failure status instead of returning from main). See assert and panic.

Current limitations

The type check does not compare the type arguments of generic structs yet, so Array<int> a = Array<String>(); compiles. Read such code carefully: the resulting program has undefined behavior.

Deprecation

When an API is on its way out, mark it [Deprecated("message")]. Code that uses it still compiles, but every use gets a warning that shows your message, the same way clang reports [[deprecated]]. That gives the code that depends on the API time to move before you remove it.

struct Temperature {
    int celsius;

    # A method on its way out: it still works, but every call warns.
    [Deprecated("use 'fahrenheit' instead", "fahrenheit")]
    to_f(): int { return fahrenheit(); }

    fahrenheit(): int { return celsius * 9 / 5 + 32; }
}

# The second argument names the replacement: the warning carries a fix that
# renames the call.
[Deprecated("use 'boiling' instead", "boiling")]
boiling_point(): Temperature { return boiling(); }

boiling(): Temperature {
    var t = Temperature();
    t.celsius = 100;
    return t;
}

main(): int {
    # Both calls warn, and both still work.
    var old = boiling_point();
    assert(old.to_f() == 212, "the old API still works");

    var t = boiling();
    assert(t.fahrenheit() == 212, "100 C is 212 F");
    return 0;
}

Compiling this prints a warning at each use of a deprecated name, with a note at its declaration. The second of the two is:

main.pep:25:16: warning: 'to_f' is deprecated: use 'fahrenheit' instead [deprecated]
   25 |     assert(old.to_f() == 212, "the old API still works");
      |                ^~~~
      |                fahrenheit
main.pep:6:5: note: 'to_f' declared here
    6 |     to_f(): int { return fahrenheit(); }
      |     ^~~~

Warnings never stop the build. The program above compiles and runs.

The replacement

The attribute takes an optional second argument: the name that replaces the deprecated one.

[Deprecated("use 'copy' instead", "copy")]
clone(): Counter { ... }

With a replacement, the warning comes with a fix that renames the use. The compiler shows the new name under the old one, as in the warning above, and the language server offers the rename as a quick fix. The fix only renames, so give a replacement only when the new declaration can be used the same way. Otherwise leave it out and explain the change in the message.

An empty message is allowed. The warning then says only 'name' is deprecated.

What it applies to

Put [Deprecated(...)] before any declaration other code refers to by name:

  • a function or an extern
  • a struct, a trait, or a type alias
  • a module-scope variable
  • a field, a method, or a constructor of a struct
  • a method in a trait or in an extension (put it on each method, not on the extend block)

A doc comment goes above the attribute, as it does for any attribute.

Which uses warn

Every use of the declaration warns, at the name you wrote:

  • a call, a reference to a function or variable, or a construction S() (a deprecated constructor is reported at the struct’s name)
  • a type name, anywhere a type is written
  • a member access x.name, a method call x.name(), a static call S.name(), and a bare field or method name inside the struct’s own methods
  • an entry in an import list, import [name] from m, and a qualified use m.name, where the warning points at name

These do not warn:

  • the declaration itself
  • a use inside a declaration that is deprecated too, including the methods of a deprecated struct and closures written inside a deprecated function, so old code can keep calling old code without noise
  • a re-export, export name as other or export [name] from source, which passes the name on without using it

Mistakes in the attribute

A malformed attribute is an error at the attribute:

main.pep:1:1: error: 'Deprecated' expects a message string: [Deprecated("message")]
    1 | [Deprecated]
      | ^~~~~~~~~~~~

The other errors are 'Deprecated' expects the replacement as a string, the replacement 'a b' is not a name, too many arguments to 'Deprecated': expected at most 2, have 3, duplicate 'Deprecated' attribute, and 'Deprecated' cannot be applied to an extension or a destructor.

Limitations

  • The compiler does not report uses it writes for you: the next() and current() calls of a for loop, the enter() call of a with block, and a call to an operator overload such as a + b.
  • The language server shows the warnings for the file you are editing. It does not show them for other files the program imports.

Documentation comments

Comments explain code to the people who read it. Pepper has one comment syntax, #, and a convention on top of it for documentation: lines starting with ## directly above a declaration document that declaration. Your editor shows them when you hover over a use of the name, and the standard library reference in this book is generated from them.

Comments

A comment starts with # and runs to the end of the line. It can take a whole line or follow code:

# Count the lines that are not blank.
var count = 0;   # blank lines are skipped below

Pepper has no block comment syntax: to comment out several lines, start each with #. (// is not a comment in Pepper.)

Doc comments

A doc comment is a run of consecutive lines that start with ##, directly above the declaration it documents:

## Returns the larger of `a` and `b`.
##
## @param a the first value
## @param b the second value
## @returns `a` when it is at least `b`, otherwise `b`
larger(a: int, b: int): int {
    return a >= b ? a : b;
}
  • The text of each line is what follows ## . A line that is just ## is a blank line in the documentation, which separates paragraphs.
  • The comment must sit directly above the declaration, with no blank line in between. Attributes and export or static may come between the comment and the declaration, so put the comment above the attributes.
  • Only lines that start with exactly two # count. A single # is an ordinary comment, and a line starting with three or more, such as a decorative #######, is not documentation.
  • A doc comment works on functions, structs, traits, extensions, type aliases, module-scope variables, and the fields, constructors and methods inside a struct, trait or extension.

What to write

Start with a description: what the declaration does, in the third person (“Returns …”, “Appends …”), followed by what a caller needs to know, such as edge cases, who owns what, how long it takes, and differences between platforms. The description is Markdown, so put code in backticks.

After the description come tags, each starting a line with @. A tag’s text runs until the next tag.

TagUse it for
@typeParam T descriptioneach generic parameter of a generic struct, trait, type alias or function
@param name descriptioneach parameter of a function, method or constructor
@returns descriptionwhat the function returns, whenever it returns a value
@panics descriptioneach way a caller can make the function panic (a failed assert, a call to panic)
@examplesample code, indented on the lines that follow; line breaks are kept
@see namea related declaration
@note texta remark that does not fit elsewhere

Write tag descriptions like clang writes errors: lowercase start, no trailing period. A field or a type alias usually needs only one line: ## The number of bytes in the buffer.

Keep notes that matter only to the people maintaining the code in ordinary # comments inside the body. The doc comment is the contract with callers.

Here is a small documented library:

# A small library, documented the way the standard library is.
# This line and the one above are ordinary comments: they start with a
# single '#'. Doc comments start with '##'.

## A last-in, first-out collection.
##
## Elements come out in the reverse of the order they went in.
##
## @typeParam T the type of the elements
## @example
##     var s = Stack<int>();
##     s.push(1);
##     s.push(2);
##     var top = s.pop();   # top == 2
struct Stack<T> {
    ## The elements, bottom first.
    var items: Array<T> = Array<T>();

    ## Adds `value` on top of the stack.
    ##
    ## @param value the element to add
    push(value: T) {
        items.append(value);
    }

    ## Removes and returns the element on top of the stack.
    ##
    ## @returns the element that was pushed last
    ## @panics if the stack is empty
    ## @see Stack.push
    pop(): T {
        assert(items.count() > 0, "pop() on an empty Stack");
        return items.remove_at(items.count() - 1);
    }

    ## Returns the number of elements.
    ##
    ## @returns how many elements the stack holds
    count(): ulong {
        return items.count();
    }
}

## Returns the larger of `a` and `b`.
##
## @param a the first value
## @param b the second value
## @returns `a` when it is at least `b`, otherwise `b`
## @note ties return `a`
larger(a: int, b: int): int {
    # An implementation note for maintainers, not part of the docs.
    return a >= b ? a : b;
}

main(): int {
    var s = Stack<int>();
    s.push(1);
    s.push(2);
    assert(s.pop() == 2, "the last element comes out first");
    assert(s.count() == 1, "one element is left");
    assert(larger(3, 7) == 7, "larger returns the larger value");
    return 0;
}

Where doc comments appear

  • In your editor. The Pepper language server shows a declaration’s doc comment when you hover over its name, anywhere it is used. The description comes first, followed by sections for the type parameters, parameters, return value, panics, related declarations, notes and examples.
  • In the standard library reference. The reference pages are generated from the doc comments in the runtime, one page per runtime file. A ## block at the very top of a file, followed by a blank line, introduces that file’s page. Building this documentation explains how to regenerate them.

The standard library is fully documented

Every public declaration in the runtime must have a doc comment: every exported declaration whose name does not start with _, and every field, constructor and method of such a struct, trait or extension whose name does not start with _. Names starting with _ are internals shared between runtime files and are exempt.

A test enforces this, and reports a clang-style error for each gap: a missing doc comment, a comment with no description, a missing @param, @typeParam or @returns, a tag with no description, a @returns on a function that returns nothing, or a @param naming a parameter that does not exist. If you contribute to the runtime, run the test suite before you send a change. The compiler does not check the doc comments in your own programs, but following the same format gets you the same editor support.

Naming conventions

Pepper code, including the standard library, follows one set of naming rules. The compiler does not enforce them, but every example in this book, the runtime, and the tools in this repository follow them, and code you contribute should too.

WhatStyleExamples
Structs, traits, type aliasesPascalCaseString, HashMap, IoResult
Generic parametersone capital letter, or PascalCaseT, K, V, TValue, TError
Data members (fields)snake_caseunit_name, is_file
Methods and free functionssnake_caseappend, remove_at, read_to_string
Parameters and local variables, const includedsnake_casecapacity, best_so_far
Module-scope variables and constantssnake_casemax_requests, line_width
Private data members and methods_ then snake_case_readings, _is_empty
 
# Types are PascalCase; acronyms are written as words (`HttpClient`, not `HTTPClient`).
struct TemperatureLog {
    # Public data members are snake_case.
    var unit_name: String = "celsius";
    # Private data members start with `_`.
    var _readings: Array<int> = Array<int>();
    var _total: int = 0;

    # Methods and their parameters are snake_case.
    add_reading(degrees: int) {
        _readings.append(degrees);
        _total = _total + degrees;
    }

    average(): int {
        return _is_empty() ? 0 : _total / (_readings.count() as int);
    }

    # A private method starts with `_` too.
    _is_empty(): bool {
        return _readings.count() == 0;
    }
}

# Generic parameters are a single capital letter or PascalCase.
struct Pair<TFirst, TSecond> {
    var first: TFirst;
    var second: TSecond;

    construct(TFirst first, TSecond second) {
        this.first = first;
        this.second = second;
    }
}

# Free functions and local variables are snake_case.
largest_of<T>(values: Array<T>): T {
    var best_so_far = values[0];
    for (var value in values) {
        if (value > best_so_far) {
            best_so_far = value;
        }
    }
    return best_so_far;
}

main(): int {
    var log = TemperatureLog();
    log.add_reading(20);
    log.add_reading(24);
    assert(log.average() == 22, "average of the readings");
    assert(log.unit_name == "celsius", "public member");

    var pair = Pair<String, int>("answer", 42);
    assert(pair.second == 42, "generic struct");

    var numbers = Array<int>();
    numbers.append(3);
    numbers.append(9);
    numbers.append(4);
    assert(largest_of<int>(numbers) == 9, "generic function");
    return 0;
}

Types

A type name is PascalCase: each word starts with a capital letter, and there are no underscores. Write an acronym as a word, so IoError and HttpClient, not IOError or HTTPClient.

An enum’s cases are PascalCase too (Color.Red, Status.NotFound).

A generic parameter is either a single capital letter (T, U, K, V) or a PascalCase name, which by convention starts with T (TValue, TError). Use a single letter when the parameter’s role is obvious, and a name when a declaration has several parameters that a reader has to tell apart.

Members, functions, and variables

Everything else a program names is snake_case: lowercase words joined by underscores. That covers a struct’s data members, its methods, free functions, parameters, local variables, and module-scope variables. A const is no exception: write const line_width = 120;, not LINE_WIDTH. Pepper has no SCREAMING_CASE.

Spell words out. A name is read far more often than it is typed, so prefer iterate, index, and directory to iter, idx, and dir. Keep an abbreviation only when it is the usual name for the thing, such as io or c_str.

An extern declaration keeps the name the C library gives the function, whatever its style (GetCurrentProcessId), because that name is what the linker looks up. The same goes for the fields of a struct that mirrors a C struct.

Private members

Pepper has no member-level private keyword; any code that can reach an object can name all of its members. A leading _ marks a data member or method as private to its struct instead: code outside the struct, and its extensions, should not use it. The rest of the name is snake_case, as for a public member (_readings, _is_empty).

The standard library relies on this convention: its generated reference leaves _ members out, and doc comments are required only on members without the prefix.

A top-level declaration does not need the prefix to be private: it is private to its file unless it is exported (see Visibility). The runtime also uses the prefix on top-level helpers that its own files export to each other, such as _pep_malloc: they are not part of its public API, and the reference leaves them out too.

Formatting with pep-fmt

pep-fmt rewrites Pepper source in one standard style, so that every Pepper file reads the same and nobody has to argue about layout. It changes only whitespace, line breaks, the order of imports, and the form of function declarations; the program means exactly what it meant before.

pep-fmt is written in Pepper. Its parser is a separate library, tools/syntax, that other tools can use.

Running it

Build it with the compiler, with the tools folder on the import path:

pepper -I tools tools/pep-fmt/main.pep -o pep-fmt

It reads a source file on standard input and writes the formatted file to standard output:

pep-fmt < main.pep > formatted.pep

If the file does not parse, pep-fmt writes nothing to standard output, prints the first syntax error to standard error, and exits with status 1:

<stdin>:2:14: error: expected ')'

The style

Layout. Indentation is 4 spaces. A brace goes on the same line as the declaration or statement that owns it, and else goes between the braces:

if count > 0 {
    print(count);
} else {
    print("none");
}

A block stays on one line only when it holds a single break, continue or return and was written on one line: if done { return; }.

Line width. Lines are at most 120 columns. A list that does not fit (arguments, parameters, an import list) moves to its own indented line, and if it still does not fit, puts one item on each line:

report_values(
    first_argument, second_argument, third_argument, fourth_argument, fifth_argument, a_much_longer_argument
);
report_values(
    first_argument,
    second_argument,
    an_argument_so_long_that_the_whole_list_no_longer_fits_on_one_line_even_after_moving_it_down_one
);

A long expression breaks before each operator, and a chain of three or more method calls that does not fit breaks before each .:

if (first_condition_that_is_long(value) and second_condition_that_is_long(value)
    or third_condition_that_is_long(value)) {
    run();
}
total = first_value_that_is_long_enough
    + second_value_that_is_long_enough
    + third_value_that_is_long_and_longer_still;
var count = words
    .iterate()
    .filter((w) => w.length() > 4)
    .map((w) => w.length() * 2)
    .count_them_all();

Comments and string literals are never reflowed, so a line can stay longer than 120 columns when it has nowhere to break.

Attributes go one per line, each on its own line above the declaration:

[Target(OS(Linux))]
[Deprecated("use 'open' instead", "open")]
export open_file(path: String): int { return 0; }

Declarations. Pepper functions and methods are written with the parameter types after the names and the return type last, add(a: int, b: int): int. The C-style form, int add(int a, int b), is kept for extern functions, which mirror C prototypes; pep-fmt rewrites it everywhere else, including operator methods (bool ==(Point other) becomes ==(other: Point): bool), indexers (T [](ulong index) becomes [](index: ulong): T) and constructor parameters.

Blank lines. At most one blank line in a row. Top-level structs, traits, extensions and functions with a body are separated by one blank line.

Comments stay where they are, attached to the code next to them. Trailing comments on consecutive lines are aligned, two spaces after the longest line:

var total = 0;      # the running sum
var count = 0;      # how many values
var average = 0.0;  # total / count

Imports that are next to each other (no blank line or comment between them) are sorted: modules first (import io;), then files of this module (import "util";), each by name.

Semicolons are added where a statement or declaration leaves its ; out (see Statements and semicolons); none are removed.

Kept as written: parentheses around conditions (if x and if (x) are both fine), fields declared either way (int x; or var x: int;), and the file’s line endings (\n or \r\n).

Standard library reference

The standard library is the Pepper runtime: the files under runtime/ that every program is compiled with. Everything here is available without an import, except the io module, which a program brings in with import io (or import [File, Path] from io).

These pages are generated from the doc comments in the runtime’s source (see Documentation comments), so they always match the version of Pepper they were built with. Use the version picker at the top of the page to read the reference for another release.

Built-in functions

Source: runtime/internals.pep

assert

assert(condition: bool, message: String): void

Panics with message when condition is false; otherwise does nothing.

The check always runs; there is no release mode that removes it. A string literal message costs nothing when the assertion holds: the compiler passes the literal’s constant bytes to the runtime without building a String. Any other message is evaluated before the call.

Parameters

  • condition — the condition that must hold
  • message — the text to print on failure; a null String prints a generic message

Panics

  • if condition is false: prints message and aborts (see panic)

Example

    assert(count > 0, "count must be positive");

panic

panic(message: String)

Prints message and aborts the program. It never returns.

Writes message and a newline to standard output, or “Assertion failed with no additional information” when message is null. Then it flushes every C output stream, so the message is not lost when stdout is a pipe, and calls C abort(). No destructors or releases run, and the process ends abnormally (SIGABRT on POSIX).

Parameters

  • message — the text to print

Panics

  • always

Example

    panic("unreachable");

String

Source: runtime/string.pep

String

struct String

A mutable, reference-counted string of UTF-8 text. String literals ("text") are String values.

Two measures describe a String, and each API says which one it uses:

  • Code points (Unicode characters): length(), empty(), the indexer s[i], substr, and iteration.
  • Bytes of UTF-8: size(), find, starts_with, and ends_with. The byte size is the authoritative length, so the data may contain NUL bytes. It is never derived from a terminator.

A String is a reference type: var b = a; shares one object, so append, +=, and grow are visible through every reference. Use copy() or + for an independent copy. Equality (==) compares content.

A StringIterator panics when the String has been changed by append, +=, or grow since the iterator was created, so modifying a String while a for loop iterates it panics.

Example

    var s = "héllo";
    assert(s.length() == 5, "5 code points");
    assert(s.size() == 6, "6 bytes: 'é' is 2");
    s.append(" world");
    assert(s.find("world") == 7, "find returns a byte offset");
    assert(s[1] == "é", "the indexer counts code points");
    for (var c in s) {
        print(c);   # one code point per line
    }

Constructor

String()

construct(__ptr<char> data = __null, ulong size = 0, ulong length = 0)

Creates a String by copying size bytes of data into a new NUL-terminated buffer. With no arguments, it creates an empty String whose c_str() is __null.

When data is not __null and size is 0, data is treated as a NUL-terminated C string:

  • with length nonzero, size becomes the byte length of its first length code points (0 if the C string is not valid UTF-8);
  • with length 0, size becomes the C string’s whole byte length.

When length is 0, it is counted from the bytes. A nonzero length is trusted, not checked against the data. Runs in O(size).

Parameters

  • data — the bytes to copy; __null for an empty String (then pass no sizes, because they are stored as given)
  • size — the number of bytes to copy from data; 0 treats data as a NUL-terminated C string
  • length — the number of code points in those bytes

Example

    var empty = String();
    var s = "abc";
    var copy = String(s.c_str(), s.size(), s.length());

Static methods

length_of

static ulong length_of(__ptr<char> data, ulong size)

Returns the number of Unicode code points in the first size bytes at data. Runs in O(size).

Returns 0 when data is __null, when size is 0, or when the bytes are not valid UTF-8: any malformed or truncated sequence makes the whole count 0.

Parameters

  • data — the UTF-8 bytes to measure
  • size — the number of bytes to measure

Returns

  • the number of code points, or 0 for invalid UTF-8

Methods

length

ulong length()

Returns the number of Unicode code points. O(1).

Returns

  • the length in code points, not bytes

See also

  • size

grow

void grow(ulong new_size)

Extends the byte buffer to new_size bytes and fills the new bytes with zeros. Does nothing when new_size is not greater than size().

A low-level primitive for append. It updates size() but not length(), so the zero bytes it adds are not counted as code points until the caller overwrites them and fixes the length. When it grows the buffer, it invalidates running iterators and may reallocate the buffer, which invalidates pointers from c_str(). Runs in O(new_size).

Parameters

  • new_size — the new byte length

append

void append(String rhs)

Appends the content of rhs to this String in place, updating size() and length().

Always invalidates running iterators, even when rhs is empty, so appending during iteration panics. It may reallocate the buffer. Every reference to this String sees the change. Runs in O(size() + rhs.size()).

Parameters

  • rhs — the String to append; it is not modified

Example

    var s = "TEST";
    s.append("ING");   # s == "TESTING"

operator []

String [](int idx)

Returns the code point at code-point index idx as a new one-code-point String.

A negative idx counts from the end (s[-1] is the last code point). length() is added until the index is non-negative, so any negative index wraps, and s[-7] of a 3-code-point String is s[2]. Runs in O(idx) because it walks the UTF-8 bytes. Use for (var c in s) to visit every code point in O(size).

Parameters

  • idx — the code-point index; negative counts from the end

Returns

  • a new String that holds the one code point

Panics

  • if idx is not less than length() after the negative index is normalized, which includes any index into an empty String

Example

    var s = "TEST";
    assert(s[0] == "T", "first");
    assert(s[-1] == "T", "last");

operator ==

bool ==(String rhs)

Returns whether this String and rhs have the same content, compared byte by byte. Strings with different length() are unequal without comparing bytes. Runs in O(size()).

Parameters

  • rhs — the String to compare with

Returns

  • true when both hold the same bytes

operator +=

String +=(String rhs)

Appends rhs in place, like append, and returns this same String, not a copy. s += t modifies the object that every reference to s shares, and invalidates running iterators.

Parameters

  • rhs — the String to append

Returns

  • this String, after appending

Example

    var s = "a";
    s += "b";   # s == "ab"

size

ulong size()

Returns the number of bytes of UTF-8, not counting the NUL terminator. O(1).

Returns

  • the length in bytes, not code points

See also

  • length

iterate

StringIterator iterate()

Returns an iterator over the String’s code points, which makes String Iterable<String>: for (var c in s) visits each code point as a one-code-point String, the same value s[i] gives.

The iterator holds a reference to this String. The whole loop runs in O(size). Modifying the String during iteration makes the iterator panic.

Returns

  • a new iterator, positioned before the first code point

Example

    var n = 0;
    for (var c in "héllo") {
        n = n + 1;   # 5 iterations
    }

empty

bool empty()

Returns whether the String has no code points. O(1).

Returns

  • true when length() is 0

c_str

__ptr<char> c_str()

Returns the String’s buffer as a NUL-terminated C string.

The pointer is owned by the String. It is valid until the String is destroyed or append, +=, or grow reallocates it. It is __null for a String built with no data (String()), so check it before you pass it to C. C functions stop at the first NUL byte in the content.

Returns

  • the String’s NUL-terminated byte buffer, or __null

copy

String copy(Copies copies = __null)

Returns a new String with the same content and its own buffer, so later changes to either String do not affect the other. Runs in O(size()).

This is also how copy(value) copies a String (the Copyable<String> implementation), which passes copies.

Parameters

  • copies — the objects copied so far, when copy(value) calls it; a String holds no other objects, so it is not used

Returns

  • an independent copy of this String

Example

    var a = "abc";
    var b = a.copy();
    b.append("d");   # a is still "abc"

clone

[Deprecated("use 'copy' instead", "copy")] String clone()

Deprecated: use copy instead. Returns a new String with the same content and its own buffer.

Returns

  • an independent copy of this String

find

long find(String needle)

Returns the byte offset of the first occurrence of needle, or -1 when it does not occur.

The offset counts bytes, not code points. To use it with s[i] or substr, convert it with String.length_of(s.c_str(), offset). An empty needle is found at 0 unless this String is empty, in which case the result is -1. The result is also -1 when either String is not valid UTF-8. Runs in O(size() * needle.size()).

Parameters

  • needle — the text to search for

Returns

  • the byte offset of the first match, or -1 when there is none

Example

    var s = "TESTING";
    assert(s.find("STI") == 2, "byte offset");
    assert(s.find("xyz") == -1, "absent");

contains

bool contains(String needle)

Returns whether needle occurs in this String (find(needle) >= 0). Because of that, an empty String contains nothing, not even "". Runs in O(size() * needle.size()).

Parameters

  • needle — the text to search for

Returns

  • true when needle occurs

starts_with

bool starts_with(String prefix)

Returns whether this String begins with the bytes of prefix. An empty prefix always matches. Runs in O(prefix.size()).

Parameters

  • prefix — the text to test for

Returns

  • true when the first prefix.size() bytes equal prefix

ends_with

bool ends_with(String suffix)

Returns whether this String ends with the bytes of suffix. An empty suffix always matches. Runs in O(suffix.size()).

Parameters

  • suffix — the text to test for

Returns

  • true when the last suffix.size() bytes equal suffix

substr

String substr(ulong start, ulong count)

Returns a new String of up to count code points starting at code-point index start. Both arguments count code points, not bytes.

count is clamped to the code points that remain. When start is at or past length(), the result is an empty String. Runs in O(size()).

Parameters

  • start — the code-point index of the first code point to copy
  • count — the maximum number of code points to copy

Returns

  • a new String that holds the selected code points

Example

    var s = "TEST";
    assert(s.substr(1, 2) == "ES", "middle");
    assert(s.substr(0, 99) == "TEST", "count is clamped");
    assert(s.substr(4, 5) == "", "start past the end");

operator !=

bool !=(String rhs)

Returns whether this String and rhs differ in content, the negation of ==. Runs in O(size()).

Parameters

  • rhs — the String to compare with

Returns

  • true when the contents differ

operator +

String +(String rhs)

Returns a new String that holds this String followed by rhs. Neither operand is modified. Runs in O(size() + rhs.size()).

Parameters

  • rhs — the String to append to the copy

Returns

  • a new concatenated String

Example

    var greeting = "Hello, " + "world";

StringIterator

struct StringIterator

An Iterator<String> over a String’s code points, returned by String.iterate() and used by for (var c in s).

Call next() to advance, then current() for the code point it moved to. Both methods panic if the String has been modified since the iterator was created. Create it with String.iterate(): a default-constructed StringIterator() has no String to walk.

Example

    var it = "ab".iterate();
    while (it.next()) {
        print(it.current());   # "a", then "b"
    }

Methods

next

bool next()

Advances to the next code point. Runs in O(1) per code point.

Returns false once it passes the end, and on every call after that. A malformed UTF-8 sequence is yielded, together with the rest of the bytes, as one final “code point”.

Returns

  • true when positioned on a code point, false at the end

Panics

  • if the String was modified since the iterator was created

current

String current()

Returns the code point that the last successful next() moved to, as a new one-code-point String.

Returns

  • a new String that holds the current code point

Panics

  • if the String was modified since the iterator was created
  • if next() has not been called yet
  • if next() has returned false (the iterator is past the end)

Array

Source: runtime/array.pep

Array

struct Array<T>

A growable, contiguous, zero-indexed sequence of T values.

Elements live in one heap buffer that doubles when full (starting at 4 slots), so append is amortized O(1) and indexing is O(1). When T is a struct type, each slot holds a counted reference, exactly like a std::vector<std::shared_ptr<T>>: storing an element acquires it, overwriting or removing one releases the old occupant, and destroying the Array releases every element it still holds. Moving elements within the Array (insert, remove_at, swap, reverse) does not change their reference counts.

Array is itself a struct, so assigning it or passing it to a function shares the same Array rather than copying its elements; use clone() for an independent copy.

An Array is Iterable<T>: for (var x in a) visits each element in index order. Every mutating operation (an element store through [], append, insert, remove_at, pop, clear, a shrinking truncate, swap, and reverse of two or more elements) invalidates running iterators, and the next step of such a loop panics with “Array modified during iteration”.

Type parameters

  • T — the element type

See also

  • ArrayIterator

Example

    main(): int {
        var a = Array<int>();
        a.append(1);
        a.append(2);
        a[0] = 5;
        var total = 0;
        for (var x in a) {
            total = total + x;
        }
        return total;   # 7
    }

Constructor

Array()

construct(ulong capacity = 0)

Creates an empty Array, optionally preallocating room for capacity elements.

With the default capacity of 0, nothing is allocated until the first element is added. Preallocating avoids the reallocations append would otherwise perform while the Array grows to a known size.

Parameters

  • capacity — the number of element slots to allocate up front; count() is still 0

Example

    var a = Array<int>(16);   # a.count() == 0, a.capacity() == 16

Methods

iterate

ArrayIterator<T> iterate()

Returns an iterator over the elements in index order; this is what for (var x in array) calls (the Iterable<T> protocol).

The iterator holds a reference to the Array, so iterating a temporary keeps it alive for the whole loop. Modifying the Array while the iterator is in use makes the iterator’s next next() or current() panic.

Returns

  • a new iterator positioned before the first element

Example

    for (var x in a) {
        total = total + x;
    }

count

ulong count()

Returns the number of elements in the Array.

Returns

  • the element count; 0 for an empty Array

capacity

ulong capacity()

Returns the number of elements the Array can hold before its buffer must be reallocated.

Returns

  • the allocated slot count, which is at least count()

empty

bool empty()

Reports whether the Array has no elements.

Returns

  • true when count() is 0

reserve

void reserve(ulong capacity)

Ensures the buffer has room for at least capacity elements, reallocating it when it is smaller.

Never shrinks the buffer: a capacity at or below the current one does nothing. Elements, their order, and their reference counts are unchanged, and running iterators stay valid. Growing copies the existing elements, which costs O(count).

Parameters

  • capacity — the minimum number of element slots required

Example

    var a = Array<int>();
    a.reserve(8);   # a.capacity() >= 8
    a.reserve(2);   # no effect: capacity never shrinks

grow

void grow()

Makes room for one more element: when the buffer is full, doubles its capacity (to at least 4); otherwise does nothing.

append and insert call this themselves; callers rarely need it. Does not change the elements or invalidate iterators.

operator []

T [](ulong idx)

Returns the element at idx; this is the indexer read a[idx].

Runs in O(1). For a struct T the result is the stored reference itself; binding it to a variable acquires it like any shared copy.

Parameters

  • idx — the zero-based position to read

Returns

  • the element at idx

Panics

  • if idx < count() does not hold (“Array index read out of bounds”)

Example

    var first = a[0];

operator []

T [](ulong idx, T value)

Replaces the element at idx with value; this is the indexer write a[idx] = value.

For a struct T the slot acquires value and releases its previous occupant; assigning an element to its own slot (a[i] = a[i]) leaves counts unchanged. Only replaces an existing element: use append to add one. Invalidates running iterators, even when the value is unchanged.

Parameters

  • idx — the zero-based position to overwrite
  • value — the new element

Returns

  • the element now stored at idx (that is, value)

Panics

  • if idx < count() does not hold (“Array index write out of bounds”)

Example

    a[0] = 42;

append

void append(T item)

Adds item to the end of the Array.

Amortized O(1): when the buffer is full it doubles (O(count) for that call). For a struct T the Array acquires a reference to item. Invalidates running iterators.

Parameters

  • item — the element to add

Example

    var a = Array<int>();
    a.append(1);
    a.append(2);   # a holds [1, 2]

insert

void insert(ulong idx, T item)

Inserts item at position idx, shifting the element there and every later element up by one.

Runs in O(count - idx), plus a reallocation when the buffer is full. idx == count() appends. For a struct T the Array acquires a reference to item; shifted elements keep their reference counts. Invalidates running iterators.

Parameters

  • idx — the position item will occupy; from 0 through count()
  • item — the element to insert

Panics

  • if idx <= count() does not hold (“Array insert index out of bounds”)

Example

    var a = Array<int>();
    a.append(1);
    a.append(3);
    a.insert(1, 2);   # a holds [1, 2, 3]

remove_at

T remove_at(ulong idx)

Removes and returns the element at idx, shifting every later element down by one.
