Runs in O(count - idx). For a struct T the Array’s reference is transferred to the caller, so the element stays alive as long as the result is held; shifted elements keep their reference counts. The capacity is unchanged. Invalidates running iterators.

Parameters

  • idx — the position of the element to remove

Returns

  • the removed element

Panics

  • if idx < count() does not hold (“Array remove index out of bounds”)

Example

    var a = Array<int>();
    a.append(1);
    a.append(2);
    var x = a.remove_at(0);   # x == 1; a holds [2]

pop

T pop()

Removes and returns the last element.

Runs in O(1). For a struct T the Array’s reference is transferred to the caller. The capacity is unchanged. Invalidates running iterators.

Returns

  • the element that was last

Panics

  • if the Array is empty (“Array pop on empty Array”)

Example

    var top = a.pop();

first

T first()

Returns the first element without removing it; the same as a[0].

Returns

  • the element at index 0

Panics

  • if the Array is empty (“Array first on empty Array”)

last

T last()

Returns the last element without removing it; the same as a[a.count() - 1].

Returns

  • the element at index count() - 1

Panics

  • if the Array is empty (“Array last on empty Array”)

clear

void clear()

Removes every element, leaving the Array empty.

Runs in O(count). For a struct T each element’s reference is released. The capacity is kept, so refilling the Array does not reallocate. Always invalidates running iterators, even on an empty Array.

truncate

void truncate(ulong count)

Shortens the Array to its first count elements, removing the rest.

Runs in O(removed elements). For a struct T each removed element’s reference is released. A count at or above the current count() does nothing (the Array never grows) and leaves iterators valid; an actual shrink invalidates them. The capacity is kept.

Parameters

  • count — the number of leading elements to keep

Example

    a.truncate(1);   # keeps only a[0]
    a.truncate(5);   # no effect when a has fewer than 5 elements

swap

void swap(ulong a, ulong b)

Exchanges the elements at positions a and b.

Runs in O(1). Reference counts are unchanged: the two references just trade slots. Swapping a position with itself is allowed and changes no element, but still invalidates running iterators like every swap.

Parameters

  • a — the position of the first element
  • b — the position of the second element

Panics

  • if a < count() does not hold (“Array swap index out of bounds”)
  • if b < count() does not hold (“Array swap index out of bounds”)

reverse

void reverse()

Reverses the order of the elements in place.

Runs in O(count) using swap, so reference counts are unchanged. An Array of fewer than two elements is left untouched and its iterators stay valid; otherwise running iterators are invalidated.

Example

    a.reverse();   # [1, 2, 3] becomes [3, 2, 1]

find

long find(T item)

Returns the position of the first element equal to item.

Runs in O(count), scanning from the front. Elements are compared with ==: when T declares an == operator (as String does) that operator decides; for any other struct T it compares references, so only the very same object matches.

Parameters

  • item — the value to look for

Returns

  • the index of the first match, or -1 when no element matches

Example

    var i = a.find(9);   # -1 if a holds no 9

contains

bool contains(T item)

Reports whether any element equals item, compared as find does.

Runs in O(count).

Parameters

  • item — the value to look for

Returns

  • true when find(item) finds a match

append_all

void append_all(Array<T> rhs)

Appends every element of rhs, in order, to the end of this Array.

Runs in O(count of rhs), reserving the needed capacity once up front. rhs is unchanged, and it may be this same Array (a.append_all(a) doubles a). For a struct T each appended element is acquired once more, since both Arrays now hold it. Invalidates this Array’s running iterators when rhs is not empty.

Parameters

  • rhs — the Array whose elements are copied

See also

  • +=

clone

Array<T> clone()

Returns a new Array holding the same elements in the same order.

Runs in O(count). The copy is shallow: for a struct T both Arrays refer to the same element objects (each acquired once more), but adding or removing elements in one does not affect the other. The new Array’s capacity equals this Array’s count().

Returns

  • an independent Array with the same elements

Example

    var b = a.clone();
    b.append(7);   # a is unchanged

copy

Array<T> copy(Copies copies)

Returns a deep copy: a new Array holding a copy of each element, made with copies.copy. This is how copy(value) copies an Array (the Copyable<Array<T>> implementation); call copy(array) rather than this method.

Unlike clone(), which shares struct elements, the result shares no element that is not thread-safe with this Array. An element that appears twice is copied once. Runs in O(count) plus the cost of copying the elements.

Parameters

  • copies — the objects copied so far

Returns

  • the copy

Example

    var names = Array<String>();
    names.append("ada");
    var copied = copy(names);   # calls this method

operator ==

bool ==(Array<T> rhs)

Reports whether both Arrays have the same count and pairwise equal elements, compared with == as find does.

Runs in O(count); stops at the first difference. Two empty Arrays are equal. capacity() is not compared.

Parameters

  • rhs — the Array to compare with

Returns

  • true when the Arrays are element-wise equal

operator !=

bool !=(Array<T> rhs)

Reports whether the Arrays differ in count or in any element; the negation of ==.

Parameters

  • rhs — the Array to compare with

Returns

  • true when the Arrays are not element-wise equal

operator +=

Array<T> +=(Array<T> rhs)

Appends every element of rhs to this Array in place (a += b); the same as append_all.

a += a is allowed and doubles a.

Parameters

  • rhs — the Array whose elements are appended

Returns

  • this Array, after the append

See also

  • append_all

Example

    a += b;   # a now ends with b's elements

operator +

Array<T> +(Array<T> rhs)

Returns a new Array holding this Array’s elements followed by those of rhs (a + b).

Runs in O(count + count of rhs). Neither operand is modified; like clone, the result shares struct elements with its operands.

Parameters

  • rhs — the Array whose elements come second

Returns

  • the concatenation of this Array and rhs

Example

    var c = a + b;

ArrayIterator

struct ArrayIterator<T>

An Iterator<T> over the elements of an Array, in index order; returned by Array.iterate() and driven by for (var x in array).

Holds a reference to the Array, so iterating a temporary keeps it alive for the whole loop. The iterator is fail-fast: if the Array is modified after the iterator was created (see Array for which operations count), its next next() or current() panics instead of skipping or repeating elements.

Type parameters

  • T — the element type of the source Array

See also

  • Array.iterate

Example

    var it = a.iterate();
    while (it.next()) {
        var x = it.current();
    }

Methods

next

bool next()

Advances to the next element.

The first call moves to index 0. Once it returns false it keeps returning false.

Returns

  • true when there is a current element, false once past the end

Panics

  • if the Array was modified since the iterator was created (“Array modified during iteration”)

current

T current()

Returns the element the last successful next() moved to.

Returns

  • the current element

Panics

  • if the Array was modified since the iterator was created (“Array modified during iteration”)
  • if next() was never called (“ArrayIterator.current() before next()”)
  • if next() already returned false (“ArrayIterator.current() past the end”)

HashMap and HashSet

Source: runtime/hash.pep

hash

hash<T>(T value): u64

Returns a 64-bit FNV-1a hash of value, the hash HashMap and HashSet use for their keys.

  • POD types (integers, floats, pointers, bool) fold the value’s own bytes, so equal values hash equally.
  • Struct types hash by identity: the object’s address, folded through FNV-1a so that aligned addresses spread across buckets. Two distinct objects with equal fields therefore hash differently and are distinct keys. A null reference hashes to the FNV-1a offset basis.
  • String hashes by content through the concrete hash<String> override, so equal strings at different addresses collide.

The result is deterministic within and across runs of a program for POD values and strings; for other struct types it depends on where the object was allocated. Reading the address does no refcount work, and a temporary argument is released afterwards.

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the value to hash

Parameters

  • value — the value to hash

Returns

  • the 64-bit hash of value

See also

  • hash<String>

Example

    var a = hash<int>(5);
    var b = hash<int>(5);   # a == b

hash

hash<String>(String value): u64

Returns a 64-bit FNV-1a hash of a String’s bytes, so equal strings hash equally wherever they are stored. Keeps equal String keys of a HashMap or HashSet in one bucket.

Hashes the size() bytes of the String (not the NUL terminator) in O(size). An empty String hashes to the FNV-1a offset basis.

Parameters

  • value — the String to hash; it must not be a null reference

Returns

  • the 64-bit hash of the String’s content

Example

    assert(hash<String>("hello") == hash<String>("hello"), "content hash");

HashMap

struct HashMap<K, V>

A hash table that maps keys of type K to values of type V.

Keys are hashed with hash<K> and compared with ==: POD keys by value, String keys by content, and other struct keys by identity (two distinct objects with equal fields are different keys). Any type can be a key.

The map holds one reference to each stored key and value. It acquires them when they are stored and releases them when they are overwritten, removed, cleared, or when the map is destroyed.

Lookups, inserts, and removals take expected O(1) time. The table doubles before an insert of a new key would fill half of it (live entries plus removed-slot tombstones), so it never shrinks, and churn (many inserts and removals of different keys) can keep growing it even while count() stays small.

There is no iterator, and entries have no defined order.

Type parameters

  • K — the key type; hashed with hash<K> and compared with ==
  • V — the value type

Example

    var ages = HashMap<String, int>();
    ages["ada"] = 36;
    ages.insert("alan", 41);
    assert(ages["ada"] == 36, "lookup");
    assert(ages.get("grace") == 0, "a missing key reads as the default");
    assert(ages.remove("alan"), "removed");
    assert(ages.count() == 1, "one entry left");

Constructor

HashMap()

construct(ulong initial_capacity = 8)

Creates an empty map whose table has room for at least initial_capacity slots.

The slot count is rounded up to a power of two, with a minimum of 8. The map holds up to half its slot count before the first grow, so pass about twice the expected number of entries to avoid regrowing. Allocates three arrays of that many slots.

Parameters

  • initial_capacity — the minimum number of slots in the table

Example

    var m = HashMap<int, int>(64);   # 64 slots, 32 entries before growing

Methods

grow

void grow()

Doubles the table and reinserts every entry, dropping all tombstones.

insert calls it automatically. Calling it directly is safe but only costs memory. Runs in O(capacity). It replaces the table’s arrays and moves entries to new slots. Reference counts are unchanged overall: each entry is acquired by the new arrays and released by the old ones.

count

int count()

Returns the number of entries in the map. O(1).

Returns

  • the number of live key-value pairs

empty

bool empty()

Returns whether the map has no entries. O(1).

Returns

  • true when count() is 0

capacity

ulong capacity()

Returns the number of slots in the table, a power of two and at least 8. The map grows before its entries and tombstones reach half of it. O(1).

Returns

  • the current slot count

cap

[Deprecated("use 'capacity' instead", "capacity")] ulong cap()

Deprecated: use capacity instead. Returns the number of slots in the table.

Returns

  • the same as capacity()

clear

void clear()

Removes every entry and releases the map’s references to their keys and values. The table keeps its capacity. Runs in O(capacity).

copy

HashMap<K, V> copy(Copies copies)

Returns a deep copy: a new map holding a copy of each key and value, made with copies.copy. This is how copy(value) copies a HashMap (the Copyable<HashMap<K, V>> implementation); call copy(map) rather than this method.

The copied keys are hashed again, so a map keyed by struct identity finds its entries under the copied keys. Runs in O(capacity) plus the cost of copying the entries.

Parameters

  • copies — the objects copied so far

Returns

  • the copy

contains

bool contains(K key)

Returns whether the map has an entry for key. Expected O(1).

Parameters

  • key — the key to look up

Returns

  • true when key is present

insert

bool insert(K key, V value)

Maps key to value, adding a new entry or overwriting the value of an existing one.

When the key is already present, only the value is replaced: the map keeps the key object it already stores and releases the old value. A new entry acquires both key and value, and may first grow the table (see grow). Expected O(1); a grow costs O(capacity).

Parameters

  • key — the key to add or update
  • value — the value to store for key

Returns

  • true when a new key was added, false when an existing key’s value was overwritten

Example

    var m = HashMap<int, int>();
    m.insert(1, 10);   # true: added
    m.insert(1, 20);   # false: overwritten; m.get(1) == 20

get

V get(K key)

Returns the value stored for key, or the default of V when key is absent.

The default is 0 for numbers and a null reference for struct types, including String. A missing key is not an error and is not inserted. When V is a struct type, call contains first rather than using a null result. The caller gets its own reference to the stored value. Expected O(1).

Parameters

  • key — the key to look up

Returns

  • the value for key, or the default of V when absent

remove

bool remove(K key)

Removes the entry for key, releasing the map’s references to its key and value. Does nothing when key is absent.

The slot becomes a tombstone: occupied for probing, empty for lookup. It counts toward the grow threshold until an insert reuses it or the table grows. Expected O(1).

Parameters

  • key — the key to remove

Returns

  • true when key was present and removed, false when absent

operator []

V [](K key)

Reads m[key]: returns the value for key, or the default of V (0, or a null reference for struct types) when key is absent. The same as get. A missing key is not inserted.

Parameters

  • key — the key to look up

Returns

  • the value for key, or the default of V when absent

Example

    var m = HashMap<String, int>();
    var n = m["missing"];   # 0; m is still empty

operator []

V [](K key, V value)

Writes m[key] = value: inserts key or overwrites its value, the same as insert.

Parameters

  • key — the key to add or update
  • value — the value to store for key

Returns

  • value, the value of the assignment expression

Example

    var m = HashMap<String, int>();
    m["hello"] = 5;

HashSet

struct HashSet<K>

A hash set of distinct elements of type K, built on the same table as HashMap but without values.

Elements are hashed with hash<K> and compared with ==: POD elements by value, String elements by content, and other struct elements by identity (two distinct objects with equal fields are both members). The set holds one reference to each member. It acquires the reference on insert and releases it on remove, on clear, or when the set is destroyed.

Membership tests, inserts, and removals take expected O(1) time. The table doubles before an insert of a new element would fill half of it (members plus tombstones), and it never shrinks. There is no iterator, and members have no defined order. The lowercase fields and the _find_slot and grow methods are the table’s internal state: they are public but may change, and writing to them breaks the set.

Type parameters

  • K — the element type; hashed with hash<K> and compared with ==

Example

    var seen = HashSet<String>();
    seen.insert("a");
    seen["b"] = "b";   # the same as seen.insert("b")
    assert(seen.contains("a"), "member");
    assert(!seen.insert("a"), "already a member");
    assert(seen.count() == 2, "two members");

Constructor

HashSet()

construct(ulong initial_capacity = 8)

Creates an empty set whose table has room for at least initial_capacity slots.

The slot count is rounded up to a power of two, with a minimum of 8. The set holds up to half its slot count before the first grow.

Parameters

  • initial_capacity — the minimum number of slots in the table

Methods

grow

void grow()

Doubles the table and reinserts every member, dropping all tombstones.

insert calls it automatically. Calling it directly is safe but only costs memory. Runs in O(capacity). It replaces the table’s arrays and moves members to new slots.

count

int count()

Returns the number of members in the set. O(1).

Returns

  • the number of members

empty

bool empty()

Returns whether the set has no members. O(1).

Returns

  • true when count() is 0

capacity

ulong capacity()

Returns the number of slots in the table, a power of two and at least 8. O(1).

Returns

  • the current slot count

cap

[Deprecated("use 'capacity' instead", "capacity")] ulong cap()

Deprecated: use capacity instead. Returns the number of slots in the table.

Returns

  • the same as capacity()

clear

void clear()

Removes every member and releases the set’s references to them. The table keeps its capacity. Runs in O(capacity).

contains

bool contains(K key)

Returns whether key is a member of the set. Expected O(1).

Parameters

  • key — the element to look up

Returns

  • true when key is a member

insert

bool insert(K key)

Adds key to the set if it is not already a member.

A new member is acquired, and the insert may first grow the table (see grow). When an equal member already exists, the set keeps the member it has and does not store key. Expected O(1); a grow costs O(capacity).

Parameters

  • key — the element to add

Returns

  • true when key was added, false when it was already a member

remove

bool remove(K key)

Removes key from the set, releasing the set’s reference to the member. Does nothing when key is not a member. The slot becomes a tombstone (see _tombstones). Expected O(1).

Parameters

  • key — the element to remove

Returns

  • true when key was a member and was removed, false otherwise

operator []

K [](K key)

Reads s[key]: returns the stored member equal to key, or the default of K (0, or a null reference for struct types) when key is not a member.

For String elements this returns the instance the set stores, which may be a different object than key with the same content. A missing element is not inserted.

Parameters

  • key — the element to look up

Returns

  • the stored member equal to key, or the default of K

operator []

K [](K key, K value)

Writes s[key] = value: makes key a member, the same as insert(key).

A set stores only keys, so value is ignored.

Parameters

  • key — the element to add
  • value — ignored

Returns

  • key (the argument, not an existing equal member), the value of the assignment expression

copy

HashSet<K> copy(Copies copies)

Returns a deep copy: a new set holding a copy of each element, made with copies.copy. This is how copy(value) copies a HashSet (the Copyable<HashSet<K>> implementation); call copy(set) rather than this method.

The copied elements are hashed again, so a set of structs compared by identity finds the copied elements. Runs in O(capacity) plus the cost of copying the elements.

Parameters

  • copies — the objects copied so far

Returns

  • the copy

Iterators

Source: runtime/iterator.pep

Iterator

trait Iterator<T>

Something a for loop can step through, one element at a time.

for (var x in source) drives a source that declares next() and current() directly: it calls next() before each pass and binds current() to the loop variable while next() returns true. A type whose for loop should go through a separate iterator implements Iterable<T> instead. A type with neither protocol is a compile error in a for loop.

The loop goes by the methods a type has, so declaring the trait is optional. Declaring it (extend Foo with Iterator<int> {}) checks that the methods match and lets the type be used as an Iterator<int> value, which a for loop drives through the trait.

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the elements produced

See also

  • Iterable

Example

    struct Countdown {
        int remaining;
        int now;

        construct(this.remaining = 0) {}

        bool next() {
            if (remaining == 0) {
                return false;
            }
            now = remaining;
            remaining = remaining - 1;
            return true;
        }

        int current() {
            return now;
        }
    }

    extend Countdown with Iterator<int> {}

    main(): int {
        var total = 0;
        for (int n in Countdown(3)) {
            total = total + n;   # visits 3, 2, 1
        }
        return total;
    }

Methods

next

bool next()

Advances to the next element.

The first call moves to the first element, if any. Once it returns false it must keep returning false.

Returns

  • true if there is a current element, false when the elements are exhausted

current

T current()

Returns the element the last successful next() moved to.

Calling it before the first next(), or after next() returned false, is an error; the runtime’s iterators panic in that case.

Returns

  • the current element

map

map<U>(transform: (T) => U): MapIterator<T, U>

Returns an iterator over transform applied to each element, in order.

Lazy: transform runs once per element, as the result is advanced, and the result advances this iterator. Every type that declares Iterator<T> gets this method.

Type parameters

  • U — the type transform returns

Parameters

  • transform — the function applied to each element

Returns

  • a MapIterator<T, U> over the transformed elements

Example

    var names = Array<String>();
    for (var size in names.iterate().map((name) => name.size())) {
        print(size);
    }

filter

filter(keep: (T) => bool): FilterIterator<T>

Returns an iterator over the elements for which keep returns true, in order.

Lazy: keep runs once per element, as the result is advanced, and the result advances this iterator. Every type that declares Iterator<T> gets this method.

Parameters

  • keep — the test an element must pass

Returns

  • a FilterIterator<T> over the elements that pass

Example

    var nums = Array<int>();
    for (var even in nums.iterate().filter((n) => n % 2 == 0)) {
        print(even);
    }

MapIterator

struct MapIterator<T, U>

The iterator Iterator.map returns: each element of the source iterator, passed through the transform function.

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the source’s elements
  • U — the type of the elements produced

See also

  • Iterator.map

Constructor

MapIterator()

construct(Iterator<T> source, (T) => U transform)

Makes an iterator over transform applied to the elements of source.

Parameters

  • source — the iterator to transform
  • transform — the function applied to each element

Methods

next

bool next()

Advances to the next element, and applies the transform function to it.

Returns

  • true if there is a current element, false when the source is exhausted

current

U current()

Returns the transformed element the last successful next() produced.

Returns

  • the current element

FilterIterator

struct FilterIterator<T>

The iterator Iterator.filter returns: the elements of the source iterator that the test function accepts.

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the elements

See also

  • Iterator.filter

Constructor

FilterIterator()

construct(Iterator<T> source, (T) => bool keep)

Makes an iterator over the elements of source that keep accepts.

Parameters

  • source — the iterator to filter
  • keep — the test an element must pass

Methods

next

bool next()

Advances to the next element that the test function accepts.

Returns

  • true if there is a current element, false when the source is exhausted

current

T current()

Returns the element the last successful next() moved to.

Returns

  • the current element

Iterable

trait Iterable<T>

Something that hands out an Iterator<T> over its elements, such as Array<T> and String (which yields one code point at a time, as a String).

for (var x in source) calls source.iterate() once, then drives the returned iterator with next() and current(). When a type declares both iterate() and next()/current(), the loop uses iterate().

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the elements produced

See also

  • Iterator

Example

    var nums = Array<int>();
    nums.append(1);
    nums.append(2);
    var total = 0;
    for (var x in nums) {
        total = total + x;
    }

Methods

iterate

iterate(): Iterator<T>

Returns a new iterator positioned before the first element.

The result may be any type that satisfies Iterator<T> (for example ArrayIterator<T>); each call starts a fresh, independent pass.

Returns

  • an iterator over the elements

Scoped

The scope protocol behind with (var x = scope) { ... } else { ... }.

A with block holds scope in a hidden local, binds x to scope.enter(), and runs the body. When enter() returns a struct reference that is null, the body is skipped and the optional else block runs instead. Both locals are released on every path out of the block (falling off the end, return, break, continue), the binding first. Cleanup is the scope object’s destructor, which calls exit().

Source: runtime/scoped.pep

Scoped

trait Scoped<T>

Something a with block can enter.

enter() runs once when the block is entered, and its result is what the header binds. exit() is the cleanup: implementations call it from their destructor (destruct { this.exit(); }), so it runs when the scope object’s last reference is released. For a scope object created in the with header that is the end of the block; for one that is also held elsewhere, it is when that last owner lets go.

A with block with no binding (with (scope) { ... }) still holds the result of enter() until the block ends. An enter() that returns void has nothing to bind.

Type parameters

  • T — the type enter() returns

Example

    var open = 0;

    struct Session {
        construct() {
            open = open + 1;
        }

        destruct {
            this.exit();
        }

        Session enter() {
            return this;
        }

        exit() {
            open = open - 1;
        }
    }

    extend Session with Scoped<Session> {}

    main(): int {
        with (var session = Session()) {
            # open == 1 here
        }
        return open;   # 0: the session was destroyed at the end of the block
    }

Methods

enter

T enter()

Enters the scope.

A null struct reference means there is nothing to use: the with body is skipped and its else block runs instead.

Returns

  • the value the with header binds

exit

exit()

Leaves the scope. Implementations call it from their destructor.

Weak

Weak references: Weak<T> points at an object without keeping it alive.

Source: runtime/weak.pep

Weak

struct Weak<T>

A reference to a struct object that does not keep it alive, for back pointers (a child’s parent, an observer’s subject) that would otherwise form a reference cycle and leak.

The object is reached only through a with block, which upgrades the weak reference to an ordinary strong one for the length of the block. Once the object has been destroyed the upgrade fails: the body is skipped and the else block runs instead.

A Weak<T> never changes what it points at, so copies of it can be shared freely.

Type parameters

  • T — the struct type pointed at

See also

  • Scoped

Example

    struct Child {
        Weak<Parent> owner;

        construct(Parent owner) {
            this.owner = Weak<Parent>(owner);
        }

        String owner_name() {
            with (var parent = this.owner) {
                return parent.name;
            } else {
                return "(gone)";
            }
            return "";
        }
    }

Constructor

Weak()

construct(T target)

Creates a weak reference to target’s object.

Parameters

  • target — the object to point at; a null reference makes a weak reference that is always expired

Methods

expired

bool expired()

Reports whether the object has been destroyed.

A false result can be out of date by the time it is used; enter the reference with with to use the object.

Returns

  • true once the object has been destroyed, or if the reference was made from null

enter

T enter()

Upgrades the weak reference for a with block.

Returns

  • a new strong reference to the object, or null once it has been destroyed

exit

void exit()

Leaves the scope. The strong reference enter() returned is released by the with block itself, so there is nothing to do.

Result

Source: runtime/result.pep

Error

struct Error

The default error type of Result: a human-readable message.

Example

    var r = Result<int>.err(Error("boom"));
    var n = r.error().message.length();   # 4

Fields

message

String message

The description of what went wrong; empty when none was given.

Constructor

Error()

[Implicit] construct(String message = "")

Creates an Error carrying message.

The constructor is [Implicit]: a String converts to an Error wherever an Error is expected, as in Result<int>.err("bad input").

Parameters

  • message — the description of the failure; defaults to the empty string

Result

struct Result<TValue, TError=Error>

The outcome of an operation that can fail: either Ok, holding a TValue, or Err, holding a TError, like Rust’s Result<T, E>.

Build one with the static ok or err functions and test it with is_ok() or is_err(), then read the outcome with value() or error(), each of which panics on the wrong kind of result. TError defaults to Error, so Result<int> is Result<int, Error>.

Result<void> is the result of an operation that returns nothing on success, like Rust’s Result<(), E>. A void type argument erases what it types: that instance holds no value, ok() takes no argument, and value() returns nothing, so it only checks that the result is Ok.

Type parameters

  • TValue — the type of the value an Ok result carries; void when there is none
  • TError — the type of the error an Err result carries; defaults to Error

Example

    main(): int {
        var a = Result<int>.ok(42);
        if (a.value() != 42) { return 1; }
        var b = Result<int>.err(Error("boom"));
        if (!b.is_err()) { return 2; }
        return b.ok_or(0);   # 0
    }

Constructor

Result()

construct()

Creates an Err result whose error and value both hold their type’s default (zero for a POD type, null for a struct type).

Prefer ok or err, which set the live field.

Example

    var e = Result<int>();   # e.is_ok() == false

Static methods

ok

static Result<TValue, TError> ok(TValue v)

Returns an Ok result holding v.

For a struct TValue the result acquires a reference to v.

For Result<void>, ok() takes no argument.

Parameters

  • v — the success value

Returns

  • an Ok result whose value() is v

Example

    var r = Result<int>.ok(42);
    var done = Result<void>.ok();

err

static Result<TValue, TError> err(TError e)

Returns an Err result holding e.

For a struct TError the result acquires a reference to e.

Parameters

  • e — the failure value

Returns

  • an Err result whose error() is e

Example

    var r = Result<int>.err(Error("boom"));

Methods

is_ok

bool is_ok()

Reports whether this result is Ok.

Returns

  • true when the result holds a value

is_err

bool is_err()

Reports whether this result is Err.

Returns

  • true when the result holds an error

value

TValue value()

Returns the success value, panicking if this result is Err.

For a struct TValue the caller gets its own reference; the result keeps its reference too.

For Result<void> it returns nothing: calling it only checks that the result is Ok.

Returns

  • the value of an Ok result

Panics

  • if the result is Err (“Result.value on an Err value”)

Example

    var n = Result<int>.ok(42).value();   # 42

error

TError error()

Returns the failure value, panicking if this result is Ok.

For a struct TError the caller gets its own reference; the result keeps its reference too.

Returns

  • the error of an Err result

Panics

  • if the result is Ok (“Result.error on an Ok value”)

Example

    var e = Result<int>.err(Error("boom")).error();   # e.message == "boom"

ok_or

TValue ok_or(TValue fallback)

Returns the success value, or fallback if this result is Err.

Never panics. fallback is evaluated by the caller whether or not it is used.

Parameters

  • fallback — the value to return when the result is Err

Returns

  • the value when the result is Ok, otherwise fallback

Example

    var n = Result<int>.err(Error("e")).ok_or(-1);   # -1

Optional

Source: runtime/optional.pep

Optional

struct Optional<T>

A value of type T that may be absent: either Some, holding a T, or None, holding nothing, like Rust’s Option<T> and Swift’s Optional.

Use it instead of a null reference or a sentinel (-1, "") wherever a value may be missing: the type says so, and reading the value goes through value(), which checks, or value_or(), which cannot fail. It works for every type, including those that have no null, such as int.

T? is shorthand for Optional<T>, and a variable or field of an optional type that is not given a value starts as None.

Build one with the static some or none functions, or Optional<T>() for None, and test it with is_some() or is_none().

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the value a Some optional holds

Example

    find_even(values: Array<int>): int? {
        for (var v in values) {
            if (v % 2 == 0) { return Optional<int>.some(v); }
        }
        return Optional<int>.none();
    }

    main(): int {
        var values = Array<int>();
        values.append(3);
        values.append(4);
        var found = find_even(values);
        if (found.is_none()) { return 1; }
        return found.value() - 4;   # 0
    }

Constructor

Optional()

construct()

Creates a None optional, one holding no value.

Example

    var nothing = Optional<int>();   # nothing.is_none() == true

Static methods

some

static some(value: T): Optional<T>

Returns a Some optional holding value.

For a struct T the optional acquires a reference to value.

Parameters

  • value — the value to hold

Returns

  • a Some optional whose value is value

Example

    var answer = Optional<int>.some(42);

none

static none(): Optional<T>

Returns a None optional, one holding no value; the same as Optional<T>().

Returns

  • a None optional

Example

    var nothing = Optional<String>.none();

Methods

is_some

is_some(): bool

Reports whether this optional holds a value.

Returns

  • true when the optional is Some

is_none

is_none(): bool

Reports whether this optional holds no value.

Returns

  • true when the optional is None

value

value(): T

Returns the value, panicking if this optional is None.

For a struct T the caller gets its own reference; the optional keeps its reference too.

Returns

  • the value of a Some optional

Panics

  • if the optional is None (“Optional.value on a None value”)

Example

    var n = Optional<int>.some(42).value();   # 42

value_or

value_or(fallback: T): T

Returns the value, or fallback if this optional is None.

Never panics. fallback is evaluated by the caller whether or not it is used.

Parameters

  • fallback — the value to return when the optional is None

Returns

  • the value when the optional is Some, otherwise fallback

Example

    var n = Optional<int>.none().value_or(-1);   # -1

Atomic

Source: runtime/atomic.pep

Atomic

[Shared] struct Atomic<T>

A value of type T that threads can read and update without data races, like C++ std::atomic<T>.

T may be a numeric type, a pointer, or a struct type. For a struct T the Atomic holds a counted reference and every operation follows std::shared_ptr rules: store and a successful compare_exchange acquire the new reference and release the one they replace, and load hands the caller its own reference. Memory ordering: load is an acquire, store a release, and compare_exchange and the fetch_* operations are acquire-release.

Atomic is a [Shared] struct, so copies of it share one slot and may be used from several threads at once: pass the Atomic around (or keep it in a const global) to share the value between threads. A struct T must be thread-safe (itself [Shared]); Atomic<String> is a compile error.

Type parameters

  • T — the type of the stored value

Example

    main(): int {
        var counter = Atomic<long>(0);
        var before = counter.fetch_add(5);   # before == 0
        if (counter.load() != 5) { return 1; }
        return 0;
    }

Constructor

Atomic()

construct(T value = __default<T>())

Creates an Atomic holding value.

For a struct T the Atomic acquires a reference to value.

Parameters

  • value — the initial value; defaults to zero for a POD T and null for a struct T

Example

    var a = Atomic<int>(41);
    var p = Atomic<Point>();   # holds null until a store

Methods

load

T load()

Atomically reads the current value, with acquire ordering.

For a struct T the returned reference is acquired for the caller, so it stays valid even if another thread stores a new value afterwards.

Returns

  • the value currently stored

store

void store(T v)

Atomically replaces the stored value with v, with release ordering.

For a struct T this is a shared_ptr assignment: the slot acquires v and releases its previous occupant, and the swap is a single atomic exchange, so concurrent stores never leak or double-release.

Parameters

  • v — the new value

compare_exchange

bool compare_exchange(T expected, T desired)

Atomically replaces the stored value with desired if and only if it currently equals expected (a compare-and-swap).

Equality is bitwise: a struct T compares references (the same object, not equal contents), and a floating-point T compares bit patterns, so 0.0 and -0.0 differ. Unlike C++ compare_exchange_strong, expected is not updated on failure; call load() to retry. On success a struct slot acquires desired and releases the old occupant; on failure no reference count changes.

Parameters

  • expected — the value the slot must hold for the exchange to happen
  • desired — the value to store when it does

Returns

  • true if the slot held expected and now holds desired, false if it was left unchanged

Example

    var cur = counter.load();
    while (!counter.compare_exchange(cur, cur + 1)) {
        cur = counter.load();
    }

exchange

T exchange(T v)

Atomically replaces the stored value with v and returns the value it replaced.

Implemented as a compare_exchange retry loop, so under heavy contention it may retry several times. For a struct T the slot acquires v, and the caller owns the returned reference.

Parameters

  • v — the new value

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the exchange

Example

    var e = Atomic<int>(1);
    var old = e.exchange(2);   # old == 1, e.load() == 2

is_lock_free

bool is_lock_free()

Reports whether this Atomic’s slot fits a native atomic instruction, so its operations never need a lock or an atomic library call.

True for every POD T of 8 bytes or less and for every struct T (whose slot is one pointer). The answer depends only on T, never on the stored value.

Returns

  • true when the operations are lock-free

fetch_add

T fetch_add(T v)

Atomically adds v to the stored value and returns the value before the addition, like std::atomic<T>::fetch_add.

Integer T only. Overflow wraps around. For a non-integer T (a floating-point or struct type) the call compiles but aborts the program when it runs.

Parameters

  • v — the amount to add

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the addition

Example

    var c = Atomic<long>(10);
    var old = c.fetch_add(5);   # old == 10, c.load() == 15

fetch_sub

T fetch_sub(T v)

Atomically subtracts v from the stored value and returns the value before the subtraction, like std::atomic<T>::fetch_sub.

Integer T only. Overflow wraps around. For a non-integer T the call compiles but aborts the program when it runs.

Parameters

  • v — the amount to subtract

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the subtraction

fetch_and

T fetch_and(T v)

Atomically replaces the stored value with its bitwise AND with v and returns the value before the operation, like std::atomic<T>::fetch_and.

Integer T only. For a non-integer T the call compiles but aborts the program when it runs.

Parameters

  • v — the mask to AND with

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the operation

fetch_or

T fetch_or(T v)

Atomically replaces the stored value with its bitwise OR with v and returns the value before the operation, like std::atomic<T>::fetch_or.

Integer T only. For a non-integer T the call compiles but aborts the program when it runs.

Parameters

  • v — the bits to OR in

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the operation

fetch_xor

T fetch_xor(T v)

Atomically replaces the stored value with its bitwise XOR with v and returns the value before the operation, like std::atomic<T>::fetch_xor.

Integer T only. For a non-integer T the call compiles but aborts the program when it runs.

Parameters

  • v — the bits to XOR with

Returns

  • the value stored immediately before the operation

The io module

Files, directories, and paths, modeled on Rust’s std::fs and std::path.

Unlike the rest of the standard library, io is a module: bring it in with import io (and write io.File, io.Path) or import names directly with import [File, Path] from io. Every fallible operation returns an IoResult<T>, a Result whose error is an IoError.

  • Path: building, splitting, and inspecting paths
  • File: opening, reading, and writing files, and whole-file helpers
  • Directory: listing, creating, and removing directories
  • Errors and platform types: IoError, IoResult, and handle types

Example

    import io;

    main(): int {
        var path = io.Directory.temp().join("greeting.txt");
        assert(io.File.write_text(path, "hello").is_ok(), "write");
        assert(io.File.read_to_string(path).value() == "hello", "read back");
        io.File.remove(path);
        return 0;
    }

Source: runtime/io/module.pep

Errors and platform types

Source: runtime/io/types.pep

FileHandle

[Target(!OS(Windows))] type FileHandle = int

The raw operating-system handle of an open file: a POSIX file descriptor.

File.Handle holds one. A valid descriptor is >= 0; a File that is closed (or was never opened) holds -1. The standard streams are descriptors 0, 1 and 2. On Windows this is a HANDLE instead.

See also

  • File

Note

  • code that must build for every target should not rely on this being an int

ErrorCode

[Target(!OS(Windows))] type ErrorCode = int

The raw platform error code carried by IoError.code: a POSIX errno value such as ENOENT (2). On Windows this is a DWORD from GetLastError() instead.

The numbers differ between platforms (and a few between Linux and macOS), so portable code classifies an error with the IoError predicates (is_not_found(), …) rather than comparing codes.

See also

  • IoError

Mode

[Target(!OS(Windows))] type Mode = uint

File mode bits as reported in Metadata.Permissions: on POSIX the mode_t permission bits mode & 07777 (the rwx bits for owner, group and others plus setuid, setgid and sticky; the file-type bits are removed). On Windows the same field holds FILE_ATTRIBUTE_* bits instead.

See also

  • Metadata

FileHandle

[Target(OS(Windows))] type FileHandle = __ptr<void>

The raw operating-system handle of an open file: a Windows HANDLE.

File.Handle holds one. A File that is closed (or was never opened) holds null; neither null nor INVALID_HANDLE_VALUE (-1) is a valid handle. The standard streams are the handles from GetStdHandle. On POSIX this is an int file descriptor instead.

See also

  • File

Note

  • code that must build for every target should not rely on this being a __ptr<void>

ErrorCode

[Target(OS(Windows))] type ErrorCode = uint

The raw platform error code carried by IoError.code: a Windows DWORD error code from GetLastError(), such as ERROR_FILE_NOT_FOUND (2). On POSIX this is an errno value instead.

The numbers differ between platforms, so portable code classifies an error with the IoError predicates (is_not_found(), …) rather than comparing codes.

See also

  • IoError

Mode

[Target(OS(Windows))] type Mode = uint

File attribute bits as reported in Metadata.Permissions: on Windows the whole FILE_ATTRIBUTE_* word of the file (FILE_ATTRIBUTE_READONLY is 1, FILE_ATTRIBUTE_DIRECTORY 16, FILE_ATTRIBUTE_REPARSE_POINT 1024, …). On POSIX the same field holds mode_t permission bits instead.

See also

  • Metadata

Offset

type Offset = long

A byte position or distance within a file, as taken and returned by the File.seek* methods: a signed 64-bit integer on every target (off_t on POSIX, LARGE_INTEGER on Windows), so files larger than 4 GiB work everywhere.

IoError

struct IoError

An I/O failure: the raw platform error code plus a human-readable message.

There are no enums, so the kind of error is a set of predicates over the code (is_not_found(), is_permission_denied(), …), each of which knows the matching codes on the current platform. Test with those rather than comparing code against numbers. Failures that Pepper detects itself (invalid UTF-8, an empty path, a short write, a closed File) use the platform’s own code for the kind they are (EINVAL / ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER for invalid input, ENOENT / ERROR_FILE_NOT_FOUND for an empty path, …), so the predicates hold for them too. A short write uses EIO / ERROR_WRITE_FAULT and a closed File uses EBADF / ERROR_INVALID_HANDLE; no predicate matches those.

See also

  • IoResult

Example

    import io;

    var r = io.File.open(io.Path("settings.txt"));
    if (r.is_err()) {
        if (r.error().is_not_found()) {
            print("no settings file");
        } else {
            print(r.error().message);
        }
    }

Fields

code

ErrorCode code

The raw platform error code: an errno value on POSIX, a GetLastError() code on Windows; 0 means no error.

message

String message

A human-readable description: the platform’s text for code (strerror on POSIX, FormatMessageW on Windows, without its trailing period and line break), or Pepper’s own text such as "empty path" for errors it detects itself.

Constructor

IoError()

construct(ErrorCode code = 0, String message = "")

Creates an error from a platform code and a message.

The default, IoError(), has code 0 and an empty message; no predicate holds for it.

Parameters

  • code — the raw platform error code (errno on POSIX, GetLastError() on Windows)
  • message — the human-readable description

Static methods

last

static IoError last()

Captures the calling thread’s last platform error now: errno on POSIX, GetLastError() on Windows, with the platform’s message for it.

Call it immediately after the failing system call; any call in between (including one that allocates) may overwrite the code.

Returns

  • an error holding the current errno / GetLastError() code and its message

Methods

is_not_found

bool is_not_found()

Returns whether the error means the file or directory does not exist.

Matches ENOENT on POSIX, and ERROR_FILE_NOT_FOUND or ERROR_PATH_NOT_FOUND on Windows. An empty path fails with this kind on POSIX (with the message "empty path"), and in File.open_with on every platform.

Returns

  • true when a path, or a directory on the way to it, is missing

is_permission_denied

bool is_permission_denied()

Returns whether the operation was refused for lack of permission.

Matches EACCES or EPERM on POSIX, and ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED on Windows (which Windows also reports for, among others, deleting a read-only file).

Returns

  • true when access was denied

is_already_exists

bool is_already_exists()

Returns whether the operation failed because its target already exists, such as Directory.create on an existing path or opening with create_new.

Matches EEXIST on POSIX, and ERROR_FILE_EXISTS or ERROR_ALREADY_EXISTS on Windows.

Returns

  • true when the target already exists

is_not_a_directory

bool is_not_a_directory()

Returns whether a path that had to name a directory names something else, such as Directory.open or Directory.remove on a file (or, on POSIX, a file used as a directory in the middle of a path).

Matches ENOTDIR on POSIX, and ERROR_DIRECTORY (267, “the directory name is invalid”) on Windows.

Returns

  • true when a directory was expected

is_is_a_directory

bool is_is_a_directory()

Returns whether an operation that needs a file was given a directory, such as reading from a directory opened as a File.

Matches EISDIR on POSIX, and ERROR_DIRECTORY_NOT_SUPPORTED on Windows (which File reads report for a directory handle).

Returns

  • true when a directory was given where a file was expected

is_directory_not_empty

bool is_directory_not_empty()

Returns whether a directory could not be removed because it still has entries (Directory.remove on a non-empty directory).

Matches ENOTEMPTY on POSIX (39 on Linux, 66 on macOS) and ERROR_DIR_NOT_EMPTY on Windows.

Returns

  • true when the directory is not empty

is_invalid_input

bool is_invalid_input()

Returns whether an argument was invalid: bad options (such as opening with no access mode), data that is not valid UTF-8 where text was expected, or a malformed path.

Matches EINVAL on POSIX, and ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER or ERROR_INVALID_NAME on Windows.

Returns

  • true when the input was invalid

is_interrupted

bool is_interrupted()

Returns whether the operation was interrupted before it finished.

Matches EINTR on POSIX and ERROR_OPERATION_ABORTED on Windows. The io functions retry interrupted POSIX opens, reads and writes themselves, so callers rarely see this.

Returns

  • true when the operation was interrupted

IoResult

type IoResult<T> = Result<T, IoError>

The result of an io operation: a value on success, an IoError on failure. Check is_ok() / is_err() before calling value() or error(); operations that produce nothing return IoResult<void>.

Type parameters

  • T — the value an operation produces on success

See also

  • IoError

Example

    import io;

    var cwd = io.Directory.current();
    if (cwd.is_ok()) {
        print(cwd.value().to_string());
    } else {
        print(cwd.error().message);
    }

Path

Source: runtime/io/path.pep

Path

struct Path

An owned file-system path: the text of the path plus operations on it (Rust’s Path and PathBuf in one type).

Every method except exists, is_file, is_dir, metadata, symlink_metadata and canonicalize is pure text manipulation: it never touches the file system, so .. is not resolved against symlinks and nothing needs to exist. “No path” is the empty Path (there is no Option): parent() of a root, for example, is Path("").

Separators: on POSIX only / separates components (\ is an ordinary byte of a name). On Windows both / and \ separate, and a path may start with a prefix: a drive (C:) or a UNC share (\\server\share). Methods that build a path (join, normalize, with_extension) insert the platform separator (separator()), but the rest of the text is kept as written. Paths are UTF-8 Strings, scanned byte by byte; separators and dots are ASCII, so splitting at them never cuts a character in two.

Example

    import io;

    var p = io.Path("docs").join("guide.md");   # "docs/guide.md"
    var name = p.file_name();                       # "guide.md"
    var ext = p.extension();                        # "md"
    var dir = p.parent();                           # io.Path("docs")
    if (p.is_file()) {
        print(p.to_string());
    }

Constructor

Path()

[Implicit] construct(String value = "")

Creates a path holding value as is: no validation, normalization or separator conversion.

The constructor is [Implicit]: a String (or a string literal) converts to a Path wherever a Path is expected, so every API that takes a Path also accepts text.

Parameters

  • value — the path’s text; the default, "", is the empty path

Example

    import io;

    var p = io.Path("/usr/lib");
    var empty = io.Path();   # is_empty() is true
    var q: io.Path = "docs";   # converted implicitly

Static methods

separator

static String separator()

Returns the separator this platform inserts when it builds a path.

Returns

  • "/" on POSIX, "\" on Windows (where / is also accepted as a separator on input)

dot_index

static ulong dot_index(String name)

Returns the byte offset of the dot that starts the extension in the file name name: the last . that is not the first byte. A leading dot (.bashrc) starts the name, not an extension.

Parameters

  • name — a file name (one component, as file_name() returns)

Returns

  • the index of the extension’s dot, or name.size when the name has no extension (including the empty name)

Methods

to_string

String to_string()

Returns the path’s text, the same String the Path holds, not a copy.

Returns

  • the path as a String

is_empty

bool is_empty()

Returns whether the path has no text at all. The empty path means “no path”: it is what parent() returns for a root or a bare name, and file-system operations on it fail.

Returns

  • true when the path’s text is ""

is_absolute

bool is_absolute()

Returns whether the path is absolute, meaning it does not depend on the current directory. Pure text: the path need not exist.

On POSIX a path is absolute when it starts with /. On Windows it is absolute when it has a drive followed by a root separator (C:\x, C:/x) or is a UNC path (\\server\share\x); a root without a drive (\x) and a drive without a root (C:x) are both relative. The empty path is relative.

Returns

  • true when the path is absolute

is_relative

bool is_relative()

Returns whether the path is relative: the opposite of is_absolute(). The empty path is relative.

Returns

  • true when the path is not absolute

join

Path join(Path other)

Returns this path with other appended as further components.

A platform separator is inserted between the two unless this path already ends in one. If other is absolute, or (on Windows) starts with a root separator or a drive or UNC prefix, it replaces this path instead, as in Rust. Joining an empty other returns a copy of this path; joining onto the empty path returns other. Nothing is normalized: a joined with ../b is a/../b.

Parameters

  • other — the path to append

Returns

  • a new Path; this path is not changed

Example

    import io;

    var p = io.Path("usr").join(io.Path("lib"));   # "usr/lib" ("usr\lib" on Windows)
    var q = io.Path("a/").join(io.Path("b"));      # "a/b"
    var r = io.Path("a").join(io.Path("/abs"));    # "/abs"
    var so = io.Path("usr").join("lib").join("x.so");   # a String converts to a Path

join_str

[Deprecated("use 'join', which accepts a String", "join")] Path join_str(String other)

Returns this path with the String other appended, exactly like join(Path(other)). Deprecated: join accepts a String directly, since a String converts to a Path implicitly.

Parameters

  • other — the path text to append

Returns

  • a new Path; this path is not changed

See also

  • join

Example

    import io;

    var so = io.Path("usr").join("lib").join("x.so");   # "usr/lib/x.so"

has_root_or_prefix

bool has_root_or_prefix()

Returns whether the path starts with a root separator or, on Windows, a drive or UNC prefix. This is what makes join replace instead of append; unlike is_absolute() it is also true for Windows paths such as \x and C:x. On POSIX it equals is_absolute().

Returns

  • true when the path has a root separator or a prefix

parent

Path parent()

Returns the path without its last component. Pure text: .. is not resolved, so the parent of a/.. is a.

Trailing and repeated separators before the last component are dropped (a/b/ and a//b both give a). The parent of a top-level name keeps the root and prefix (/a gives /, C:\x gives C:\, C:x gives C:).

Returns

  • the parent path, or the empty path for a root (/, C:\), a bare name (a, ., ..), or the empty path

Example

    import io;

    var p = io.Path("/a/b/c.txt").parent();   # "/a/b"
    var root = io.Path("/a").parent();        # "/"
    var none = io.Path("/").parent();         # "" (empty)

file_name

String file_name()

Returns the last component of the path, ignoring trailing separators (a/b/ gives b).

Returns

  • the last component, or "" for a root, the empty path, or a path whose last component is . or ..

Example

    import io;

    var name = io.Path("/a/b/c.txt").file_name();   # "c.txt"
    var none = io.Path("a/..").file_name();         # ""

file_stem

String file_stem()

Returns the file name without its extension: everything before the last dot, unless that dot is the first character.

Returns

  • the stem (c.tar for c.tar.gz, .bashrc for .bashrc, foo for foo.), or "" when there is no file name (see file_name())

extension

String extension()

Returns the file name’s extension, without its dot: the text after the last dot, unless that dot is the first character. Only the file name counts; a dot in a directory name (a.b/c) is not an extension.

Returns

  • the extension (gz for c.tar.gz), or "" when there is none (noext, .bashrc, foo.) or no file name

with_extension

Path with_extension(String ext)

Returns this path with its file name’s extension replaced by ext, added when it has none, or removed when ext is empty. Trailing separators after the file name are dropped.

Parameters

  • ext — the new extension without a leading dot (a dot is inserted; ".md" would give b..md), or "" to remove the extension

Returns

  • the new path, or a copy of this path unchanged when it has no file name (a root, ., .., or the empty path)

Example

    import io;

    var md = io.Path("a/b.txt").with_extension("md");   # "a/b.md"
    var rs = io.Path("a/b").with_extension("rs");       # "a/b.rs"
    var bare = io.Path("a/b.txt").with_extension("");   # "a/b"

components

Array<String> components()

Returns the path split into its components: the prefix (Windows drive or UNC share, such as C:), then the root separator (one character, as written), then each non-empty part between separators.

Repeated and trailing separators produce no empty parts. . parts are dropped, except a leading one in a path with no prefix or root (./a gives . and a). .. parts are kept as they are.

Returns

  • the components in order; empty for the empty path

Example

    import io;

    var c = io.Path("/usr//lib/./x/").components();   # ["/", "usr", "lib", "x"]
    var d = io.Path("./a/b").components();            # [".", "a", "b"]

normalize

Path normalize()

Returns the path cleaned up lexically, without touching the file system: . parts and repeated or trailing separators are dropped, and each .. removes the part before it.

A .. cannot climb above a root (/../x gives /x) and is kept at the start of a relative path (../a/.. gives ..). The prefix is kept, and every separator becomes the platform separator. Because symlinks are not consulted, link/.. becomes . even when link points elsewhere; use canonicalize() for the path the file system resolves.

Returns

  • the normalized path; . when the path cleans to nothing (a/.., ./), and the empty path for the empty path

See also

  • canonicalize

Example

    import io;

    var p = io.Path("a/./b/../c").normalize();   # "a/c" ("a\c" on Windows)
    var q = io.Path("a/..").normalize();         # "."

exists

bool exists()

Returns whether something exists at this path, following symlinks. A relative path is resolved against the current directory.

Any error counts as not existing: a dangling symlink, a path under a directory that cannot be read, and the empty path all give false. Use metadata() to tell those apart.

Returns

  • true when the path (or a symlink’s target) can be stat’ed

is_file

bool is_file()

Returns whether the path names a regular file, following symlinks.

Returns

  • true for a regular file or a symlink to one; false for anything else, and on any error (including a missing path)

is_directory

bool is_directory()

Returns whether the path names a directory, following symlinks.

Returns

  • true for a directory or a symlink to one; false for anything else, and on any error (including a missing path)

is_dir

[Deprecated("use 'is_directory' instead", "is_directory")] bool is_dir()

Deprecated: use is_directory instead. Returns whether the path names a directory, following symlinks.

Returns

  • the same as is_directory()

metadata

IoResult<Metadata> metadata()

Returns the metadata of the file or directory at this path, following symlinks: the result describes the target (so on POSIX is_symlink is always false). stat / statx on POSIX; on Windows the path is opened for attributes only.

Returns

  • the Metadata of the path (or of a symlink’s target); an error when the path cannot be stat’ed: is_not_found() when it (or a symlink’s target) is missing or the path is empty, is_permission_denied() when a directory on the way cannot be searched, and on POSIX is_not_a_directory() when a file is used as a directory in the middle of the path

See also

  • symlink_metadata

Example

    import io;

    var m = io.Path("data.bin").metadata();
    if (m.is_ok()) {
        var size = m.value().size;
    }

symlink_metadata

IoResult<Metadata> symlink_metadata()

Returns the metadata of the path itself without following a symlink at the end of it, so for a symlink is_symlink is true and the rest describes the link rather than its target. lstat / statx with AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW on POSIX; on Windows the path is opened with FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT, and any reparse point (junctions included) reports is_symlink, together with is_directory for a directory link. For anything that is not a symlink it equals metadata().

Returns

  • the Metadata of the path itself; an error as for metadata(), except that a dangling symlink succeeds

See also

  • metadata

canonicalize

IoResult<Path> canonicalize()

Returns the absolute path the file system resolves this path to: relative to the current directory, with every symlink, . and .. resolved. Unlike normalize(), the path must exist.

POSIX uses realpath. Windows opens the path and asks for its final name; the result uses \, and the \\?\ prefix Windows reports is removed (\\?\UNC\server\share becomes \\server\share).

Returns

  • the canonical absolute path; an error when the path cannot be resolved: is_not_found() when it (or a symlink’s target) is missing or the path is empty, is_permission_denied() when a directory on the way cannot be searched

See also

  • normalize

Example

    import io;

    var real = io.Path("a/../b.txt").canonicalize();
    if (real.is_ok()) {
        print(real.value().to_string());
    }

operator ==

bool ==(Path rhs)

Returns whether two paths have exactly the same text. The comparison is byte by byte, with no normalization and case-sensitive on every platform: a/b differs from a//b, from a/b/, and (on Windows) from a\b and A/b. Compare normalize() or canonicalize() results to ignore spelling differences.

Parameters

  • rhs — the path to compare with

Returns

  • true when both paths’ texts are equal

operator !=

bool !=(Path rhs)

Returns whether two paths differ in their text: the opposite of ==, with the same byte-by-byte comparison.

Parameters

  • rhs — the path to compare with

Returns

  • true when the paths’ texts differ

File

Source: runtime/io/file.pep

OpenOptions

struct OpenOptions

Describes how File.open_with (or OpenOptions.open) opens a file: which access it asks for, and whether the file is created or truncated.

A builder in the style of Rust’s std::fs::OpenOptions. A new OpenOptions has every option off; each setter stores its flag and returns this same OpenOptions, so calls chain. The rules, checked when the file is opened:

  • At least one of read, write or append must be set, or opening fails with is_invalid_input().
  • create, create_new and truncate need write or append, or opening fails with is_invalid_input().
  • append implies write access, and every write goes to the current end of the file whatever the position; it wins over write.
  • create opens an existing file as it is (combine with truncate to empty it) and creates a missing one.
  • create_new creates the file and fails with is_already_exists() when it already exists, atomically (O_EXCL on POSIX, CREATE_NEW on Windows); create is then irrelevant.
  • truncate empties an existing file on open.
  • Without create or create_new, a missing file fails with is_not_found().

On POSIX a created file gets mode 0666 less the process umask, and the descriptor is opened close-on-exec. On Windows every file is opened sharing read, write and delete access with other openers.

See also

  • File.open_with

Example

    var r = OpenOptions().read(true).write(true).create(true).open(Path("data.bin"));
    if (r.is_err()) {
        return 1;
    }
    var f = r.value();

Constructor

OpenOptions()

construct()

Creates an OpenOptions with every option off. Set at least one of read, write or append before opening.

Methods

read

OpenOptions read(bool v)

Sets whether the file is opened for reading.

Parameters

  • v — true to request read access

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

write

OpenOptions write(bool v)

Sets whether the file is opened for writing. Writes go to the current position and overwrite what is there.

Parameters

  • v — true to request write access

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

append

OpenOptions append(bool v)

Sets append mode: the file is opened for writing and every write goes to the current end of the file, whatever the position (seeking still moves the position for reads). Implies write access, and takes precedence over write.

Parameters

  • v — true to open in append mode

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

truncate

OpenOptions truncate(bool v)

Sets whether an existing file is truncated to 0 bytes when it is opened. Requires write or append.

Parameters

  • v — true to truncate an existing file

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

create

OpenOptions create(bool v)

Sets whether a missing file is created. An existing file is opened as it is unless truncate is also set. Requires write or append.

Parameters

  • v — true to create the file when it is missing

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

create_new

OpenOptions create_new(bool v)

Sets whether the file must be newly created: opening fails with is_already_exists() when the path already exists. The check and the creation are one atomic step. Requires write or append.

Parameters

  • v — true to require that the file does not exist yet

Returns

  • this OpenOptions, for chaining

open

IoResult<File> open(Path path)

Opens path with these options. The same as File.open_with(path, this).

Parameters

  • path — the file to open

Returns

  • the open File, positioned at the start; an error when the options break a rule (is_invalid_input()), the path is empty or missing (is_not_found()), it exists under create_new (is_already_exists()), or the system refuses (is_permission_denied() and others)

See also

  • File.open_with

Example

    var log = OpenOptions().append(true).create(true).open(Path("log.txt")).value();
    log.write_string("started");

Metadata

struct Metadata

Describes a file system object: its kind, size, read-only flag, permission bits and timestamps, as a snapshot taken when it was queried.

Returned by File.metadata(), Path.metadata() (which follows symlinks), Path.symlink_metadata() (which does not) and DirEntry.metadata(). The timestamps are nanoseconds since the Unix epoch (1970-01-01 00:00:00 UTC). On POSIX the metadata comes from statx (Linux) or stat (macOS); on Windows from GetFileInformationByHandle.

Example

    var m = Path("notes.txt").metadata();
    if (m.is_ok() && m.value().is_file) {
        var bytes = m.value().size;
    }

Fields

size

ulong size

The file’s length in bytes.

is_file

bool is_file

Whether this is a regular file. On Windows, anything that is neither a directory nor a reparse point (symlink) counts as a file.

is_directory

bool is_directory

Whether this is a directory.

is_symlink

bool is_symlink

Whether this is a symbolic link itself. Only metadata that does not follow links (Path.symlink_metadata()) can report one. On Windows this is any reparse point (FILE_ATTRIBUTE_REPARSE_POINT), which includes junctions.

read_only

bool read_only

Whether the file is read-only: on POSIX, no write permission bit (0222) is set for anyone; on Windows, FILE_ATTRIBUTE_READONLY is set.

modified

long modified

The time of the last modification of the contents, in nanoseconds since the Unix epoch.

accessed

long accessed

The time of the last access, in nanoseconds since the Unix epoch (file systems mounted with noatime or relatime update it rarely or never).

created

long created

The creation (birth) time, in nanoseconds since the Unix epoch; 0 where unavailable, such as on Linux file systems that do not record one.

permissions

Mode permissions

The raw permission bits: on POSIX the mode_t permission bits (mode & 07777, for example 256 for owner read); on Windows the FILE_ATTRIBUTE_* bits.

Constructor

Metadata()

construct()

Creates a Metadata with every field zero or false. Metadata is normally obtained from a query such as File.metadata().

File

[NotCopyable("owns an operating-system handle and cannot be copied; send its path, and open the file again on the receiving thread")] struct File

An open file, or a standard stream, read and written through its platform handle (a file descriptor on POSIX, a HANDLE on Windows).

Open one with File.open (read-only), File.create (write-only, created or truncated), or File.open_with / OpenOptions.open for anything else. The static conveniences read_bytes, read_to_string, write_bytes, write_text, copy, rename and remove work on a whole file by path.

I/O is unbuffered: each read and write is one system call. Reads and writes start at the current position and move it forward; the seek_* methods move it explicitly.

Closing: the File’s destructor closes an owned handle when the last reference to the File is released, and ignores any error. Call close() to close earlier or to observe the error. The standard streams (stdin(), stdout(), stderr()) are never closed by a File. Every operation on a closed File fails with an error that none of the IoError predicates identify (EBADF on POSIX, ERROR_INVALID_HANDLE on Windows).

Errors are IoError values carrying the platform’s code; use its predicates (is_not_found(), is_permission_denied(), …) to tell kinds apart portably.

Example

    var f = File.create(Path("out.txt")).value();
    f.write_string("hello");
    f.close();
    var text = File.read_to_string(Path("out.txt")).value();   # "hello"

Fields

path

Path path

The path the File was opened with; <stdin>, <stdout> or <stderr> for a standard stream, and empty for a File() never opened.

Constructor

File()

construct()

Creates a closed File that owns nothing yet: is_open() is false and every I/O method fails. Use File.open, File.create or File.open_with to get an open one.

Static methods

open

static IoResult<File> open(Path path)

Opens an existing file for reading only. The position starts at 0.

A directory can be opened this way too, but reading it fails with is_is_a_directory().

Parameters

  • path — the file to open

Returns

  • the open File; an error with is_not_found() when the path is empty or does not exist, is_permission_denied() when access is refused, or another platform error

Example

    var r = File.open(Path("config.txt"));
    if (r.is_err() && r.error().is_not_found()) {
        return 0;
    }
    var text = r.value().read_string();

create

static IoResult<File> create(Path path)

Opens a file for writing only, creating it if it is missing and truncating it to 0 bytes if it exists.

On POSIX a created file gets mode 0666 less the process umask.

Parameters

  • path — the file to create or truncate

Returns

  • the open File, empty and positioned at 0; an error with is_not_found() when the path is empty or its parent directory does not exist, is_permission_denied() when access is refused, or another platform error (on POSIX, is_is_a_directory() when the path is a directory)

Example

    var f = File.create(Path("report.txt")).value();
    f.write_string("done");

open_with

static IoResult<File> open_with(Path path, OpenOptions options)

Opens path as options describe. OpenOptions.open is the same call written as a builder.

The options are checked first, as in Rust: at least one of read, write or append is required, and create, create_new or truncate require write or append. The position starts at 0 (append mode still writes at the end).

Parameters

  • path — the file to open
  • options — the access mode and creation behavior

Returns

  • the open File; an error with is_invalid_input() when no access mode is set or create, create_new or truncate is set without write or append; is_not_found() when the path is empty, or the file (or a parent directory) is missing and neither create nor create_new is set; is_already_exists() when create_new is set and the path exists; is_permission_denied() when access is refused; or another platform error

Example

    var opts = OpenOptions().write(true).create_new(true);
    var r = File.open_with(Path("lock"), opts);
    if (r.is_err() && r.error().is_already_exists()) {
        return 1;
    }

std_file

static File std_file(int which, String name)

Returns a File for one of the process’s standard streams. The File does not own the handle, so neither close() nor the destructor closes the stream. stdin(), stdout() and stderr() are the usual way to call this.

On POSIX which is used directly as the file descriptor. On Windows it selects GetStdHandle(STD_INPUT_HANDLE - which), so only 0, 1 and 2 are meaningful there; a process without a console or redirection may get a null handle, and the File is then not open.

Parameters

  • which — the stream: 0 for standard input, 1 for standard output, 2 for standard error
  • name — the display name stored in the File’s path, such as <stdout>

Returns

  • a File on that stream, that does not close the stream when released

stdin

static File stdin()

Returns a File reading the process’s standard input. Each call returns a new File on the same stream; the stream is never closed by a File. A read returns 0 at end of input (on Windows, also when the writing end of a pipe has closed).

Returns

  • a File on standard input, named <stdin>

stdout

static File stdout()

Returns a File writing to the process’s standard output. Each call returns a new File on the same stream, and close() only marks that File closed: the stream stays usable. Writes are unbuffered.

Returns

  • a File on standard output, named <stdout>

Example

    var out = File.stdout();
    out.write_string("hello");

stderr

static File stderr()

Returns a File writing to the process’s standard error. Each call returns a new File on the same stream, and close() only marks that File closed: the stream stays usable. Writes are unbuffered.

Returns

  • a File on standard error, named <stderr>

read_bytes

static IoResult<u8[]> read_bytes(Path path)

Reads the whole file at path into a byte array (Rust’s fs::read).

Parameters

  • path — the file to read

Returns

  • the file’s bytes; an error as for File.open (such as is_not_found()) or read_all

Example

    var r = File.read_bytes(Path("image.png"));
    if (r.is_ok()) {
        var n = r.value().count();
    }

read_to_string

static IoResult<String> read_to_string(Path path)

Reads the whole file at path as UTF-8 text (Rust’s fs::read_to_string).

Parameters

  • path — the file to read

Returns

  • the file’s text; an error with is_invalid_input() when it is not valid UTF-8, or an error as for File.open (such as is_not_found()) or read_all

Example

    var r = File.read_to_string(Path("notes.txt"));
    if (r.is_err()) {
        return 1;
    }
    var text = r.value();

write_bytes

static IoResult<void> write_bytes(Path path, u8[] data)

Writes data as the whole contents of the file at path, creating it or replacing what it held (Rust’s fs::write), then closes it.

Not atomic: the file is truncated first, so a failure part way leaves it truncated or partly written.

Parameters

  • path — the file to write
  • data — the new contents

Returns

  • an Ok result once the data is written and the file closed; an error as for File.create (such as is_not_found() for a missing parent directory), write_all, or close

Example

    var data = Array<u8>();
    data.append(1 as u8);
    File.write_bytes(Path("one.bin"), data);

write_text

static IoResult<void> write_text(Path path, String s)

Writes s as UTF-8 as the whole contents of the file at path, creating it or replacing what it held, then closes it. No newline is added.

Not atomic: the file is truncated first, so a failure part way leaves it truncated or partly written.

Parameters

  • path — the file to write
  • s — the new contents

Returns

  • an Ok result once the text is written and the file closed; an error as for File.create (such as is_not_found() for a missing parent directory), write_all, or close

Note

  • this is the design proposal’s write_to_string, renamed because write_string is the instance method’s name and methods do not overload

Example

    var r = File.write_text(Path("hello.txt"), "hello, world");
    if (r.is_err()) {
        return 1;
    }

copy

static IoResult<ulong> copy(Path from, Path to)

Copies the contents of the file from to to, creating to or truncating it first, in 64 KiB chunks.

Only the contents are copied: to keeps its own permissions and timestamps (a new file gets the defaults of File.create). A failure part way leaves to partly written.

Parameters

  • from — the file to copy
  • to — the destination file

Returns

  • the number of bytes copied; is_invalid_input() when from and to are the same file (after resolving symlinks), which truncating to would empty before it was read; otherwise an error as for File.open on from (such as is_not_found()), File.create on to, or a read, write or close failure

Example

    var n = File.copy(Path("a.txt"), Path("b.txt")).value();

rename

static IoResult<void> rename(Path from, Path to)

Renames or moves from to to, replacing an existing file at to (rename on POSIX, MoveFileExW with MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING on Windows).

Works for directories too. Moving between file systems (volumes) fails rather than copying.

Parameters

  • from — the existing path
  • to — the new path

Returns
